# Fire & Life Safety: A State of the Market

## A Buy-Side Assessment for Acquirers · Published 30 July 2026

**Sprinkler and suppression · alarm, detection and monitoring · extinguisher, kitchen and special hazard.**

1. **The demand is written into law, and the law names the interval.** A private consensus standard becomes an enforceable bill through three links — a jurisdiction adopts a model code, the model code references the standard, and the code's referenced-standards chapter freezes the edition. What follows is not a preference a building owner can defer indefinitely; it is a dated, repeating, licensed obligation.
2. **The mandate is real. Capture of it is not.** In an actively enforced city running mandatory electronic inspection reporting, 1,933 of 2,083 fire protection systems were found compliant — and only 1,313, or 63%, were current on their annual inspection (City of Rockville, 2024-01-31). That single gap is simultaneously the bear case on the annuity and the bull case on the opportunity.
3. **The multiple the industry quotes for this sector was never paid.** The 17x–20x that circulates as a sector fact originates in one 2024 report describing prospective bids, on a business that did not sell. No investment bank publishes fire-specific multiples by size tier at all.

---

## EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

- **There is no published size for the US fire and life safety services market, and the report does not manufacture one.** One sub-vertical separates cleanly from a primary government table: building sprinkler system installation contracting at **$12,018,607 thousand** (Census `EC2223KOB`, variable `CONKB`, code 8221, 2022) — a distinct code from lawn sprinkler installation. Alarm and detection has a **ceiling and no size**, because both available instruments fuse fire with burglar at the definition level. Extinguisher, kitchen and special hazard has **no revenue denominator in the public record**, and what replaces it is a count of legally obligated sites.

- **The obligated base is countable even where the revenue is not.** **737,325** commercial and institutional sites carry a recurring kitchen-suppression duty, and the portable extinguisher installed base runs **8,570,933 to 24,105,750** units against a codified maximum floor area per extinguisher. A single-tenant commercial building with one wet-pipe riser, one monitored alarm, extinguishers and a moderate-volume kitchen generates **11 contractor-performed scheduled site events a year**.

- **Inspection is bought for the work it finds.** The pull-through from inspection into repair is the best-evidenced leg of the thesis, and it is why the inspection book is defended rather than discounted. The mix shift is visible at the one listed acquirer that reports it: inspection, service and monitoring rose from **40% of revenue in 2021 to 54% in 2025** (APi Group, Q4 2025 earnings call, 2026-02-25).

- **The recurring revenue may not travel with the deal.** Three executed inspection contracts obtained in full are structurally incompatible: a contractor's own form runs 60 months with auto-renewal and an explicit right to assign; one owner's form is terminable on 45 days by either party and may not be assigned without consent; a municipal vehicle is a rate-card purchase order cancellable in 30 days "for any reason whatsoever." The best-named logos are frequently the ones least likely to convey.

- **No fire contractor discloses an inspection-book renewal, retention or attrition rate** — checked across every listed and private participant of scale. There is no fire or life-safety-services S-1 in EDGAR, so the disclosure document that would normally settle this does not exist. The one usable commercial proxy is a **≈91% customer retention** figure on a blended commercial security-and-fire book, measured as of 2021-12-31 (ADT Inc., *Business Overview and Financial Modeling*, April 2022).

- **The competitive set is 17, not thirty-odd.** US fire and life-safety service contractors under a live private-equity sponsor number **17** at platform level. A further **5** security-led integrators carry fire, **3** are manufacturers under sponsors, **8** are permanent-capital or family holders with no exit clock, and **5** have already been absorbed. Registers that blend these classes overstate the set of buyers a bidder actually meets and the set of assets that will come to market.

- **Add-on cadence is bifurcated, and the top of it is an order of magnitude clear of the field.** One platform reported **57 acquisitions in 2025**; a sector tracker counts **41** for the same company and period on different cut-offs, and both stand. The long tail runs at **2.4 to 3.0 a year**.

- **The spread the entire roll-up thesis rests on cannot be tracked.** The only benchmark that exists at the small end is industry-agnostic: **6.4x** TEV/EBITDA for $10–25M enterprise values, YTD 2025, on **2,084** observations (GF Data, Q3 2025). Against it, the one disclosed figure at the tuck-in end in US fire and life safety is a bolt-on ceiling of **under 6x**, held in every year from 2019 to 2024. That licenses at least 0.4 to 0.7 turns of entry advantage, and no more — and when one methodology measures both ends of the size curve, the premium is **≈1.24x to 1.61x**, not the 1.88x to 3.33x in circulation.

- **The insurance-pressure thesis currently cuts the other way.** Global commercial property rates fell **12% in Q2 2026**, after 9% declines in each of the two preceding quarters (Marsh, 2026-07-22). A carrier with surplus capacity has less leverage to compel a retrofit. The code-mandated inspection annuity is untouched by this; the carrier-driven upgrade cycle is not. Separately, **no carrier or broker publication establishes a quantified premium differential for sprinklered versus unsprinklered commercial risk** — the industry's most-repeated commercial claim has no traceable public source.

- **The best diligence question in this market is about a building somebody else owns.** Whether a target owns its listed central station, and if not, whose paper its recurring revenue sits on, is worth roughly ten turns of recurring monthly revenue at the market level and carries the change-of-control exposure that decides whether the book conveys at all.

---

# PART I — THE MANDATE


Every commercial building in the United States that carries a fire protection system also carries a continuing legal duty to inspect, test and maintain it. The duty does not arise from insurance, from tenant demand or from NFPA membership. It arises from the fire code the local Authority Having Jurisdiction has adopted, it attaches to the owner, and it runs for as long as the system exists. That obligation — not construction volume, not fire loss, not insurance pressure — is what makes fire and life safety service revenue recur.

The obligation itself is unambiguous and can be traced, jurisdiction by jurisdiction, to a numbered section of enforceable law. What is not established anywhere in the public record is the share of it that is actually being met, and met under contract with a third party. Those two facts — a hard mandate and an unmeasured capture rate — are the whole investment case and the whole risk in the same sentence.

## 1.1 The chain of obligation

A building owner is never bound by "NFPA 25." The owner is bound by the edition of NFPA 25 that the Authority Having Jurisdiction has adopted, and that arrives through three links.

| Link | Mechanism | Instrument |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Model code adoption | A state — or, in home-rule states, a municipality — adopts the ICC International Fire Code, or the NFPA 1 Fire Code / NFPA 101 Life Safety Code family, by statute or administrative rule | State administrative code; municipal ordinance; fire district resolution |
| 2. Reference into the model code | The model code mandates inspection, testing and maintenance by reference rather than by restating it | IFC family: **IFC Section 901.6** and **IFC Table 901.6.1**, "Fire Protection System Maintenance Standards." NFPA family: **NFPA 1 Chapter 13** |
| 3. Edition freeze | The referenced standard's edition is fixed by the model code's **Chapter 80** (Referenced Standards) list, not by whatever NFPA has most recently published | Chapter 80 of the adopted IFC edition |

The load-bearing wording, in the version an acquirer is most likely to encounter in a dense urban route: the NYC Fire Code (2022) at **FC 901.6** requires that "Fire protection systems shall be maintained in good working order at all times," with systems not in working order to be "repaired or replaced as necessary." **FC 901.6.1** routes to the referenced standards listed in **FC Table 901.6.1**, which maps portable extinguishers to NFPA 10, sprinkler systems to NFPA 25, fire alarm systems to NFPA 72 and carbon dioxide systems to NFPA 12 (NYC FC Chapter 9, FDNY).

Two features of that wording carry the commercial weight. It is an affirmative and continuing duty to *maintain*, not a duty to inspect — inspection is how the owner demonstrates the duty is being met. And the duty is discharged only by performing what the referenced standard requires at the interval it sets, so the scope of work is fixed by a private consensus document the owner never adopted and cannot negotiate.

The edition freeze makes the third link commercially real rather than academic. NFSA states the mechanism directly: jurisdictions adopting the 2024 editions of the IBC, IFC, NFPA 1 or NFPA 101 "will also begin enforcing the latest versions of several critical fire protection standards, including but not limited to" NFPA 13-2022, NFPA 20-2022, NFPA 14-2024, NFPA 72-2022 and NFPA 25-2023 (NFSA, 2025-07-02). The annuity is therefore a function of the *adopted* edition, not the current one: route density and revenue per site in Maryland, on NFPA 1-2024 and therefore NFPA 25-2023, is not the same obligation set as in a jurisdiction whose fire marshal guidance still cites NFPA 25 at its 2011 edition. A diligence model that prices scope off the current edition prices a market that does not exist yet in most of the country.

One further hook belongs here because it connects two sub-verticals structurally. **IFC Section 903.4** requires valves controlling the water supply for automatic sprinkler systems to be supervised by a listed fire alarm control unit, and where IFC 903.4 and NFPA 13 conflict, the IFC controls (NFSA, 2023-02-03). That single section forces a monitored alarm panel onto sprinklered commercial stock, converting a one-time sprinkler installation into a recurring monitoring line.

## 1.2 What the code requires, by system and interval

The tables below carry the intervals that generate a contractor visit, and mark the ones an owner may lawfully perform in-house. Section numbers move between editions — notably the NFPA 25 valve chapters at 13.x in the 2011 edition against component chapters at 5.x/6.x/8.x/13.x/14.x in the 2020 and 2023 editions — so each is cited to a stated edition and should be re-checked against the edition actually adopted in the target's operating footprint.

### Water-based systems — NFPA 25

| Interval | Scope of the visit | Performed by | Section (edition) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Weekly | Gauges on dry pipe, preaction and deluge systems; control valves secured with **seals only**; fire pump casing and pressure relief valves | Owner-performable (Minnesota permits facility staff visual inspection of control valves and gauges) | 5.2.4.2, 13.3.2.1, 13.5.7.1, 13.5.7.2 (2011) |
| Weekly / monthly | Fire pump no-flow (churn) test — **conflict, both values carried below** |  | 8.3.1 (2023) |
| Monthly | Gauges on wet pipe systems; control valves locked or electrically supervised; alarm valves, external | Owner-performable | 5.2.4.1, 13.3.2.1, 13.4.1.1 (2011) |
| Quarterly | Waterflow alarm devices, inspect and test; fire department connections; sprinkler pressure-reducing valves; hydraulic nameplates; main drain test where a backflow preventer or PRV sits on the riser | Contractor | 5.2.5 / 13.2.6, 13.7.1, 13.5.1.1, 13.2.5 (2011); 5.3.1, 13.8.1 (2023) |
| Semiannual | Vane-type and pressure-switch waterflow switches; valve tamper switches; water tank high and low level alarms. The 2026 edition adds a three-option semiannual dry and preaction waterflow test — bypass connection, inspector's test connection, or automated testing | Contractor | — |
| Annual | Floor-level sprinkler, pipe, fitting, hanger, seismic bracing, signage and spare-cabinet inspection; main drain test at each riser; antifreeze concentration; dry pipe valve **partial** trip test; preaction and deluge valve trip test at full flow, with interior inspection and cleaning; control valve full-range operation and OS&Y stem lubrication; hose connection and hose rack valves; backflow preventer forward-flow test | Contractor | 5.2.1.1 / 5.2.2, 13.4.4.2.2, 13.4.3.2.2, 13.4.4.1.5, 13.3.3.1, 13.3.4, 13.5.2.1, 13.5.3.1 (2011); 5.2.1, 5.3.3 (2023) |
| Annual | Fire pump full-flow test at churn, 100% and 150% of rated capacity | Contractor | 8.3.3 (2023) |
| Every 3 years | Dry pipe and preaction valve **full-flow** trip test; occupant-use fire hose service test after the first 5 years | Contractor | 13.4.4.2.2.2 (2011); NFPA 1962 (2008) 4.3.2 |
| Every 5 years | **Internal pipe assessment** for corrosion, scale and obstructing material; check valve internal inspection; gauges replaced or tested against a calibrated gauge; **standpipe flow test at the hydraulically most remote outlet**; manual standpipe hydrostatic test; pressure-reducing valve full-flow test; underground and exposed private fire service main flow test | Contractor | 14.2.1, 13.4.2.1 (2011), 5.3.2.1, 6.3.1.1, 6.3.2.1 |
| Long cycle | Sprinkler sample testing — 50 years standard response, 25 or 20 years fast response, 20 years ESFR, CMSA and dry, 5 years harsh environment. A **replacement-driving event**, not an inspection | Contractor | 5.3.1.1.1 (2011) |

> **Conflict — fire pump churn test.** One trade source working from the 2023 edition reports electric pumps monthly and diesel pumps weekly (Ironsmith Fire, 2026-06-12). The ASHE hospital schedule, citing NFPA 25-1998 and NFPA 25-2011, reports weekly for fire pumps generally, with monthly permitted under a CMS waiver (ASHE, 2014). Both values stand. The weekly obligation is the one that generates the larger route-labour requirement and the one an AHJ can cite against, and it binds in any jurisdiction still enforcing a pre-2017 edition and in CMS-certified facilities without the waiver.

> **Conflict — sprinkler sample test triggers.** NFSA, reading the 2023 edition, gives 50 years for standard response, **25 years** for fast response other than ESFR and CMSA, 20 years for ESFR, CMSA and dry sprinklers, and 5 years in harsh or corrosive environments, each then repeating every 10 years (NFSA, 2023-08-17). The Getz Fire matrix, reading the 2011 edition, gives 50 years for standard response, **20 years** for fast response, and 5 years for extra-high-temperature solder type under continuous maximum ambient (Getz Fire, 2020-09). Both stand; the 20-year trigger is the binding one in pre-2020 jurisdictions. Commercially the reading is the same either way — the trigger is arriving now for buildings sprinklered in the 2000–2006 construction wave, and it produces replacement work rather than inspection work.

### Alarm, detection and monitoring — NFPA 72

Cited to NFPA 72-2010 as reproduced by the Minnesota Department of Health (MN DOH, June 2016), corroborated for the 2019 and later editions by UpToCode.

| Device or component | Inspect | Test | Section / table (2010) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fire alarm control unit, trouble signals; remote annunciators | Semiannually | Annually | Table 14.3.1 |
| Initiating devices — smoke, heat, pull stations | Semiannually | Annually | Table 14.3.1; Table 14.4.5 |
| Notification appliances — horns, strobes, speakers | Semiannually | Annually | Table 14.3.1; Table 14.4.5 |
| Duct detectors | Semiannually | Annually, plus airflow verification | UpToCode (2019 and later) |
| **DACT / supervising-station transmitter** | Semiannually | Annually | Table 14.3.1 item 15(a); Table 14.4.5 item 22 |
| Interface devices — elevator recall, HVAC shutdown, door release | Semiannually | Annually | Sec. 14.4.5 |
| Emergency voice / alarm communication | Semiannually | Annually, plus intelligibility verification | UpToCode |
| Kitchen hood extinguishing system interface switches | Semiannually | Annually | MN DOH text |
| Waterflow switches | Semiannually | **Quarterly** | UpToCode; NFPA 25 cross-reference |
| **Smoke detector sensitivity** | — | Within 1 year of installation, then every alternate year; after two consecutive successful tests the interval may extend to 5 years | Sec. 14.4.5.3 |
| Heat detectors, non-restorable fixed-temperature | — | **Every 15 years**: replace, or laboratory-test 2 per 100 | Table 14.4.2.2, item 14.d.3 |
| Heat detectors, restorable fixed-temperature | — | Annually test 2 or 20% per circuit, whichever is greater; **all detectors within 5 years** | Sec. 14.4.5.5 |
| Lead-acid batteries | **Monthly** | Semiannual, plus annual load/discharge | Table 14.3.1; Table 14.4.5 |
| Sealed lead-acid batteries | Semiannually | Semiannual and annual; **replace every 4 years** | Table 14.3.1; Table 14.4.5 |
| Primary (dry cell) batteries | **Monthly** | **Monthly** | Table 14.3.1; Table 14.4.2.2 |

Supervising-station path supervision sets the monitoring layer's own cadence: a **single** communications path must be timer-tested at intervals not exceeding **60 minutes**, and **multiple** paths at intervals not exceeding **6 hours** (NFSA, 2023-02-03). Records under **NFPA 72 §14.6.2.4** must be kept for all inspection, testing and maintenance performed, held on premises for a minimum of three years, and **§10.4.3** requires that only properly trained and competent persons perform the work (MN DOH, June 2016).

### Extinguisher, kitchen and special hazard

| System | Interval | Scope | Performed by | Section (edition) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Portable extinguishers | **Monthly**, at 30-day intervals | Visual inspection, initials and date on the tag | **Owner-performed**; the 2026 edition permits electronic monitoring in lieu | NFPA 10 §7.2.1, §7.2.4 (2010) |
| Portable extinguishers | Annual | Maintenance by a trained technician using manufacturer manuals; new tamper seals on rechargeable units; CO₂ hose conductivity test | Contractor | NFPA 10 §7.3.1.1.1, §7.3.1.3 (2010) |
| Portable extinguishers | **6 years** | Stored-pressure units subject to the 12-year hydrostatic test emptied and internally examined; durable metallic label, minimum 2 in. × 3½ in., applied by a heatless process | Contractor | NFPA 10 §7.3.1.2.1, §7.3.3.1 (2010) |
| Portable extinguishers | **5 or 12 years** | Hydrostatic test — 5 years for pressurized water, water mist, CO₂, wet chemical (Class K), and dry chemical in stainless steel shells; 12 years for standard dry chemical and halogenated agent; non-rechargeable stored-pressure units removed from service 12 years from date of manufacture with no hydrostatic option | Contractor | NFPA 10 Table 8.3.1 (2010) |
| Commercial kitchen exhaust | **Monthly** solid fuel · **quarterly** high volume (24-hour, charbroiling, wok) · **semiannual** moderate volume · **annual** low volume (places of religious worship, seasonal, senior centres) | Exhaust system inspected for grease buildup by a properly trained, qualified and certified person; cleaning triggered where grease exceeds the depth thresholds | Contractor | NFPA 96 Table 12.4, §12.6.1 (2024); §11.4 and Table 11.4 (2011); IFC §609.3.3.1 and Table 609.3.3.1 (2015) |
| Kitchen wet-chemical suppression | **Semiannual** | Maintenance by a certified person; fusible link replacement | Contractor | NFPA 96 §12.2.1 (2024), §11.2.1 (2011); NFPA 17A §7.3.3 (2009) |
| Kitchen hood suppression | Monthly | Owner or staff inspection | **Owner-performed** | MN DOH on NFPA 96 (2011) |
| Clean agent | Monthly visual · **semiannual** container weigh or measure · **annual** functional test of detection, releasing panel and supervisory circuits · **5-year** cylinder hydrostatic per 49 CFR §180.205 · enclosure integrity to **ASTM E2174** verifying the **10-minute hold time** | Cylinders in place, gauges in range, actuators accessible, no damage or corrosion | Monthly usually owner-performed; the rest contractor | NFPA 2001 §8.1.1, §8.4, §8.6, §8.7, §8.8, §5.5.3.4 (2022) |
| Clean agent | Annual thorough inspection · **semiannual** cylinder weighing · 5-year-plus container hydrostatic retest · 5-year hose test · annual room integrity | — | Contractor | NFPA 2001 §7.1.1, §7.1.3, §7.2.1, §7.3.2.1, §7.4 (2008) |
| Dry chemical | **Semiannual** maintenance, agent examined for caking; **12-year** container and hose hydrostatic test | — | Contractor | NFPA 17 §11.3.1, §11.3.1.1, §11.5.1 (2009) |
| Wet chemical | **Semiannual** maintenance; **12-year** container and hose hydrostatic test | — | Contractor | NFPA 17A §7.3.3, §7.5.1 (2009) |
| Carbon dioxide | Monthly (30-day) system inspection · **annual** system test by competent personnel · **semiannual** weighing of high-pressure cylinders · 5-year hose test · 5-to-12-year container retest with a 12-year service limit | — | Contractor | NFPA 12 §4.8.1, §4.8.3.2, §4.8.3.5.1, §4.8.2.3, §4.6.5.2 (2011) |
| Halon 1301 | **Semiannual** thorough inspection and room integrity · 5-year-plus container retest · annual hose inspection and 5-year hose test | — | Contractor | NFPA 12A §6.1.1, §6.2.1, §6.3, §6.3.2 (2009) |

> **Conflict — NFPA 96 section numbering, and whether the interval is an inspection or a cleaning.** Three sources place the cooking-volume schedule in three locations: Table 12.4 of the 2024 edition, §12.6.1 in the 2021 and 2024 editions with §11.4 in 2017 and earlier, and "Sec. 11.4 and Table 11.4" for the 2011 edition (Facilitec Southwest; US Made Supply; MN DOH, July 2016). All three are carried. The more material conflict is the second one: Facilitec states that Table 12.4 is an inspection schedule and that cleaning is a separate event with a separate trigger, while MN DOH reports a flat 6-month cleaning frequency extendable to 12 months with documented minimal grease production and AHJ approval. The inspection-and-cleaning split is the correct reading of the standard's structure; the IFC counterpart at §609.3.3.1 and most AHJ practice enforce the interval as a service-visit cadence, and hood-cleaning contractors bill it that way. Revenue models should follow the AHJ practice and diligence should follow the standard.

> **Conflict — fusible links.** Facilitec, on the 2024 edition, reports annual replacement under §12.2.4 of that edition. MN DOH, on the 2011 edition, reports metal alloy links replaced at least semiannually and other types examined, cleaned or replaced at least annually. Both stand. The semiannual metal-alloy interval aligns with the semiannual suppression service visit and is therefore already inside the route.

> **Conflict — clean agent base interval.** The 2008 edition of NFPA 2001 sets an annual thorough inspection with semiannual weighing; the 2022 edition sets a monthly visual with semiannual container check and annual functional test. Both stand. The monthly visual is normally owner-performed either way, so the **billable** clean-agent cadence remains semiannual plus annual under both editions.

**The split between owner-performed and contractor-performed work is the structural fact that determines how much of the mandate is contractable.** No jurisdiction in §1.3 bars in-house work outright. The pattern is a split by task: owner and facility staff may perform the monthly and semiannual *visual* work — NFPA 10 monthly extinguisher inspection, NFPA 72 semiannual visual, NFPA 25 weekly and monthly gauge and valve checks, the monthly kitchen hood and clean-agent visuals — while annual functional testing, dry-pipe trip tests, sensitivity testing, fire pump flow tests and all suppression-system maintenance require a licensed or certified contractor. The contracted annuity therefore concentrates in the annual and semiannual visits rather than the monthly ones, and revenue-per-site models built off total code intervals will overstate the contractable share.

## 1.3 Who is permitted to perform the work

| Jurisdiction | Requirement | Citation |
|---|---|---|
| **New York City** | Supervision by a **Certificate of Fitness holder** for sprinkler, standpipe, foam, fire alarm, private hydrant and yard hydrant systems, with an exception for Group R-3 sprinkler supervision | FC 901.6.3 |
| **New York City** | Detailed **monthly** inspection reports for standpipe and sprinkler systems completed by the CoF holder and **posted near the main water supply control valve** | FC 901.6.2.1 |
| **Utah** | Automatic fire sprinkler systems, standpipes and fire pumps inspected annually by a person holding a **certificate of registration**; newly installed systems exempt from the annual test for one year from approval | R710-5-7(4)(a), R710-5-7(6) |
| **Iowa** | **Contractor licence plus technician licence** — technician works under a licensed contractor — plus technician-trainee licence, with endorsements for automatic sprinkler installation and maintenance inspection, special hazards, pre-engineered dry chemical and wet agent, and pre-engineered water-based. The contractor must name a **responsible managing employee** holding a current qualifying certification | Iowa Code ch. 100C and 100D; 481 IAC ch. 265 and 266 |
| **Washington** | **Fire Protection Sprinkler System Contractor Certificate** from the State Fire Marshal, four levels (U, III, II, I); **Level U requires NICET ASSL Level III**, while levels I–III may be earned by in-house examination. Separate contractor registration with Labor & Industries | RCW 18.160; WAC 212-80; RCW 18.27 |
| **Minnesota** | Licensed contractor required for sprinkler heads, dry-pipe trip testing and most maintenance; facility staff may perform visual inspection of control valves, gauges and, with training, waterflow alarms. On the alarm side, facility staff limited to inspection and basic testing with documented training, with an approved servicing contractor required for complex and annual testing | MN DOH on NFPA 25 (2011) and NFPA 72 (2010) §10.4.3 |
| **California** | **C-16 Fire Protection Contractor** is a standalone CSLB classification. Industry Bulletin 24-02 confirms a **Fire Sprinkler Fitter Certification** requirement for C-16 qualifiers and journey-level personnel installing or repairing water-based systems in commercial or multi-family dwellings, with a Fire Sprinkler Fitter Registration for apprentices and trainees; one- and two-family dwellings are excluded | CSLB Industry Bulletin 24-02, 2024-05-10 |
| **Maryland** | Fire Sprinkler Contractor licence in **eight classes** — I, IIa, IIb, IIc, IId, IIIa, IIIb, IIIc — expiring two years after issue | Maryland Office of the State Fire Marshal |
| **Texas** | Separate licensing regimes for **fire extinguisher, fire alarm and fire sprinkler** firms, administered by the TDI State Fire Marshal's Office, which licenses companies that sell, install, certify and service those systems | TDI SFMO |
| **South Carolina** | Sprinkler contractor licensure through the SC Contractor's Licensing Board | SC LLR CLB |
| **Standards layer** | NFPA 96 (2024) requires exhaust system inspection and extinguishing system maintenance "by a properly trained, qualified, and **certified** person(s)." NFPA 25 (2026) requires personnel "qualified…for the specific tasks performed," letting the AHJ set standards **by task type** rather than uniformly | NFPA 96 §12.4, §12.2.1; NFPA 25 Ch. 4 |

Two buy-side consequences follow. First, the licence is frequently attached to a **named individual** rather than to the entity — Iowa's responsible managing employee, Washington's Level U holder carrying NICET ASSL Level III, California's C-16 qualifier — and in a lower-middle-market acquisition that person is often the seller. Whether the qualifying licence survives a change of control, and on what timetable a replacement qualifier must be seated, is a question for **regulatory counsel licensed in each state of operation**; the asks are whether the licence transfers, what notice the state fire marshal or licensing board requires on a change of control or change of qualifier, and what the cure period is before work must stop. That should be answered before signing. Second, the task-based split means a target's headcount mix, not its site count, determines how much of the obligated work it can bill. Certification counts by discipline — water-based, fire alarm, ITM, special hazards — are the practical constraint on route expansion, and no national count of certification holders by discipline is published.

## 1.4 Enforcement mechanics — the ITM-to-repair pull-through

A failed inspection compels a repair through three separate mechanisms.

**The base code obligation.** IFC and NYC FC 901.6 require systems to be maintained in good working order at all times and, where they are not, "repaired or replaced as necessary." The duty is affirmative and continuing, so a documented deficiency is a documented violation until it is corrected.

**The reporting loop.** Third-party electronic ITM reporting removes the owner's ability to hold a deficiency privately. The dominant platform is The Compliance Engine, operated by BRYCER LLC, which states it is trusted by over **1,420 Authorities Having Jurisdiction** (BRYCER, vendor claim). Named jurisdiction mandates:

| Jurisdiction | Mandate | Effective | Mechanics |
|---|---|---|---|
| **San Diego, CA** | All service providers who inspect, install, test and repair fire protection systems in the city must register and submit all ITM reports through the platform; **14 system types** covered, including sprinkler, fire alarm, standpipe, fire pump, kitchen hood suppression, smoke control and removal, dry chemical, clean agent and CO₂, and emergency standby power | **2020-04-15** | No registration fee; the **service provider pays a filing fee per report submitted**, with no fee on repair reports. Reports with deficiencies must be resubmitted with corrective-action documentation **within 14 days of the rejection date** |
| **Raleigh, NC** | All compliant and non-compliant fire protection systems must be reported to the city electronically | **2015-11-01**, Ordinance No. 2015-492 | — |
| **Vancouver, WA** | ITM results submitted on an approved form through the fire department online system **within 30 days of the service date**; where deficiencies are repaired within 30 days the inspection and repair reports may be combined, otherwise repairs must be documented and uploaded to the fire marshal contractor portal within 30 days of repair completion | Ordinance M-4485 § 27, 2024 | **VMC 16.04.166**, an explicit local addition to IFC 901.6 |
| **Seattle, WA** | System testing reports must be submitted online; the central station must be notified to place the fire alarm system in test mode | — | — |

**Federal payment leverage in healthcare.** CMS ties Life Safety Code deficiencies to an escalating ladder: initial citation usually at scope and severity D, E or F minimum, **denial of payment for new admissions required at 3 months** where substantial compliance is not achieved, and **termination from Medicare required at 6 months**, with civil monetary penalties where noncompliance is serious. CMS stated it "does not have authority to allow extensions of the August 13, 2013 deadline" (CMS S&C-13-55-LSC, 2013-08-16).

Records are the enforcement substrate. IFC §901.6.3 and IFC §110.3 require records available to the fire code official; NFPA 25 §§4.3–4.3.5 (2020) set the record requirements, with retention of a minimum of three years on site or at an approved location — "three years is a minimum, not a cap" — and the practical rule of keeping each record one year past the next occurrence of that type, which makes five-year-cycle records an effective retention of about six years. Initial records and operation manuals are retained for the life of the installation, and where the fire code and NFPA 25 differ on retention, the fire code controls (NFSA, 2025-11-06). NFPA 72 §14.6.2.4 sets its own three-year on-premises minimum. The 2024 IFC change to §901.6.3 was editorial, code change F58-21, and Table 901.6.1's title was updated editorially with no technical change (WA State Building Code Council, 2024-07-24). The diligence question all of this produces is precise and testable: **repair revenue divided by inspection revenue, split between jurisdictions with mandatory electronic reporting and those without.** A target operating in electronic-reporting jurisdictions should show materially higher repair attach than one operating on paper reports, and if it does not, the gap is either an operational failure or an indication that the inspection base is smaller than represented.

## 1.5 The mandate is real; capture of it is not

The obligation in §1.1 attaches to every system in every commercial building. The evidence that it is being met is thin, single-jurisdiction and in part vendor-published — and the two instrumented jurisdictions in the record report figures that look contradictory until the metrics are separated.

Burlington, Vermont reports **686 systems actively tracked, with 89% in full compliance** (BRYCER, vendor claim); the complement is **11%, or about 75 systems** (A1). Rockville, Maryland reports **2,083 fire protection systems**, of which **1,933 (90%) were found compliant** and **1,313 (63%) are current on annual inspections** (City of Rockville, 2024-01-31). The two cities agree closely on systems found compliant, at 89% and 90%. They part company on the measure that generates contractor revenue: **currency on the annual inspection, where Rockville is at 63%.** Those are different metrics, not conflicting estimates, and the gap between them inside a single city is the finding. Alongside both, the reporting operator attributes **32% of false alarms** to systems that are not compliant with inspection, testing and maintenance requirements (BRYCER, vendor claim).

**The bear reading.** On the compliance measure, in the jurisdictions that have gone to the trouble of mandating electronic ITM reporting — the most instrumented, most enforced condition available — roughly nine systems in ten are already accounted for. If that is representative, the incremental opportunity is not a vast pool of untouched buildings; it is share taken from incumbents who already hold the work, in a market whose largest disclosed participant describes its markets as "local in nature and highly fragmented" (Cintas FY2025 10-K). Growth then has to come from route density, price and repair attach rather than from converting the unserved.

**The bull reading.** In the same actively enforced city, **37% of legally inspectable systems were not current on an annual inspection.** That is the largest measured pool of latent, legally owed, uncaptured inspection demand anywhere in the record, and it sits in a jurisdiction chosen for enforcement rather than neglect. Nothing establishes the rate in the 2012-IFC states, in jurisdictions with no reporting mandate, or in the owner-performed tiers of §1.2 where the only evidence of compliance is initials on a tag. Two structural leaks a compliance percentage does not capture: owners may lawfully perform the monthly and semiannual visual work themselves, and the largest disclosed operator names in-housing as a live competitive alternative — "[b]usinesses may decide to perform certain services **in-house** instead of outsourcing these services" (Cintas FY2025 10-K).

**No published source establishes the national share of legally inspectable fire protection systems that are current on their required inspection, testing and maintenance.** That absence is itself the most important finding this market has to offer about itself. It means the top-down "compliance gap" pitch cannot be sourced, and it means the only defensible route to a capture estimate runs bottom-up through a target's own book: obligated systems per site, visits per system per year, and the proportion of those visits invoiced. That is a data-room exercise, and it is the exercise that should decide the price.

## 1.6 Adopted editions, and the adoption lag

Two published counts of the IFC/NFPA family split exist and they disagree. Both are carried; neither is adequate for deal diligence on its own.

| Source | Count |
|---|---|
| PlainFireData, "Fire Code Adoption by State" | 33 states IFC · 11 states NFPA · 3 state-specific (CA, NY, OR) · 4 local-only (MO, MT, SD, WY) |
| ICC Master I-Code Adoption Chart, January 2024 | 43 jurisdictions with a statewide IFC edition listed · 15 jurisdictions flagged as local-only, with overlap where a state has both a statewide code and local adoption authority |

The two sources classify Indiana, Iowa, Louisiana, Massachusetts and Michigan inconsistently; the ICC chart lists Louisiana on the 2021 IFC, Massachusetts on the 2015 IFC and Michigan on the 2015 IFC statewide. The ICC chart is the sounder instrument for the IFC column because it is the ICC's own adoption tracking and carries edition years. Several states genuinely run both families at once — Georgia adopts an IFC-derived State Minimum Standard Fire Code and NFPA 101 for state fire marshal jurisdiction.

### IFC edition adopted statewide (ICC Master I-Code Adoption Chart, January 2024)

| IFC edition | States |
|---|---|
| **2021** | AL, AK, AR, CA, CT, DE, FL, LA, MD, MT, NV, NJ, NC, ND, OR, SC, SD, UT, VA, WY |
| **2018** | AZ, GA, HI, ID, MN, MS, NE, NH, NY, OK, PA, RI, WA, WV |
| **2015** | DC, ME, MA, MI, NM, OH, WI |
| **2012** | TN, TX |
| Local adoption only (chart "X") | CO, IL, IN, IA, KS, KY, MO, NV, NJ, NM, OK, TX, VT, WA, WY |

States appear in both a statewide row and the local-only column where the statewide code is a floor and home-rule municipalities adopt independently. **Texas and Tennessee, on the 2012 IFC, are the extreme lag cases in the chart**, and Texas is a large market.

### Primary adoption instruments, named and dated

| Jurisdiction | Family | Adopted edition | Instrument | Effective / dated |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Florida** | NFPA | NFPA 1, Florida 2021 Edition and NFPA 101, Florida 2021 Edition (Florida Fire Prevention Code, 8th Edition) | F.A.C. 69A-60.003 and 69A-60.004 | Effective **2023-12-31**; prior amendments 2022-04-26, 2020-12-31, 2018-11-11, 2017-12-31 |
| **Florida, next cycle** | NFPA | 9th Edition (2026) FFPC | Rule hearing | Hearing scheduled **2026-07-14** |
| **Maryland** | NFPA | **NFPA 1, 2024 edition** | Maryland State Fire Prevention Code | "As of June 23, 2025, Maryland has officially adopted the 2024 edition of NFPA 1" (NFSA) |
| **Massachusetts** | NFPA | NFPA 1, 2021 edition with Massachusetts amendments | 527 CMR 1.00 | In effect **2022-12-09**; update effective **2023-05-12** |
| **Illinois** | NFPA 101 | NFPA 101, 2015 edition, replacing the 2000 edition | 41 Ill. Adm. Code Part 100 | Effective **2020-01-01** |
| **California** | IFC-derived, state-specific | **2025 California Fire Code**, Title 24 Part 9, based on the 2024 IFC | California Building Standards Commission triennial code | Published **2025-07-01**, effective **2026-01-01** |
| **New York State** | IFC | 2020 Fire Code of New York State, based on the 2018 IFC | 19 NYCRR | — |
| **New York City** | Own code | NYC Fire Code 2022, Title 29 | NYC Administrative Code | — |
| **Ohio** | IFC | Ohio Fire Code | OAC 1301:7-7 | Rule text effective **2025-11-20** |
| **Minnesota** | IFC | Minnesota State Fire Code; ICC chart shows the 2018 IFC | — | — |
| **Utah** | IFC | 2021 IFC per the ICC chart; **R710-5 amends NFPA 25 Chapter 5, Section 5.1, Table 5.1 directly** | Utah Admin. Code R710-5-7 | — |
| **Central Valley Fire District, MT** | IFC | 2021 IFC by Board Resolution 222307, approved by the Montana State Fire Marshal | Board Resolution 222307, adopted **2023-04-11** | Effective **2023-04-12**; projects submitted before that date use the **2012 IFC** |
| **Vancouver, WA** | IFC plus local amendment | Adds ITM reporting to IFC 901.6 | VMC 16.04.166, Ordinance M-4485 § 27 | 2024 |
| **Sun City Fire District, AZ** | IFC | 2024 IFC with district amendments | District code adoption document, as amended | 01-2026 |

Two features of that table matter more than any individual row. Utah amends NFPA 25's frequency table **directly in state administrative code**, which means the standard as published is not the standard as enforced. And a Montana fire district moved from the 2012 IFC to the 2021 IFC by board resolution on its own clock, with a hard cutover date — adoption is not a state-level variable everywhere.

### Which NFPA edition each IFC edition freezes

The Chapter 80 reference list is the hinge of the whole chain and it is not freely published. Two pieces of direct evidence exist. Central Valley Fire District, on the 2021 IFC, lists its adopted referenced standards as **NFPA 10 (2021), NFPA 13 (2019), NFPA 25 (2020) and NFPA 72 (2019)**; the 2021 IFC was published in 2020, so an NFPA 10-2021 reference inside it is chronologically implausible and the district has more likely listed the editions it enforces rather than Chapter 80's list verbatim. For the 2024 family, NFSA gives **NFPA 13-2022, NFPA 14-2024, NFPA 20-2022, NFPA 25-2023 and NFPA 72-2022** (NFSA, 2025-07-02). Adoption must therefore be confirmed jurisdiction by jurisdiction against the administrative code and the adopted Chapter 80 list. This is a legal determination rather than a research task: engage **regulatory counsel or a code consultant licensed in each state in the target's footprint**, and ask for two things in writing — the citation and effective date of the currently adopted fire code, and the specific editions of NFPA 25, 72, 10 and 96 that its Chapter 80 or equivalent reference list freezes.

### The lag, quantified

| Standard | Current edition | Example adopted edition | Lag |
|---|---|---|---|
| NFPA 25 | 2026 | **2011** (Minnesota State Fire Marshal guidance) | **15 years, 5 edition cycles** |
| NFPA 25 | 2026 | 2020 (any 2021-IFC jurisdiction) | 6 years, 2 cycles |
| NFPA 25 | 2026 | 2023 (Maryland, NFPA 1-2024, from 2025-06-23) | 3 years, 1 cycle |
| NFPA 72 | 2025 | 2019 (2021-IFC jurisdiction) | 6 years, 2 cycles |
| NFPA 72 | 2025 | 2016 (Seattle FD guidance) | 9 years, 3 cycles |
| NFPA 72 | 2025 | **1999** (CMS / ASHE hospital baseline) | **26 years** |
| NFPA 96 | 2024 | 2017 or 2021 ("most AHJs") | 3–7 years |
| NFPA 10 | 2026 | 2010 (MN DOH), 2021 (Central Valley FD) | up to 16 years |
| IFC | 2024 | **2012** (Texas, Tennessee per the ICC chart) | **12 years, 4 cycles** |

Minnesota is the clean illustration of why the chain must be traced to the end rather than to the model code: the ICC chart shows Minnesota on the 2018 IFC, while the Minnesota State Fire Marshal's own fire code page states "the 2011 edition of NFPA 25." Both statements are published; they describe different links in the same chain, and the enforced obligation is the one the fire marshal names. A model that prices ITM scope off the current edition will overstate scope in Texas, Tennessee, Ohio, Michigan, Maine, New Mexico, Wisconsin and DC, and understate the rate of future scope expansion in states running a fast cadence — Florida triennial by rule, California triennial by statute, and Maryland having just moved onto NFPA 1-2024 scope.

## 1.7 Dated forcing functions

### Retroactive mandates — the installation wave and its ITM residue

| Jurisdiction | Scope | Instrument | Dated deadlines |
|---|---|---|---|
| **New York City** | Office buildings **100 ft or more** in height, and buildings converting to office use above 100 ft at conversion | **Local Law 26 of 2004** (§§ 1, 3, 23); Building Code §§ 26-248, 27-228.5, 27-929.1 | Owner's affidavit **2005-07-01** → 7-year report **2011-07-01** → 14-year report **2018-07-01** → **full sprinklering and certification of compliance by 2019-07-01**, with hardship time extensions and partial waivers available for interior Landmark designation or structural impracticability |
| **Chicago** | "every existing building exceeding **80 feet** in height above grade" protected throughout by an approved automatic sprinkler system | Municipal Code of Chicago **13-196-205**, with a companion life safety evaluation at 13-196-206 | Compliance plan to the fire commissioner by **2005-09-01** → **⅓ of gross square footage by 2009-01-01** → **⅔ by 2013-01-01** → **100% by 2017-01-01**, with exceptions for open-air parking, open-air stadium portions, Class A-2 non-transient residential buildings and their garages, Chicago Landmarks, contributing buildings in landmark districts, and buildings colour-coded red or orange in the 1996 Chicago Historic Resources Survey — buildings using exceptions 3–7 must instead comply with the 13-196-206 life safety evaluation |
| **Nursing homes, nationwide** | All Medicare and Medicaid-participating nursing homes | **42 CFR 483.70(a)(8)**, now 483.90; cited under tag **K056**; five-year phase-in from the final rule published 2008-08-13 | **Fully sprinklered as of 2013-08-13** |

The New York City and Chicago programmes are expired as installation demand. Their commercial residue is the resulting **ITM base**: a 2005–2019 retrofit wave now entering the 20-to-25-year fast-response sample-test window of §1.2 and the five-year gauge and valve replacement cycle. That is replacement and repair revenue on an installed base whose sprinkler dates are a matter of public record in two of the densest route markets in the country.

### Standard-driven events already dated

| Event | Instrument | Commercial effect |
|---|---|---|
| Residential sprinklers in dwelling units in place **50 years** must be replaced with fast-response models, or tested to confirm the thermal response index does not exceed 65 (m·s)^½ | NFPA 25 (2026) | Replacement work on an ageing residential base |
| Escutcheons and cover plates on recessed and concealed sprinklers must be replaced for physical damage, detrimental corrosion, loading, non-manufacturer paint or adhered covers | NFPA 25 (2026) §5.2.1.1.5 | Converts a visual finding into a parts-and-labour ticket on every annual visit |
| Abandoned systems must be physically removed or disabled and distinctly marked; ice in piping explicitly defined as an impairment requiring thawing or replacement; spare sprinkler cabinet list must add K-factor, temperature rating, wrench model numbers and quantity by type | NFPA 25 (2026) §4.1.13 (new), §5.4.1.6.6.1, impairment chapter | Decommissioning scope, emergency-service driver, recurring deficiency line item |
| Corrosion-mitigation systems — nitrogen generators, vapour inhibitors — must be maintained to manufacturer specification or the system requires recalculation | NFPA 25 (2026), impairment chapter | Pulls nitrogen inerting into the ITM contract |
| Dry and preaction semiannual waterflow test, three permitted options including automated testing | NFPA 25 (2026) | Adds a semiannual visit, or opens an automated-monitoring substitute |

### Adoption dates already on the calendar

Florida's 9th Edition Fire Prevention Code rule hearing was scheduled for **2026-07-14**; the 2025 California Fire Code was published **2025-07-01** and takes effect **2026-01-01**; Maryland moved to NFPA 1-2024 on **2025-06-23**; the Ohio Fire Code rule text took effect on **2025-11-20** — each of them a dated scope change for every route operating in the state concerned, and each knowable in advance.

## 1.8 What is shrinking or reversing

The mandate is real, but it is not monotonic, and several of the forces that would be cited as tailwinds are currently running the other way. **A retroactive mandate was re-timed, and the re-timing is more instructive than a deletion would have been.** Maryland's adoption of the NFPA 1 and NFPA 101 2024 editions, effective 2025-06-23, retains the high-rise retrofit obligation and changes the clock that runs it. The amendment to NFPA 101 replaces "by January 1, 2033" with "within 12 years of the date of the original violation notice issued by the fire authority having jurisdiction", and the replacement text for NFPA 1 §13.3.2.24.2 requires existing high-rise buildings to be "protected throughout by an approved automatic sprinkler system" on the same rolling clock, with a 180-day intent-to-comply filing and mandatory signage reading "WARNING: THIS HIGH-RISE BUILDING IS NOT PROTECTED THROUGHOUT WITH AN AUTOMATIC FIRE SPRINKLER SYSTEM" (COMAR 29.06.01.07 and 29.06.01.08, effective 2025-06-23). Maryland's own Fire Prevention Commission states that it "will continue to pursue options to have fire sprinkler systems installed" (State Fire Prevention Commission, FY2025 report).

The consequence for underwriting is specific, and it is not that mandates get repealed. **No compliance clock starts until an authority having jurisdiction issues a violation notice.** An obligation whose deadline is triggered by enforcement rather than by statute defers indefinitely wherever enforcement capacity is thin — which converts a dated forcing function into a bet on AHJ staffing. A thesis that underwrites future retrofit volume from pending state mandates should discount for that explicitly in the model rather than in a growth rate.

| What is running the other way | Detail |
|---|---|
| The two largest retrofit programmes are finished | New York City's Local Law 26 window closed on 2019-07-01 and Chicago's phase-in completed on 2017-01-01, and neither programme generates installation demand any longer |
| Adoption lag compresses scope rather than merely delaying it | A jurisdiction on the 2012 IFC or NFPA 25-2011 enforces a smaller scope of work today. Texas and Tennessee on the 2012 IFC, and DC, Maine, Massachusetts, Michigan, New Mexico, Ohio and Wisconsin on the 2015 IFC, are markets where the 2026-edition revenue items in §1.7 are not billable and will not be for years |
| NFPA 25 (2026) removes work as well as adding it | Pipe between a fire department connection and the check valve of 10 ft or less may skip the five-year hydrostatic test where it is visually inspected internally and externally (QRFS, 2026-06-23) |
| A code interval has been made satisfiable by a device rather than a visit | NFPA 10 (2026) permits electronic monitoring in lieu of the manual monthly extinguisher inspection. That interval was owner-performed in the first place, so the immediate revenue effect is small; the same logic appears in NFPA 25 (2026) as an automated option for the semiannual dry and preaction waterflow test |
| The labour qualification gate is loosening at the standard level | NFPA 25 (2026) requires personnel "qualified…for the specific tasks performed," letting the AHJ set standards by task type. The licensed-contractor requirement is a large part of why the contracted annuity exists, and a task-based regime is at the margin a narrower moat than a single contractor licence covering all work on the system |
| In-house substitution is named by the largest disclosed operator | "[B]usinesses may decide to perform certain services **in-house** instead of outsourcing these services" (Cintas FY2025 10-K). The owner-performed tier in §1.2 is the legal room for that substitution, and it is wider than the monthly extinguisher check alone |

**Healthcare is not simply "state scope plus."** CMS references NFPA 101 and NFPA 99 at their **2012 editions** with TIAs (42 CFR 482.41(b)(1)(i)), and the ASHE hospital schedule works from the NFPA 25-1998 and 2011, NFPA 72-1999, NFPA 10-1998 and NFPA 96-1998 editions. Hospitals therefore sit on 1998 and 1999-vintage frequency tables by federal reference, which is *older* than most state adoptions. The healthcare premium in this market is the enforcement ladder in §1.4, and the adjacent scopes — fire and smoke damper testing one year after installation then every six years, and annual sliding and rolling fire door inspection — not a broader base frequency table.

---

---

# PART II — MARKET SIZING


## 2.1 What can be sized, and what cannot

The instrument problem in fire and life safety is not that data is scarce. It is that every published container fuses fire with an adjacent trade, and the fusion happens inside the classification rather than at the reporting stage, so it cannot be netted out after the fact.

Receipts below are carried as the Census publishes them, in thousands of dollars.

| Container | What it is | Why it is not a fire figure |
|---|---|---|
| NAICS **238220** Plumbing, Heating, and Air-Conditioning Contractors | 114,427 establishments, 297,608,835 receipts (Census EC2223BASIC, 2022) | Fire sprinkler installation sits inside this label alongside all plumbing and HVAC contracting. Fire sprinkler work is on the order of 4% of the container's receipts |
| NAICS **238210** Electrical Contractors and Other Wiring Installation Contractors | 81,249 establishments, 249,247,389 receipts (Census EC2223BASIC, 2022) | Fire alarm sold without monitoring sits inside all of electrical contracting |
| NAICS **561621** Security Systems Services (except Locksmiths) | 7,462 establishments, 31,313,513 receipts (Census EC2256BASIC, 2022) | Fire alarm is not separated from burglar and security by definition |
| NAICS **811310** Commercial and Industrial Machinery Repair and Maintenance | 23,925 establishments, 54,640,745 receipts (Census EC2281BASIC, 2022) | Extinguisher service without installation sits inside all industrial machinery repair |
| NAICS **561990** All Other Support Services | 12,269 establishments, 29,678,192 receipts (Census EC2256BASIC, 2022) | No fire content is identifiable in the container at all |

**None of those five is a fire code.** Any count taken from them is a container count, and the scale of the distortion is easy to state: reporting 238220's 114,427 establishments as a fire-protection establishment count would overstate the sub-vertical by more than an order of magnitude. One instrument does separate cleanly. The 2022 Economic Census publishes a **kind-of-business classification for construction, `CONKB`**, independent of NAICS, which contains fire-specific lines and separates fire sprinkler from lawn sprinkler. It carries §2.2 and bounds §2.3, and it does not reach §2.4 at all: **the `CONKB` code list contains no extinguisher, clean-agent or kitchen-suppression line**, and nothing between 8221 and 8299 "Miscellaneous building equipment installation contractor" names portable extinguishers or special hazard.

Two limits travel with every figure taken from the clean instrument. **`EC2223KOB` publishes only `RCPTOT`** — value of business done. There is no establishment count, no employment and no payroll by kind of business, so the fire-specific revenue line has no companion establishment count in any Census product. And `CONKB` is published at industry levels 2 and 3 only, so "8221 within 238220" is not published. For the extinguisher, kitchen and special-hazard layer, the product-line table that would resolve it does not reach the services sectors on the openly accessible endpoint, and **no NAPCS product line for fire-extinguisher servicing, fire-suppression servicing or kitchen suppression is established in the public record**. Nor does the largest disclosed participant fill the gap: **Cintas states no total addressable market, no market size and no market share**, for fire protection or for any segment, in its FY2025 10-K. What it states instead is a structure claim — "The primary markets served by each of the Cintas operating segments are **local in nature and highly fragmented**" (Cintas FY2025 10-K).

A reader who has absorbed this section will not ask for a single fire and life safety market size, because there is not one to give. What follows is one clean figure, one ceiling, one floor and a set of obligated-base counts. They are not additive and they are not comparable on a like basis.

## 2.2 Sprinkler and suppression contracting

**Instrument: table `EC2223KOB`, variable `CONKB`, measure `RCPTOT` "Value of business done ($1,000)", reference year 2022, United States.**

| `CONKB` | Label, verbatim | Value of business done, NAICS 23 ($1,000) | Same, NAICS 238 ($1,000) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **8221** | "Building sprinkler system installation contractor" | **12,018,607** | 12,014,511 |
| 8212 | "Fire and security systems installation and service contractor" | 18,514,295 | 18,508,938 |
| 8222 | "Lawn sprinkler installation contractor" | 1,776,660 | 1,728,134 |
| 8220 | "Heating, ventilation and air-conditioning contractor (HVAC)" | 123,488,876 | 123,465,608 |
| 8224 | "Plumbing contractor" | 76,125,406 | 76,100,456 |
| 8210 | "Electric power installation and service contractor, including lighting" | 178,844,538 | 178,362,969 |
| 8000 | "Special trade contractors, total" | 1,198,309,008 | 1,165,801,524 |
| 001 | "Total" | 2,920,771,250 | 1,281,526,572 |

`CONKB` 8221 is the only fire-specific revenue figure in the published record that comes from a primary government table without derivation: **$12,018,607 thousand**, or **$12.0186 billion**, of value of business done in 2022 (Census EC2223KOB, 2022). It is **0.4115%** of all construction and **0.9375%** of the NAICS 238 subsector. **It is a distinct code from lawn sprinkler:** `CONKB` 8222 "Lawn sprinkler installation contractor" is a separate line at 1,776,660 in the same thousands, which is what makes 8221 usable — the fusion problem that defeats every other container does not apply here.

Three caveats travel with the figure, and the first is a measurement basis rather than a caveat. **`CONKB` is not an establishment classification.** The construction questionnaire asks each respondent to "report the percent of the total represented by the following construction and non-construction business activities", so this line is the **value of building-sprinkler-installation work allocated across all of sector 23** — not the revenue of establishments classified as sprinkler contractors. That is why the table carries no establishment count: establishments are never assigned to a `CONKB` code, and no implied contractor count can be derived from this revenue. The label also says *installation*, so standalone inspection, testing and maintenance performed by firms outside sector 23 is not in it. It excludes non-employer firms. And "building sprinkler system" is not further split between wet, dry, standpipe or special hazard. Commercial-vendor sizings of the US fire sprinkler market run several times larger than the Census line, and the tension is definitional rather than arithmetical: `CONKB` 8221 counts value of business done by construction establishments primarily doing building sprinkler installation, whereas vendor market figures typically bundle device manufacture, distribution, monitoring and standalone ITM firms outside the sector 23 boundary. **The Census figure is the right construction-contracting denominator for underwriting, and any vendor figure should be treated as a differently-scoped total rather than a competing estimate of the same thing.**

The one fire-specific labour denominator available is also unusually clean: **Road Sprinkler Fitters Local 669, 16,631 members** on a 2025 filing (UnionFacts, republishing US DOL Office of Labor-Management Standards filings; page updated 2026-04-23). Local 669 is the single national local with jurisdiction over sprinkler fitting, so this is a floor on the organised segment and says nothing about open-shop headcount. On the association side, AFSA states "more than 1,000 member companies" (AFSA, About page, no publication date shown), which is a partial census of the open-shop and merit-shop sprinkler contractor population.

## 2.3 Alarm, detection and monitoring — a ceiling, not a size

Two instruments bound this sub-vertical from above. They are differently scoped, they are not alternatives, and averaging them would produce a number that describes nothing.

| Instrument | Scope | Value ($1,000) |
|---|---|---|
| `CONKB` **8212** "Fire and security systems installation and service contractor" (Census EC2223KOB, 2022) | Contractors classified in sector 23 selling installation and service | **18,514,295** |
| NAICS **561621** Security Systems Services (except Locksmiths), `RCPTOT` (Census EC2256BASIC, 2022) | Establishments in sector 56 selling alarm systems **with monitoring** | **31,313,513** |

Together they bound the construction-side and services-side alarm layer at **$49,827,808 thousand**, of which an unknown — and from published Census unknowable — share is fire. **Both fuse fire with burglar**, and they do so at the definitional level: `CONKB` 8212's own label names both, and NAICS 561621 is defined as security systems services. The occupational denominator carries the same fusion: **SOC 49-2098, Security and Fire Alarm Systems Installers, national employment 85,900 (2024)**, with projected 2024–2034 growth described as much faster than average at 7% or higher and **9,400** projected annual openings (O\*NET OnLine, attributing the figures to BLS 2025 wage data and 2024–2034 employment projections). Like the two revenue instruments, the occupation title fuses security with fire and is not a fire-only headcount.

What can be said about structure rather than size: **561621 is the most branch-intensive container in the set**, at 7,462 establishments against 6,161 firms, a ratio of **1.211** establishments per firm, against 1.021 for 238220 and 1.029 for the 238210 container. That is the footprint pattern of national monitoring platforms operating multi-site networks, and it is the one place in the published data where a multi-location operating model is visible. The code hook that makes the sub-vertical recur is the one named in §1.1: **IFC Section 903.4** requires sprinkler water-supply control valves to be supervised by a listed fire alarm control unit, which forces a monitored panel onto sprinklered commercial stock. A buyer acquiring sprinkler ITM in an IFC jurisdiction is acquiring a structural referral into monitoring, whether or not the target monetises it.

## 2.4 Extinguisher, kitchen and special hazard — a floor, not an estimate

**There is no revenue denominator for this sub-vertical in the public record.** No Census product line names it, no association publishes a membership or market figure, and the largest disclosed operator makes no market-size statement. What exists instead is a defensible **count of code-obligated sites**, because every one of those sites carries a recurring statutory duty under §1.2 whether or not anyone is currently billing it.

| Denominator | Value |
|---|---|
| Code-obligated commercial kitchens — Type I hood, NFPA 96 cleaning plus NFPA 17A semiannual suppression service | **≥ 737,325 sites** |
| Portable extinguishers in commercial buildings under NFPA 10 Class A spacing | **8.57 million (code floor) – 24.11 million**, central case ≈ 16.07 million |
| Commercial buildings carrying the NFPA 10 obligation | **5,918 thousand buildings / 96,423 million sq ft** (CBECS 2018) |
| Largest single disclosed revenue line adjacent to this sub-vertical, any US filer | **Cintas Fire Protection Services $817.463M (FY2025 10-K)** |

The bottom-up dollar figure that falls out of those counts, at route pricing:

| Layer | Low | High |
|---|---|---|
| Extinguisher ITM — 16,070,500 units, annual maintenance plus the six-year internal and twelve-year hydrostatic cycles at route pricing | $415.2M | $830.3M |
| Hood and duct cleaning — 737,325 sites on a semiannual schedule at standard single-hood pricing | $663.6M | $1,769.6M |
| **Partial recurring service revenue** | **$1,078.8M** | **$2,599.9M** |

Route pricing is **$15 – $30** per extinguisher for annual inspection and maintenance, **$40 – $80** for six-year maintenance and **$50 – $100+** for the twelve-year hydrostatic test (IronClad Fire Protection, 2025-11-07). A consumer price aggregator gives **$40 – $100** for a professional inspection, split by agent type, which is 2.7 to 3.3 times the route price (HomeGuide, no date shown); the gap is a population difference rather than an error — the consumer figure prices a single-unit call-out including the trip, the route figure prices a stop with dozens of units under one trip charge. This is by definition a route business, so the route price is the correct basis, and the consumer price applies only to one-off retail service. Hood cleaning has its own live price conflict, carried rather than resolved. On a semiannual schedule, a standard single-hood range implies **$900 – $2,400 per year** (Facilitec Southwest, 2026-06-16, updated 2026-07-01, on the source's own statement that these are industry-typical 2026 Southwest ranges rather than its own pricing), while a second operator publishes **$1,800 – $4,800 per year** for the same schedule (Total Fire Protection, 2026-06-08). The second figure is roughly twice the first because its semiannual band is weighted to multi-hood systems while the first is a single-hood price. On the second basis the hood layer alone is **$1,327.2M – $3,539.2M** instead.

**This total is a floor, and the reason is arithmetic rather than caution.** Cintas alone books **$817.463M** of Fire Protection Services revenue (Cintas FY2025 10-K). If the whole service pool were $1.1–2.6 billion, then even on the assumption that only half of Cintas's fire line falls in this sub-vertical, Cintas would hold **15.7% to 37.9%** of a market its own 10-K describes as highly fragmented. That is not a credible share, and the correct response is to read the bottom-up figure as a floor rather than to adjust it into agreement. Three explanations are available and none can be sized from published sources: real extinguisher density materially exceeds code-minimum spacing once corridors, stairwells, kitchens and mechanical rooms are counted against the 75-ft travel distance; CBECS excludes manufacturing and industrial floorspace, which is the extra-hazard highest-density tier, and excludes multifamily common areas and vehicle fleets entirely; and the excluded revenue lines may simply be the majority of the money.

**One revenue line is missing from the arithmetic entirely, and it is missing for a stated reason.** The **NFPA 17A wet-chemical suppression service line** — "At least semiannually, maintenance shall be conducted" (NFPA 17A §7.3.3, 2009), reinforced by NFPA 96 §12.2.1 — is real revenue, twice a year, at every one of 737,325 sites, and it is not in the total above. The only semiannual suppression-inspection price in the record is **$500–800** for engineered systems priced by detector count, with annual figures of $800–1,000 below 30 detectors and $1,800–2,000 above 30 (Kinetix Fire, 2021-02-25, modified 2021-12-07). That is an engineered detector-count price, not a restaurant hood-system price, and applying it to 737,325 restaurant and institutional kitchens would be a category error. **No restaurant or commercial wet-chemical hood-suppression semiannual service price exists in the public record**, so the line is left out rather than estimated in. Also entirely absent from the total: the clean-agent and industrial special-hazard layer, for which neither a national systems count nor a service price is published — the Fire Suppression Systems Association's own industry page carries no member count, market value or systems-installed figure — plus all equipment sales, all installation (kitchen wet-chemical systems at **$3,000 to $5,000** installed in Texas against a table range of $2,000–6,000, per FireTron, 2025-08-16), recharge after discharge, cabinets, signage and mounting.

**Do not present this sub-vertical alongside §2.2 as "$12.0186B versus $1.1–2.6B."** The first is value of business done by construction establishments; the second is a bottom-up service-revenue floor that omits equipment, installation and two whole service lines. What can be said about relative firm population comes from the one registry publishing both classes: in Nevada, **portable-extinguisher service licences are 56.4% of automatic-fire-sprinkler company licences** — 84 against 149 (Nevada State Fire Marshal, Licensing Website List, 2022-03-07). Those are licences rather than firms, one firm may hold several, and it is one state on a list four years old, but it indicates a firm population of the same order as sprinkler even though the measured revenue is not.

## 2.5 The obligated base — sites, systems and units

| Population | Count | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Commercial buildings | **5,918 thousand** | CBECS 2018 |
| Commercial floorspace | **96,423 million sq ft** | CBECS 2018 |
| Full-Service Restaurants (722511) | **254,201** establishments | Census EC2272BASIC, 2022 |
| Limited-Service Restaurants (722513) | **271,243** | Census EC2272BASIC, 2022 |
| Cafeterias, Grill Buffets, and Buffets (722514) | **4,590** | Census EC2272BASIC, 2022 |
| Hotels (except Casino Hotels) and Motels (721110) | **56,260** | Census EC2272BASIC, 2022 |
| Casino Hotels (721120) | **400** | Census EC2272BASIC, 2022 |
| Operating public elementary and secondary schools | **99,297** (SY 2023-24) | NCES CCD Public Elementary/Secondary School Universe Survey, 2023-24 Final v1a, Table 3 |
| Private schools | **30,492** (SY 2019-20) | NCES Digest Table 105.50 |
| All US registered hospitals | **6,100** (2024 survey) | AHA Fast Facts on U.S. Hospitals, 2026 |
| CMS-certified nursing facilities | **14,742** (July 2025) | KFF, from CMS Nursing Home Compare / CASPER |
| **Core obligated commercial-kitchen base** | **737,325 sites** | Sum of the restaurant, lodging and institutional components above |
| Portable extinguisher installed base, code floor to extra-hazard bound | **8,570,933 – 24,105,750**, central ≈ **16,070,500** | Derived from CBECS floorspace and Cal. Code Regs. tit. 19 §568 Table 2 |

The extinguisher band rests on a legally binding maximum spacing rather than a survey. **Cal. Code Regs. tit. 19, § 568, Table 2** codifies the NFPA 10 Class A distribution table into enforceable state law: maximum floor area for an extinguisher of **11,250 sq ft** in all three hazard classes, with maximum floor area per unit of A of 3,000 sq ft for light hazard, 1,500 sq ft for ordinary and 1,000 sq ft for extra hazard, and a maximum travel distance of 75 ft for each unit. A count derived from it is a code floor, not a guess, because every compliant commercial building must carry at least that density. The band excludes manufacturing and industrial floorspace, which CBECS does not cover, along with multifamily residential common areas, vehicle and fleet extinguishers, marine, and any building under 1,000 sq ft of floorspace. The kitchen count is likewise a floor. It excludes correctional facilities, degree-granting postsecondary institutions, corporate and campus dining, military dining, stadiums and arenas, non-hotel casinos, convenience stores with fryers and grocery hot-bar and deli operations — **every one of which carries an NFPA 96 obligation**. It also deliberately excludes snack and nonalcoholic beverage bars (78,110), drinking places (40,911), food service contractors (30,086), caterers (12,579) and mobile food services (10,155).

> **Conflict — restaurant unit count. Three sources, three numbers, not averaged.** Census counts **701,875** employer establishments in NAICS 722 for 2022 (Census EC2272BASIC, 2022). The National Restaurant Association states "more than 1 million restaurant and foodservice outlets" alongside $1.55 trillion projected 2026 sales and 15.8 million jobs (NRA, 2026-02-12). A franchise marketing page states "nearly 750,000 restaurants in the United States alone" (HOODZ, no date). These measure different things: Census counts employer establishments inside a NAICS boundary in 2022, while the association counts outlets in 2026 on a definition that plausibly includes non-employer operations, in-store foodservice inside retail and grocery, and institutional outlets that Census classifies outside NAICS 722 altogether. The Census figure is the sound establishment denominator; the association figure is the outlet-level ceiling. The roughly 300,000-outlet gap between them is itself the size of the institutional and in-store kitchen layer Census 722 does not see, which is consistent with the 207,291 institutional and lodging sites counted separately above.

**Visits per site per year.** The obligated base converts to visits without a growth assumption, but it does require an assumption about site configuration. For a single-tenant commercial building with one wet-pipe sprinkler system on a riser with a backflow preventer, one monitored fire alarm system, portable extinguishers, and one moderate-volume commercial kitchen with a wet-chemical hood suppression system, the code intervals in §1.2 produce **11 contractor-performed scheduled site events per year**: four sprinkler visits (quarterly, one of which carries the annual scope), two fire alarm visits (semiannual, one of which carries the annual functional test), one extinguisher annual maintenance visit, two kitchen suppression services and two hood and duct inspections. That is before any three-year dry-pipe full trip test, five-year internal pipe, gauge or standpipe event, six-year extinguisher internal examination, or deficiency-driven repair visit. The building configuration is an assumption and should be replaced with the target's actual site mix in diligence; the frequencies are not assumptions. For the extinguisher, kitchen and special-hazard layer alone the conversion is tighter still: **one visit a year for extinguishers, two for kitchen suppression, and one to twelve for hood cleaning depending on cooking volume.**

## 2.6 Structural fragmentation

**Instrument: table `EC2223LOCCONS`, variable `EMPSZFE`, measure `ESTAB`, reference year 2022, United States.** The Census band label is "less than 5," not "1–4"; the Economic Census universe is employer establishments, so establishments with no employees in the reference period sit inside that band.

| Employment size band (verbatim label) | 238220 ESTAB | Share of entire-year | 238210 ESTAB | Share of entire-year |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| "All establishments" | **114,427** | — | **81,249** | — |
| "Establishments operated for the entire year" | 93,416 | 100.00% | 67,642 | 100.00% |
| "…entire year with less than 5 employees" | **49,926** | 53.44% | **35,717** | 52.80% |
| "…with 5 to 9 employees" | 19,350 | 20.71% | 13,783 | 20.38% |
| "…with 10 to 19 employees" | 12,587 | 13.47% | 8,945 | 13.22% |
| "…with 20 to 49 employees" | 7,730 | 8.27% | 5,976 | 8.83% |
| "…with 50 to 99 employees" | 2,346 | 2.51% | 1,892 | 2.80% |
| "…with 100 to 249 employees" | 1,134 | 1.21% | 988 | 1.46% |
| "…with 250 to 499 employees" | 248 | 0.27% | 211 | 0.31% |
| "…with 500 employees or more" | 95 | 0.10% | 130 | 0.19% |
| "Establishments not operated for the entire year" | 21,011 | — | 13,607 | — |

**74.16%** of entire-year 238220 establishments have fewer than 10 employees and only **1.58%** have 100 or more; the same shape holds for 238210 at **73.18%** under 10 employees and **1.96%** at 100 or more. Multi-location intensity is near-unitary in both construction containers — **1.021** establishments per firm for 238220 and **1.029** for 238210 — against 1.211 for 561621, 1.162 for 561990 and 1.087 for the 811310 container.

**These are container counts, not fire counts, and the distinction is not a technicality here.** The table describes the plumbing, HVAC and fire-sprinkler trade together for 238220, and all of electrical contracting for the 238210 container. A fire-protection contractor running a 20-employee ITM crew is structurally closer to the 20-to-49 band than to the sub-five mode of the container, and the 53% sub-five figure must not be read as the fire-protection size distribution.

What the distribution does license for a buyer:

- **The trade population is atomised and overwhelmingly single-location.** An establishment-to-firm ratio of 1.021 in 238220 means branch networks are the exception, not the norm, which is consistent with a roll-up thesis built on adding locations rather than buying networks.
- **The large end is thin in absolute terms.** Across the entire 238220 container — plumbing, HVAC and fire sprinkler combined — 1,477 establishments have 100 or more employees. Whatever fire-specific subset of those exists is smaller still, which constrains how many platform-scale assets exist to be bought and supports the fragmentation claim the largest disclosed operator makes about its own markets.
- **Unit economics of the containers frame the labour model.** 238220 runs 10.2 employees per establishment, $68,695 of payroll per employee and $254,434 of receipts per employee; 238210 runs 11.9, $71,341 and $257,292; 561621 runs 18.9, $61,819 and $222,277; 811310 runs 9.6, $63,405 and $238,918 (2022 Economic Census, on `EMP`, `PAYANN`, `ESTAB` and `RCPTOT`).

What it does **not** license: any fire-specific establishment count, because the fire-specific instrument publishes revenue only and **no published Census product pairs the $12,018,607 thousand sprinkler line with an establishment count**; any national count of licensed extinguisher, sprinkler or alarm firms, because only two state registries produce counts — Utah at 246 fire extinguisher companies, 92 fire suppression companies and 51 kitchen exhaust systems companies, and Nevada as listed in §2.4 — and scaling those to national population produces figures that disagree by 2.68 times, since both registries include out-of-state firms holding a licence to work in that state and per-capita scaling double-counts national platforms once per state; and any read on size distribution in the extinguisher, kitchen and special-hazard layer, because employment-size bands for 561621, 811310 and 561990 are not published at all. A national licensed-firm count is buildable by deduplicating firm names across all fifty registries. It does not exist today.
