---

# PART III — THE ANNUITY, UNDERWRITTEN


## 3.1 Pull-through: why inspection is bought for the work it finds

Inspection is not bought for the inspection revenue. It is bought because a technician standing in a mechanical room with a tag gun and a code obligation generates a second, larger, higher-margin revenue stream that nobody else is positioned to win. That is the load-bearing claim of the whole sector thesis, and it is the one leg of it that carries a first-party, on-the-record number.

**The ratio, stated by the largest filer in the industry, twice.** APi Group's investor day put it on a slide: "$3 – $4 of incremental service work generated annually for every $1 of inspection work" (APi Group investor day, slide 19, 2025-05-21). The slide's own footnote bounds it: "Based on leadership estimate for U.S. inspection revenues, excludes purely route based service revenues (primarily portable fire extinguishers)." So it is a leadership estimate, US-only, with the portable-extinguisher route book carved out — which means it does not transfer to route-based sub-vertical 3 economics. Nine months later the chief executive restated it unprompted on an earnings call: "If you recall, we generate someplace between $3 and $4 worth of service work for every dollar of inspection revenue that we generate. And that continues to grow at that high-single-digit clip" (APi Group Q4 2025 earnings call, Russell A. Becker, 2026-02-25).

The clause after the ratio carries as much weight as the ratio. Management is asserting the multiple is not decaying — the derived service book compounds at high single digits alongside the inspection base it hangs from. The earlier form of the same claim shows how much the disclosure has firmed: "Our go to market strategy is to sell inspection work first, because we estimate that every dollar sold can lead to subsequent service work" (APi Group Form 424B5, 2021-09-13). "Can lead to" in 2021 became a stated $3–$4 by 2025. The population is APi's Safety Services business, predominantly commercial and industrial fire and life safety in North America and Europe. It is one issuer's own management estimate, with no published methodology, and no independent replication of it exists. It is the market's best-supported claim rather than an audited statistic, and it should be carried that way.

**The mix shift the ratio produced is disclosed and it is large.** "We increased our percentage of revenue coming from inspections, service, and monitoring from 40% in 2021 to 54% in 2025" (APi Group Q4 2025 earnings call, Becker, 2026-02-25), against a stated long-term objective of "60%+ long-term net revenues from inspection, service and monitoring revenues" (APi Group investor day, slide 55, 2025-05-21). Alongside it: "19 Consecutive Quarters of Double-Digit Organic Inspection Revenues Growth" (same deck, slide 17), updated to "over 20 quarters of this trend" by the chief financial officer on the Q4 2025 call.

Two mix series are in circulation and blending them produces a false trend. The consolidated inspection-service-and-monitoring series runs 40% in 2021 to 54% in 2025. A separate and older series covers service as a proportion of net revenues of the pre-Chubb Life Safety business only, "from approximately 20% in 2008 to approximately 41% in 2020" (APi Group Form 424B5, 2021-09-13). The denominators are different businesses.

**Three independent software-platform datasets corroborate the mechanism on different measures.** None of them is a fire-only, whole-population statistic, and each is disclosed by a vendor with a commercial interest in the finding, which is why they are carried as corroboration of direction rather than as the figure.

| Measure | Figure | Basis of measurement | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pull-through as share of work orders, top performers | **19.5%** and **19.6%** | count of work orders | ServiceTrade Fire and Life Safety benchmark, 2024 |
| Pull-through repair revenue as share of invoiced dollars | **10.3%** (E13) | dollars, platform aggregate | ServiceTrade platform data, FY2025 |
| Contractors reporting over 20% of revenue from pull-through | **75%** of respondents | self-reported, MEP-weighted survey | ServiceTitan, 2025-07-14 |
| Service dollars per inspection dollar | **$3–$4** | dollars, one issuer | APi Group, 2025-05-21 and 2026-02-25 |

The two ServiceTrade work-order figures come from the same report rendered for different technician-count cohorts; both are carried, 19.5% and 19.6%, with no average taken. The platform aggregate behind the 10.3% is "$822 million in repair revenue tied to recurring inspections or maintenance work" against "$8 billion in invoices", across "over 1,300 fire protection and mechanical service contractors" (ServiceTrade, 2026-02-05). The ServiceTitan figure comes from a survey of "over 1,000 commercial owners and executives across the trades" fielded 2025-05-05 to 2025-05-23, and the same survey reports that "63% of contractors report that over half of their customer base is secured through PMAs". These are not in conflict; they measure different quantities. But the tension is worth carrying into an investment committee memo. APi's $3–$4 implies service is 75% to 80% of the inspection-plus-service block, while the platform aggregate implies pull-through repair is about a tenth of all invoiced dollars. APi's ratio counts all subsequent service work off an inspection relationship; the platform figure counts only repair work explicitly tied to a recurring inspection or maintenance job. **The $3–$4 is the wider definition and should be read as the ceiling, not the base case.**

**The most useful operating insight in the sector decomposes the ratio into two behaviours.** Top performers identified potential repairs on about 25% of work orders against about 10% for bottom-half performers, and "Top performers consistently converted between 50% and 60% of identified equipment issues into quotes for their customers. Bottom-half performers only quoted identified issues about 10% of the time." Quote approval rates were similar between the cohorts — "the difference in revenue came from volume rather than conversion quality." In fire protection specifically, "Top-half performers earned 38% more per work order than bottom-half performers" and 99% more per customer (ServiceTrade, "5 Steps to Growing Pull-Through Repair Revenue").

The pull-through gap is therefore not a closing-skill gap. It is a detect-and-quote gap. A contractor who does not systematically record deficiencies at the inspection and does not systematically quote them leaves roughly half the annuity's economics on the floor — and that is a defect a buyer can test in diligence from the target's own work-order and quote history, and fix post-close with process and software rather than with people. It is one of the few genuinely underwritable operational improvements in the sector. The revenue is composed of many small compelled repairs rather than a few large ones. Platform quote data shows "1.4 million quotes, achieving a 63% acceptance rate with 40% of the quoted value approved" (ServiceTrade, 2026-02-05) — acceptance by count runs well ahead of acceptance by value, so the mean accepted quote is materially smaller than the mean issued quote (E13). That is consistent with APi's disclosed "≈$7K Average Ticket Size in Safety Services: North America" (APi Group investor day, slide 17, 2025-05-21).

**The mandate that generates the inspection is real; capture of it is not.** "Nearly all facilities that have existing life safety systems are required by law to have that system inspected on an annual basis" (APi Group Form 424B5, 2021-09-13), and the Chubb acquisition announcement described a business in which "50%+ of our revenue will be service based with meaningful, statutorily-required, recurring revenue" (APi Group release, 2021-07-27). The municipal record shows the duty is widely unenforced, and the size of that shortfall is the more commercially interesting number.

| Jurisdiction or source | Measure | Figure | Date |
|---|---|---|---|
| City of Rockville, MD | fire protection systems in the city | **2,083** | 2024-01-31 |
| City of Rockville, MD | current on annual inspections | **1,313 (63%)** | 2024-01-31 |
| City of Rockville, MD | found compliant due to ongoing inspection and testing | **1,933 (90%)** | 2024-01-31 |
| City of Rockville, MD | reduction in unwanted fire alarm and sprinkler activations citywide | **30%** | 2024-01-31 |
| Buncombe County, NC | systems out of compliance or with an outstanding deficiency, pre-programme | **12–15%** | programme adopted July 2024 |
| Buncombe County, NC | outstanding deficiencies, post-programme | **2% or less** | as reported |
| BRYCER (vendor claim) | commercial and multi-tenant inspectable buildings in violation of code requirements | **30–40%** | undated |
| BRYCER (vendor claim) | life safety systems uninspected or unmaintained annually | **40%** | undated |

Rockville's own arithmetic reconciles on the currency figure and not on the compliance figure: 1,313 of 2,083 is 63.03%, while 1,933 of 2,083 is 92.80% against a stated 90% (E14). Both stated values are carried. Read the 63% carefully. In a city running an active electronic third-party reporting programme — one of the more enforced jurisdictions in the country — 37% of inspectable fire protection systems were not current on an annual inspection. The mandate is not self-executing. That caps the "everyone must buy this" story and simultaneously identifies the largest measured pool of latent, legally owed, uncaptured inspection demand anywhere in the record.

The three figures above measure three different states — inspection currency, outstanding deficiency, and code violation — and the industry uses the words interchangeably. **A deficiency rate presented in a confidential information memorandum means nothing until the seller specifies which of the three is being counted.** No aggregated national deficiency rate exists in the public record; the operator of the largest third-party reporting dataset in the country, spanning "over 1,420 cities, counties, and fire departments using our platform", publishes only marketing claims from it. Third-party reporting itself "is utilized in at least 37 states" (NFSA, 2018-12-05). One fee schedule quantifies why inspection revenue per site exceeds a naive one-inspection-a-year model: Seattle charges a "$35 filing fee for submitting a report. One fee is charged per system type, per year, except for hood systems, which are per every six-month service", with reports due "within seven calendar days" and a late fee of "$12 per report" (Seattle Fire Department). A single building with sprinkler, alarm, extinguishers and a kitchen hood generates several separately reportable events a year.

## 3.2 What "recurring" actually means in an executed contract

No filer discloses the composition of its inspection book by contract form, so the question is answered from the contracts themselves. Three executed or published standard-form agreements are available in full, and they are structurally incompatible with one another. That incompatibility is the finding.

| | **Contractor's own form** | **National-account, owner's paper** | **Public-sector award** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Parties | Fire Protection, Inc. (Seattle), published master service agreement | Johnson Controls Fire Protection, LP — Physicians Realty L.P., contract 919272 | Pye-Barker Fire & Safety — Lee County, FL, solicitation B210350NAT |
| Term | "60 months commencing on the date of service activation" | 2022-07-01 to 2025-06-30, three years | "one (1) three-year (3) period" |
| Renewal | "shall renew automatically for successive 60 month terms thereafter under the same terms and conditions" | "the term shall automatically renew for successive one (1) year periods if not terminated" | renewals "not to exceed the initial Agreement term of three (3) years", "Upon mutual written agreement of both parties" |
| Termination | written notice "at least 60 days prior to the expiration of any term, extension, or renewal" | "may be terminated at any time by either party upon forty-five (45) days' prior written notice" | County may terminate "upon thirty (30) days' written notice" for "any reason whatsoever" |
| Escalation | "by an amount not to exceed the greater of six percent (6%) each year or the percent equal to the most recent 12-month percentage change in CPI-U" | in the price schedule: **+9.95%** year 1 to 2, **+9.03%** year 2 to 3 (E12) | none specified |
| Assignment | "FPI shall have the right to assign this Agreement to a company licensed to perform the services and shall be relieved of any obligations"; customer may not assign without consent | "Contractor shall not assign this Agreement without the prior written consent of Owner" | assignee must agree "in writing with the non-assigning party"; assignment of payment claims to financial institutions or a bankruptcy trustee permitted without County approval |
| Volume commitment | scheduled service at contracted rates | fixed annual price, "monthly in advance, net 30" | work "on an as needed basis" contingent on County purchase orders; rate card **$80–$180/hour** by technician grade, **25%** materials markup |
| Deficiency handling | "If system devices or panels are deficient, FPI will provide Customer with a written proposal for service and/or repairs" | annual inspections with semi-annual visuals and quarterly testing across multiple properties | not specified |

The contractor's own form is the asset a consolidator wants: five-year evergreen term, a contractual escalator at the greater of 6% or CPI-U, a unilateral assignment right in the contractor's favour, and a written deficiency-to-proposal step. On that paper the pull-through mechanism of §3.1 is a contractual obligation of the contractor rather than a sales habit — which is exactly the difference between a detect-and-quote discipline that survives a change of ownership and one that leaves with the founder.

The national-account form is the mirror image. Its executed price schedule is real evidence of inspection pricing power: total contract value **$288,845** across three years, at **$87,576**, **$96,290** and **$104,979**, an escalation of 9.95% and then 9.03% (E12) — well above general inflation over that period, in a competitively awarded multi-site institutional account. But two clauses dismantle the annuity story. Termination for convenience on 45 days' notice by either party means that on a three-year face term the economic term is 45 days. And no assignment without owner consent means the contract does not travel unless the owner agrees to let it. The public-sector award is not a revenue contract at all. It is a rate-card master agreement with zero volume commitment, cancellable in thirty days for any reason whatsoever. It confers a right to be called, not a right to revenue. Diligence that counts a public-sector "three-year contract" as contracted backlog is mis-stating the asset.

**On assignability the answer is structural rather than statistical, and it turns entirely on whose paper the agreement is written on.** The contractor's own standard form is assignable by the contractor unilaterally. An institutional owner's negotiated form is consent-gated. A public-sector award is consent-gated and volume-uncommitted. The practical consequence is that an equity or stock purchase preserves the book while an asset purchase puts the best-named accounts at risk of consent — and the perverse result follows directly: **the accounts a buyer most wants, the multi-site national logos, are precisely the ones least likely to travel in an asset deal, because they are the ones written on the customer's paper.**

No source discloses what share of any contractor's book sits on each of the three forms — not a filer, not a rating agency, not a franchise disclosure document. It is the single most commercially decisive unquantified variable in the industry, and it is a diligence question rather than a research question: the answer exists in the target's contract file and nowhere else. Engage transaction counsel with contract-assignment and change-of-control experience before structure is fixed, and ask three things: what proportion of the inspection book by revenue sits on the target's own form; which named accounts contain anti-assignment or consent clauses; and whether the deal structure contemplated triggers them.

**The filers' own language is internally contradictory, and the contradiction is live.** Against "Maintenance and service revenues are less cyclical, and are reasonably recurring due to consistent renewal rates and deep customer relationships" (APi Group Form 424B5, 2021-09-13) sits the risk-factor formulation "the fact that our revenues are derived primarily from contracts with durations of less than six months and the risk that customers will not renew or enter into new contracts" (APi Group Form DRS, 2019-12-23). That phrase is not a stale artefact; EDGAR full-text search returns it in APi filings through the Form 10-Q filed 2025-10-30. The FY2025 risk factors go further: "our customers are not contractually committed to procure specific volumes of services under a contract. Revenue estimates reflected in our backlog can be subject to change due to a number of factors, including contract cancellations" (APi Group FY2025 10-K).

Accounting treatment in the adjacent route-service industry corroborates the short-duration reading. Approximately 95% of Cintas revenue comes from "route servicing … performed by a Cintas employee-partner, at the customer's location of business", recognised "over time as services are performed", and "The Company's performance period generally corresponds with the monthly invoice period" (Cintas Form 10-Q, period ended 2025-08-31). A route-service business whose performance period is one month has no long-dated contract asset at all.

**"Recurring" in fire and life safety is a behavioural fact about customer habit and legal duty, not a balance-sheet fact about contracted term.** Every source reached is consistent with that reading. The one route-based recurring service business that does disclose a term sets the benchmark from outside the sector: "We primarily serve our customers pursuant to written service contracts that range in duration from three to five years" (UniFirst Form 10-K, fiscal 2017), across 300,000-plus customer locations. Fire inspection's own contractor-side standard form, at 60 months, sits at or above that. Its national-account forms, at a 45-day out, sit far below.

## 3.3 Retention and attrition

**One usable commercial proxy exists.** ADT's commercial segment disclosed customer retention of ≈91% and a customer life of ≈10 years, alongside recurring revenue at ≈30% of segment total revenue (ADT Inc. investor deck, "Business Overview and Financial Modeling", April 2022, pages 9 and 10).

Label the population precisely. It measures ADT Commercial — the commercial fire, life safety and security business divested to GTCR in October 2023 and rebranded Everon, and the direct organisational ancestor of one of the named platforms in the consolidator register. It does not measure a pure inspection, testing and maintenance book; it is a blended commercial security-plus-fire book. It carries no published definition, so whether the 91% is logo retention, revenue retention or recurring-monthly-revenue retention is unstated, and those are three different numbers. It is an April 2022 marketing-deck figure, published before divestiture, and no successor figure has been published under the new ownership. Discounted for all three caveats, ≈91% retention with a ≈10-year customer life remains a materially different animal from residential monitoring attrition, and it is the right anchor for a commercial inspection book provided it is labelled a proxy every time it is used.

**The residential number, with its definition attached, so the two are never confused.**

| ADT Inc. | FY2025 | FY2024 |
|---|---|---|
| Gross customer revenue attrition (TTM) | **13.1%** | **12.7%** |
| Revenue payback (TTM) | **2.3 years** | **2.2 years** |
| End-of-period recurring monthly revenue | **$359M** at 2025-12-31 | flat year on year |
| Total revenue | **$5,129M** | — |
| Monitoring and related services revenue | **$4,354M** | — |
| Security installation, product and other | **$775M** | — |

Source: ADT Inc. Q4 and full-year 2025 results, 2026-03-02. The company's own definition: "Gross attrition is calculated on a trailing twelve-month basis, the numerator of which is the RMR lost during the period due to attrition, net of dealer chargebacks and reinstated customers, excluding contracts monitored but not owned and DIY customers, and the denominator of which is total annualized RMR based on an average of RMR under contract at the beginning of each month during the period" (ADT Inc. investor deck, April 2022).

The population is residential and small-business monitoring subscribers, and the denominator is recurring monthly revenue rather than revenue or customers. A 13.1% attrition rate on that denominator and a 91% commercial customer-retention rate are computed on different numerators, different denominators and different populations, and must never be placed on the same axis. Read together, the residential figures describe a book being replaced rather than grown at a slightly worsening cost: recurring monthly revenue flat year on year while attrition widened 40 basis points and payback lengthened by a tenth of a year. On a constant-hazard reciprocal the 13.1% implies a customer life of 7.63 years against 7.87 years at the prior year's rate (E11) — an approximation that overstates life for any real book, since early-life attrition runs higher than late. One directional statement exists from a named commercial operator with no figure attached to it: on the multifamily monitoring portfolio acquired from ADT's business-to-business channel, Everon's chief executive described "a valuable market with a traditionally low attrition rate" (Everon release, 2025-09-15). No number accompanies the assertion and none is inferred here.

**No fire protection contractor discloses an inspection-book renewal rate, retention rate or attrition rate.** Not one — public filer or private platform. APi's disclosure describes the concept without ever quantifying it: revenue is "reasonably recurring due to consistent renewal rates", with no rate given.

The structural reason the usual source does not exist is worth stating, because it closes off the research route a buyer would otherwise fund. **There is no fire or life-safety-services S-1 in EDGAR.** The registration statement is the document that normally compels a services business to publish retention, churn and cohort economics, and no fire or life safety business has filed one. APi came to market via a UK-listed special purpose vehicle and a Form S-4; its draft registration statement of 2019-12-23 is the functional equivalent of an S-1 and its financial and operational metric tables are redacted with placeholder marks. EDGAR full-text search on Form S-1 for the phrase "inspection, testing and maintenance" returns eight registrants, none of them a fire or life safety business. The second route is closed for a different reason. Credit opinions on the large private platforms exist but are entitlement-gated, because the issuers are 144A private placements rather than public registrants; no indexable rating actions are published for them. Obtaining platform-level operating metrics is therefore a paid-database exercise — S&P Capital IQ, Moody's Analytics, Debtwire, Reorg, LCD — and should be scoped and budgeted as one rather than pursued as open-web research.

**Underwriting instruction.** Any inspection renewal rate presented by a seller is an unaudited management figure with no public benchmark to check it against. Test it against the target's own site-level invoice history across three consecutive inspection cycles, not against an industry average, because there is no industry average. The obligation itself recurs regardless of who performs it — a single-tenant commercial building generates eleven contractor-performed scheduled site events a year before any three-, five- or six-year event and before deficiency repairs — so retention in this sector is a competitive outcome, not a contractual one.

## 3.4 Gross margin by work type

The only fire-specific margin-by-work-type bands in the public record come from a sell-side sector report, and they are the economic reason the inspection book is defended rather than harvested.

| Work type | Gross margin |
|---|---|
| Testing and inspection | **50%+** |
| Repair and maintenance | **40%–50%** |
| Retrofit | **35%–45%+** |
| New construction | **25%–35%** |

Source: Meridian Capital, *Fire & Life Safety Services M&A Market Update*, Winter 2025, Brian Murphy and Aaron Franzheim. The same report characterises service revenue as "generally characterized as recurring/reoccurring with strong margins". The spread does the work. Against the new-construction band, testing and inspection at its floor carries 15 percentage points more gross margin at the top of that band and 25 points more at the bottom of it (E2). The report's own reading of the migration APi discloses — inspection, service and monitoring from 40% to 54% of revenue between 2021 and 2025 — is that it is worth roughly 15 to 20 points of gross margin on the migrated revenue. On either construction, **the value being paid for in a mix shift is the margin on the migrated dollar, not the recurrence of it.** Recurrence without the margin band is a route business with a low ceiling; the margin band without recurrence is project work.

Directional corroboration from inside a single filer, on a different measure and a different cut: APi's Safety Services segment carried adjusted gross margin of **37.3%** in FY2025 against 36.4% in FY2024, while its Specialty Services segment carried **18.9%** against 19.7% — a spread of 1,840 basis points (E3). That is a difference between two different businesses rather than a work-type differential inside one, and it must not be used as the latter. What it establishes is that the service-weighted segment carries roughly twice the gross margin of the project-weighted segment under the same management, procurement and overhead.

The bands also explain the defensive behaviour a buyer will observe in the field. A contractor holding a testing-and-inspection book at a 50%-plus gross margin, which also generates the repair work at 40% to 50%, has more to lose from a lost site than a project contractor has from a lost bid — which is why incumbency is fought over at renewal in a way that installation work is not.

## 3.5 Recurring-revenue comparators, on one basis

All figures below use **total revenue as the denominator**. That single choice matters more than any of the individual values, because the sector's most-cited recurring-revenue statistic is quoted on a narrower denominator and flatters the top of the ladder by about fifteen percentage points.

| Business | Recurring measure | Figure | Date | Population |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ADT Inc. | annualised recurring monthly revenue ÷ total revenue | **84.0%** (E10) | FY2025 | residential and small-business monitoring |
| ADT Commercial (later Everon) | recurring ÷ segment revenue | **≈30%** | April 2022 | commercial fire, life safety, security |
| APi Group | inspection + service + monitoring ÷ net revenue | **54%** | FY2025 | fire and life safety services, global |
| APi Group | same measure, stated objective | **60%+** | long term | same |
| APi Life Safety segment | service ÷ net revenue | **41%** | 2020 | pre-Chubb Life Safety only |
| Chubb Fire & Security | "recurring in nature" | **"over 60%"** | pre-2021 | fire and security, 17 countries |
| Rollins | recurring services ÷ revenue | **≈75%** | FY2025 | pest control, route-based |
| Cintas | route-serviced ÷ revenue | **≈95%** | Q1 FY2026 | uniform, first aid, fire protection |

The denominator point, stated once so it does not have to be argued again: annualised recurring monthly revenue of **$4,308M** measures **98.9%** of ADT's monitoring and related services revenue and **84.0%** of its total revenue (E10). Both are true and they answer different questions. Only the second is comparable to any other platform in this sector, because total revenue is the only denominator every platform reports. Comparing a monitoring-revenue ratio for one company against total-revenue ratios for the rest overstates the first by about fifteen points and makes residential monitoring look like a different species from an inspection contractor when the real gap is narrower.

Two conclusions a buyer should carry out of the table. **First, 54% and "over 60%" bracket the practical ceiling for a services-led fire platform**, and the largest operator's own long-term objective of 60%-plus says management believes the ceiling sits near there. A target represented in diligence as materially above 60% recurring is either defining recurring more loosely than the sector's largest filer does, or is not comparable — and the first explanation is more common than the second.

**Second, pest control at ≈75% and route-serviced uniform rental at ≈95% run far higher recurring shares than fire inspection does, and the gap is structural rather than managerial.** Fire platforms carry construction and retrofit revenue that pest control and uniform rental do not. When a fire platform is compared to a pest-control or uniform-rental recurring-revenue benchmark, that structural gap is the thing being papered over.

At the other end of the ladder, a business showing recurring monthly revenue at roughly 6% to 9% of total revenue is not selling monitoring contracts at all — it is selling inspection agreements. That distinction, rather than the percentage itself, is what changes the diligence programme: above roughly 10%, the asset is a set of assignable term-dated contracts with measurable decay; below it, the asset is a site list, an inspection history and a habit, and its assignability turns on whose paper the agreements are written on (§3.2).

---

---

# PART IV — THE CONSOLIDATION MAP


## 4.1 How to read this map

Sub-vertical codes used throughout: **SV1** sprinkler and suppression contracting, install plus inspection, testing and maintenance (ITM) · **SV2** alarm, detection, monitoring and low-voltage life safety · **SV3** extinguisher, kitchen, clean agent and industrial special hazard.

The owner classes below are not interchangeable, and a register that blends them misleads. A sponsor with a fund clock will produce a secondary asset; a permanent-capital holder will not. A manufacturer under a sponsor competes for supplier assets, not for route density. An entity already absorbed into another platform is not a target at all, however live its nameplate still looks. Separating them is this section's main contribution, because a single headline platform count overstates the competitive set a buyer actually faces.

| Class | Definition | Count |
|---|---|---|
| **1a** | US fire and life-safety service contractors under a live private-equity sponsor | **17** |
| **1b** | Security-led integrators under a live sponsor that carry fire and life safety in their own service description | **5** |
| **2** | Fire manufacturers and product platforms under sponsors | **3** |
| **3** | Permanent-capital, family and strategic holders with no exit clock | **8** |
| **4** | Platform-level entities already exited to a strategic or absorbed into another platform | **5** |

Two further groups sit outside the counts and are named because they distort registers that include them. First, **fire platforms of scale whose owner does not appear in the public record**: ORR Protection and CPI Security Systems. Second, **monitoring infrastructure under a sponsor**: Becklar, whose brands include AvantGuard, Freeus, Armstrongs and Eyeforce, passed to Graham Partners on 2024-12-10 with BV Investment Partners rolling its stake from its 2020 investment (Security Info Watch, 2024-12-10). Becklar is not a fire contractor, but it owns paper that fire-alarm recurring revenue sits on, and it added Dynamark Monitoring on 2025-09-03.

Read together: the sponsor-backed US fire contracting set at platform level is **17**, not the thirty-odd entities a blended tracker reports. Two of the 17 carry a sponsor identified only by trade press or a directory listing — RapidFire Safety & Security (Concentric Equity Partners) and Telgian (The Miller Group) — and both should be graded a step below the rest.

## 4.2 Public strategics

**APi Group Corporation (NYSE: APG)** is the only issuer in the sector that discloses a fire and life-safety segment at all, which makes it the sector's benchmark set by default. FY2025 net revenues were **$7,911 million** against $7,018 million in FY2024, with Safety Services at **$5,456 million** on 13.7% reported and 6.7% organic growth, and Specialty Services at **$2,460 million** (APi Group IR, 2026-02-26). Safety Services segment earnings margin was **16.8%**, up 90 bps. The two segments sum to $7,916 million against a stated total of $7,911 million; both figures are carried as filed and the $5 million difference is not netted here. Q1 2026 net revenues were **$2.0 billion**, Safety Services **$1,415 million**, and FY2026 guidance was raised to **$8,475–$8,675 million** (Businesswire, 2026-04-30). A 2026 trade guide puts FY2025 net revenue at about **$7.0 billion** (CT Acquisitions, retrieved 2026-07-29); the issuer figure is $7,911 million and the trade-guide figure matches APi's stated FY2024. Both are carried.

APi's stated strategy is inspection-first. On CertaSite it said the acquisition *"shifts mix towards 60% of revenues from inspection, service, and monitoring"* (APi Group, 2025-12-10). FY2025 acquisitions consumed **$186 million** of net cash. Chubb Fire & Security was acquired from Carrier for **$3.1 billion**, announced 2021-07-27 and completed 2022-01-03 (Businesswire). In the two quarters after CertaSite, APi completed **Onyx-Fire Protection Services, Inc.** in Canada, about **$190 million** of annual revenue, on 2026-06-08, and **WTech Fire Group** in Europe, about **$175 million** of annual revenue, on 2026-07-01 (Businesswire, 2026-06-09 and 2026-07-02). Of the *"more than $1 billion across these three acquisitions"* the CEO described, only CertaSite is a US business, and the other two remove nothing from a US target board.

**Johnson Controls International plc (NYSE: JCI)** did not exit fire. FY2025 sales were **$23.6 billion**, with Products and Systems at **$16.124 billion** and Services at **$7.472 billion**, up 7% year on year, and Systems and Services backlog at **$14.9 billion**, up 13% organically (JCI, PRNewswire, Nov 2025; JCI FY2025 Form 10-K). Legacy segment sales for the twelve months to 2025-09-30 were Americas $15.831 billion, EMEA $4.968 billion and APAC $2.797 billion. What JCI sold was HVAC, not life safety: Residential and Light Commercial HVAC went to Bosch Group in July 2025 for **$8.3 billion** of total consideration, of which JCI's portion was approximately **$6.9 billion**. Fire and security remain inside the Building Solutions Americas core.

**Carrier Global Corporation (NYSE: CARR)** sold both of its fire businesses and now retains none. Industrial Fire went to Sentinel Capital Partners at **$1.425 billion** enterprise value, announced 2024-03-05, with net proceeds expected to exceed $1.1 billion. Commercial and Residential Fire went to an affiliate of Lone Star Funds at **$3 billion** enterprise value, closing 2024-12-02, with estimated net proceeds of $2.2 billion (Carrier corporate releases). Access Solutions, including LenelS2, went to Honeywell (SecurityInfoWatch). Kidde, Edwards, Marioff, Autronica, Det-Tronics, Fireye and LenelS2 all sit under other owners.

**Honeywell International (NASDAQ: HON)** bought Carrier's Access Solutions business including LenelS2 and is mid-reorganisation: the Solstice Advanced Materials spin-off is complete and an updated segment structure was announced ahead of the Aerospace spin-off (Honeywell, Oct 2025). No fire or life-safety-specific revenue line for Honeywell Building Automation exists in the public record.

**Securitas AB (OM: SECU B)** holds Securitas Technology, which reported FY2025 revenue of **$3,459,840,000** and recurring monthly revenue of **$102,000,000** at 2025-12-31, both survey estimates, ranking second on the SDM 100 (SDM 100, 2026). It completed the STANLEY Security acquisition on 2022-07-22 at **$3,200 million** on a debt- and cash-free basis (Stanley Black & Decker; Securitas AB).

**Cintas Corporation (NASDAQ: CTAS)** runs a Fire Protection Services operating segment covering extinguishers, sprinkler and alarm testing and special hazard suppression, reported inside the **"All Other"** reportable segment. All Other revenue was **$1,146,018 thousand** in FY2025 against **$1,064,082 thousand** in FY2024, on total company revenue of **$10,340,181 thousand** (Cintas FY2025 Form 10-K). Fire Protection Services is not separately disclosed and is strictly smaller than the segment. Cintas buys extinguisher businesses directly, including White Fire Extinguisher Inc.

**ADT Inc. (NYSE: ADT)** has left commercial fire. It sold its commercial business to GTCR, and then sold its B2B multifamily segments to that same business, now Everon. FY2025 revenue was **$4,354,000,000** on recurring monthly revenue of **$358,700,000** (SDM 100, 2026). ADT is a seller of commercial fire assets, not a bidder for them.

**The negative findings, which matter as much as the positive ones.** A reader will assume the large listed mechanical and electrical contractors carry a fire line. They do not disclose one.

- **Comfort Systems USA (NYSE: FIX)** describes its mechanical segment as one that *"principally includes HVAC, plumbing, piping and controls, as well as off-site construction, monitoring and fire protection"* (Form 10-Q, period ended 2025-06-30). That is the whole of it. Revenue for the six months ended 2025-06-30 was **$4,004,605 thousand** and FY2025 revenue was **$9,101.6 million**. No percentage or dollar breakout by trade exists in the filings, so no fire-protection denominator for Comfort Systems exists in the public record.
- **EMCOR Group (NYSE: EME)** reported FY2025 revenues of **$16.99 billion** against $14.57 billion in FY2024, with US Mechanical Construction and Facilities at **$7,050.5 million**, 42% of the total (Businesswire, 2026-02-26). The results release contains no mention of fire protection or life safety as a distinct service line.
- **Bosch Group** appears in this market only as the buyer of JCI's residential HVAC. No US fire sprinkler or suppression contracting asset under Bosch appears in the public record.

Neither Comfort Systems nor EMCOR is a disclosed fire consolidator. Both are credible acquirers of mechanical contractors that happen to carry sprinkler capability, and neither can be benchmarked on fire economics, because those economics are not broken out.

## 4.3 Sponsor-backed platforms

Revenue is not in the public record for almost any fire-led platform in this class. The exceptions are narrow and specific: the platforms that also file into the SDM 100 survey report revenue and recurring monthly revenue there, and CertaSite's revenue entered the record only because a public acquirer had to state it. Empty cells below are figures absent from the public record, not omissions, and no revenue has been derived from headcount or location count.

| Zeus Fire and Security | Access Holdings, platform formed 2022-02-22 | | 2022-02-22 | fire in the corporate name; two UL-listed monitoring centres | PA, NJ, MD, IL, MO, WI, HI | over 750 employees; FY2025 revenue $205,859,618, RMR $6,467,326, SDM 100 #11; 14 member brands |

**Security 101** passed from Gemspring Capital to **Morgan Stanley Capital Partners** on 2026-02-24. Neither the acquisition announcement nor the company's own site names fire or life safety anywhere; the service description is access control, video surveillance, intrusion detection and visitor management. It is excluded from this register, and the exclusion is the finding: a sponsor-backed integrator of that size with no fire line will not appear as a bidder for fire assets.

**Owner not established.** Two fire-relevant platforms of scale have no owner in the public record. **ORR Protection** operates 15 branches serving 50 states under the Mission Critical Fire Protection positioning, reported FY2025 revenue of **$169,428,932** and recurring monthly revenue of **$1,254,129**, ranking #32 on the SDM 100, is led by CEO **Woodie Andrawos**, previously president of National Monitoring Center, and has acquired **Compass Fire Protection** (Aug 2025, union sprinkler capability and Pacific Northwest presence) and **Detection & Suppression International** (Oct 2024, Texas). **CPI Security Systems Inc.** reported FY2025 revenue of **$185,000,000** and recurring monthly revenue of **$13,100,000**, ranking #8. For a buyer, an active acquirer with no identified owner is the most dangerous entry in a register: it cannot be graded on hold period, and it may or may not be a competing bidder.

## 4.4 Permanent capital, family and strategic holders

Different underwriting logic applies here, and it has two consequences a buyer should price in. These holders do not create the supply of secondary assets a sponsor does, so none of them will return to market on a fund clock. And they compete differently for deals, because they can win a founder-led process on continuity and values rather than on price.

| Holder | Asset | Basis of holding | Scale evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Markel Group (NYSE: MKL)**, Markel Ventures | **VSC Fire & Security, Inc.** | agreement announced 2019-11-08, close expected Q4 2019; additional terms not disclosed | over 1,200 people; 21 offices across 9 states and the District of Columbia; founded 1958 in Ashland VA as Virginia Sprinkler Company; has itself acquired Arkansas Automatic Sprinkler and United Fire Suppression |
| **Minimax Viking** | **Cosco Fire Protection**, **Cosco DAS**, **Firetrol Protection Systems, Inc.** | Gryphon Investors held Consolidated Fire Protection, LLC from 2005 and exited in **2007** to *"MX Mercury Beteiligungen GMBH ('Minimax'), a global fire and life safety company owned by Industrial Kapital and management"* (Gryphon Investors portfolio page, retrieved 2026-07-29) | Cosco branches in Fresno, Los Angeles, Sacramento, San Diego, San Francisco and San Juan Capistrano CA; Seattle, Spokane and Vancouver WA; Vancouver OR; Denver CO; Las Vegas NV |
| **Mark Ein** (entrepreneur-investor, Chairman) | **Kastle Systems International** | individual owner, not a fund | FY2025 revenue $282,000,000, RMR $12,800,000, SDM 100 #9; took a majority stake in **i2G Systems** (Sterling VA) in March 2025, described as its biggest-ever investment |
| **Armstrong Group** | **Guardian Protection** | widely reported, not primary-verified | FY2025 revenue $214,411,000, RMR $12,762,000, SDM 100 #10; acquired **Monitronics' Commercial Alarm Accounts Division** on 2025-03-27 — 8,300 commercial and 4,300 residential accounts in PA, WV and OH, adding branches in Lancaster PA, Morgantown WV and Williamsport PA, its fourth acquisition since 2021 |
| **The Philadelphia Contributionship** | **Vector Security** | partnered since **1982**; no ownership percentage appears on the holder's own page and none is assumed here | FY2025 revenue $454,590,000, RMR $20,075,000, SDM 100 #5 |
| **Westphal family** | **Bay Alarm Company** | family-owned | FY2025 revenue $263,766,868, RMR $17,240,000, SDM 100 #7 |
| **Family-owned** | **Per Mar Security Services** | 70th anniversary noted in 2023 | FY2025 revenue $158,462,703, RMR $5,197,677, SDM 100 #14 |
| **Independent** | **Guardian Alarm Company** | | SDM 100 #13 |

Markel is the clearest case and the one most often left out of sponsor trackers. An insurance balance sheet buying a sprinkler contractor is a structurally different competitor from a fund with a five-year hold, and Markel's own release quotes the seller on values and continuity rather than price: VSC CEO **Michael Meehan** said *"we knew we needed a partner that shared our values and would allow us to continue running VSC the way we have."* Minimax Viking matters for a different reason: the western-US sprinkler franchise that trackers still attribute to a private-equity sponsor has been strategically held for nearly two decades, and neither Cosco's nor Firetrol's own site states an owner.

## 4.5 Manufacturers under sponsors

Product platforms are underwritten separately from route businesses. They carry engineering and channel economics rather than inspection density, they matter to a services buyer chiefly as supplier-side counterparties, and their brand names are the single most dangerous class of token in a target register.

| Platform | Sponsor | Transaction | Brands and domains | Scale evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Kidde Global Solutions** | Lone Star Funds | Carrier's Commercial and Residential Fire business, closed **2024-12-02** at **$3 billion** enterprise value (Carrier; Businesswire, 2024-12-01) | Kidde (kidde.com) · Badger (badgerfire.com) · Edwards Signaling (edwards-signals.com) · FIREX (kiddepro.com) · Edwards (edwardsfiresafety.com) · Kidde Commercial (kidde-esfire.com) · GST (gst.com.cn) · Aritech (firesecurityproducts.com) | a product platform, not a services roll-up |
| **Spectrum Safety Solutions** | Sentinel Capital Partners | Carrier's Industrial Fire business, closed **2024-07-02** at **$1.425 billion** enterprise value (Sentinel Capital Partners; Carrier, 2024-03-05) | spectrum-safety.com · Autronica (autronicafire.com) · Det-Tronics (det-tronics.com) · Fireye | ≈1,400 employees in more than 20 countries; HQ Stamford CT; no US branch network published |
| **Marioff** | Inflexion | agreed **2026-03-31**; terms not disclosed (Sentinel Capital Partners, PRNewswire, 2026-03-31) | marioff.com; HI-FOG high-pressure water mist | serves more than 70 countries across marine and land-based segments; ≈660 people |

The strategic that exited fire entirely is **Carrier**. Both sponsor-owned manufacturer platforms above exist only because Carrier left, and the sequence inside Spectrum is the more instructive half of that story: Sentinel bought a four-brand bundle in July 2024 and agreed to sell one of the four twenty months later, leaving Spectrum focused on *"its market-leading detection and monitoring businesses Autronica, Det-Tronics, and Fireye."* Special-hazard product brands clear separately.

The register consequence is stated in §4.6 and repeated here because it inverts the usual failure mode. A US business whose name contains **Kidde**, **Badger**, **Edwards**, **FIREX**, **GST** or **Aritech** is overwhelmingly a dealer, distributor or independent service company using the product, not an owned entity. Name-matching those tokens into an "already owned" bucket suppresses genuinely independent targets.

Two absences carry weight. Pye-Barker publishes 144 brand names and no brand-level domain, which is the largest single unmatched estate in the industry by count. Encore's 75 named brand partners likewise carry no domains and no geography. Both are name-only rosters attached to the two most acquisitive platforms in the sector.

### Why a target list must be matched on domain, not name

The traps below are not edge cases. They are the ordinary condition of this market, and each one produces a specific, silent error: a name match either buries an independent target inside an owned bucket, or surfaces an already-owned business as available.

**Name-collision traps.**

| Token | Owners and nameplates | Resolution |
|---|---|---|
| **Guardian** | **Five** unrelated entities: Guardian Fire Services (Investcorp, Nashville TN) · Guardian Fire Protection Services (Knox Lane, Rockville MD) · Guardian Protection (Armstrong Group) · Guardian Alarm Company (independent) · "Guardian" as a named Encore affiliated brand partner | domain only, maximum severity |
| **Absolute** | **Four** nameplates under three owners plus one variant: Absolute Fire Protection under Viking Fire Protection Group (APi, Midwest) · Absolute Fire Protection under Marmic (Paragould AR) · Absolute Fire Protection under National Fire & Safety (Glendale AZ, absolutefireaz.com) · Sciens Absolute Protective Division (Piscataway NJ) | domain plus state |
| **Elite Fire** | Sciens Elite Fire Division (Detroit MI) · Elite Fire Protection Systems (National Fire & Safety) · Elite Action Fire (Encore) | geography or domain |
| **Allstate** | Allstate Fire Protection (Pye-Barker) · Allstate Fire Equipment and Allstate Fire Equipment of Rhode Island (Encore) · plus Allstate the insurer | domain only |
| **Reliable Fire** | Reliable Fire (Pye-Barker) · Reliable Fire Protection (Encore) · plus Reliable Automatic Sprinkler Co., an unrelated manufacturer | domain only |
| **National Fire & Safety** | the Highview platform (natfiresafety.com) · National Fire Suppression under Western States Fire Protection (APi; Decatur IL and Kansas City KS) · "National Fire & Safety Solutions" listed by Encore | domain only; the exact phrase is still unsafe |
| **Superior Fire Protection** | Pye-Barker (Montrose CO) · Encore affiliated brand partner | domain only |
| **Star Fire / Starfire** | Star Fire Protection (Altus, trading on starfireny.com) · Starfire Systems and Starfire Extinguisher (CertaSite, Wisconsin) | domain only |
| **Approved** | Approved Protection Systems and Approved Safety and Security (CertaSite) · Approved Fire Protection (Encore) | domain only |
| **Advanced Fire** | Advanced Fire (CertaSite, Pennsylvania, now trading as CertaSite) · Advanced Fire Protection (APi National Service Group) | domain only |
| **Premier** | Premier Fire & Security (APi, Paducah KY) · Premier Electronics (CertaSite, Michigan) · Premier Security Solutions (Pavion) | domain only |
| **Mid-Atlantic Fire Protection** | Mid Atlantic Fire Protection under American Fire Protection Group (APi, Virginia Beach VA) · Mid-Atlantic Fire Protection (Encore) | domain only |
| **Total Fire** | Total Fire (Fire Safety and Protection, founded 1998) · Total Fire & Safety, LLC (Marmic) · Total LifeSafety Corporation (Pye-Barker) | domain only |
| **Alpine** | Alpine Fire and Safety Systems (Pye-Barker) · Alpine Sprinkler (Encore) | domain only |
| **AAA Fire** | three Pye-Barker variants — AAA Fire & Safety Equipment, AAA Fire Safety & Alarm, AAA Fire Extinguisher · AAA Fire Services (Encore) | domain only |
| **Coastal** | Coastal Sprinkler (Pye-Barker, Beaumont TX) · Coastal Fire Protection (Encore) | domain only |
| **ASG** | listed by Zeus Fire and Security **and** by Pye-Barker | domain only |
| **United Alarm Service(s)** | United Alarm Service Inc. (Pye-Barker, Brookfield CT) · UAS / United Alarm Services (Zeus) · United Fire Alarm Service (APi, Murrells Inlet SC) | domain only |
| **Empire** | Sciens Empire Division (Miami FL) · Empire Fire & Safety (Pye-Barker) | domain or geography |
| **Western … Fire Protection** | Western States Fire Protection (APi, 45+ western US locations) · Sciens Western Fire Protection Division (Poway CA) | full string required |
| **Summit** | the SFP Holding brands · Summit Pipeline Services ULC (APi, non-fire) · heavy reuse industry-wide | full string or domain |
| **FIREX** | a Kidde Global Solutions manufacturer brand **and** a named Encore affiliated brand partner | domain only |
| **Kidde, Badger, Edwards, GST, Aritech** | manufacturer brands; matches will be dealers and distributors, not owned entities | domain only |
| **Cosco** | collides with Costco and with COSCO Shipping | domain only |
| **Firetrol** | collides with the Firetrol fire-pump controller lineage | domain only |
| **Minuteman** | collides with Minuteman Press and Minuteman International | match *Minuteman Security* or the domain |
| **Sonitrol** | a franchise network; Pye-Barker owns some franchises — Charleston and The Midlands, Louisville and Western Kentucky, New England, SW Ohio, Upstate South Carolina, Pacific, the Carolinas, South Carolina — and not all franchisees nationwide | domain only |
| Short acronyms — **NEFS, FSI, OSI, ESS, WSA, FS&S, LPS, ASG, HEIM, SEi, PASS, SGTS, VSC, ORR, ACT, IFP, NFS, TFA, RCI, GW, 3S, DU** | four characters or fewer; these collide across industries | never match bare initialisms |
| Surname-only tokens — **Gorham, Franklin, Patterson, Kimble, Craynon, Teasley, Hamrick, Eastman, Spears, Flannery, Rich, Ellis** | match unrelated firms in every trade | name plus state, or domain |

**Two sponsor-name coincidences that are not what they look like.** **Blackstone Fire Control** was acquired by Impact Fire and has nothing to do with Blackstone the sponsor — which happens to own Impact Fire's parent, AI Fire, making the coincidence actively misleading rather than merely awkward. **Sentinel Fire Safety** is a named Encore affiliated brand partner and has nothing to do with Sentinel Capital Partners, which owns Spectrum Safety Solutions.

One sponsor name is also rendered two ways in print. ABF Journal's 2025 headline gives Pye-Barker's majority holder as *"Atlas"*; the sponsor's own newsroom and the PRNewswire release give **Altas Partners**. Both renderings circulate; the sponsor is Altas.

**Stale-rebrand traps.**

- **Sciens prefixed rather than retired.** All 31 legacy nameplates now trade as *"Sciens \<Legacy\> Division"* — W.W. Gay Fire, Bass United, ARK Systems, Mammoth, Anchor Fire, Cen-Cal Fire Systems, Standard Electronics, Time & Alarm, Alarmtechs, Christian Cable, Absolute Protective, Classic Protection, Educational Electronics, LS Systems, Elite Fire, West Fire, Southern Fire, Empire, Sabah, Western Fire Protection, FSI, FS&S, ESS, OSI, WSA and the rest. A list carrying them as independents is wrong 31 times from one platform.
- **Pye-Barker uses two formulas that mean different things.** *"Pye-Barker Fire & Safety, formerly Moore Fire Protection"* means the legacy sign is down. *"Cascade Fire & Security, A Pye-Barker Fire & Safety Company"* means the legacy sign is still up and a name match still works. Both are equally owned, and only the second is still findable by name.
- **Marmic rebrands everything.** There is no brand family and no sub-brand pages; every acquired business becomes Marmic Fire & Safety. Marmic must be caught by domain and by legacy names such as Balco Systems (formerly Sound Photo, 1965), Northwest Fire Protection, West Memphis Fire Extinguisher, Kansas City Fire & Security, F.L. Sons Fire Equipment Company, Total Fire & Safety and Fire Control Systems.
- **ADT Commercial and ADT Multifamily are Everon.** ADT Inc. still exists as a separate residential company, so "ADT" must not resolve to Everon.
- **Protegis Fire & Safety and Performance Systems Integration are absorbed** into Summit Fire & Security, and neither appears as a live trading brand on Summit's own branch-locations pages.
- **Corbett Technology Solutions (CTSI) is Pavion.**
- **Convergint's twelve acquired names** — JSC Systems, Ballou Fire Systems, Esscoe, A+ Technology & Security Solutions, Digital Visions, Fiber Solutions, Simpson Security Systems, Delco Security, Panavideo, MVP Tech, Nusource Financial, Helinick — are absorbed and useful only as stale-list entries.
- **The Davis-Ulmer family renamed from sprinkler to fire.** Davis-Ulmer Sprinkler Co. is Davis-Ulmer Fire Protection; Cogswell Sprinkler Company is Cogswell Sprinkler; Grunau Company, Inc. is Grunau Fire Protection; W&M Sprinkler Company, Inc. is W&M Fire & Security; SRI Fire Sprinkler, LLC is SRI Fire & Security; Beach Lake Sprinkler Corp is Beach Lake Sprinkler; Valley Fire Protection Services, LLC is Valley Fire Protection under Viking Fire Protection Group.

**The current subsidiary exhibit under-counts live trading names.** APi's FY2025 EX-21 exhibit, filed 2026-02-25 under accession 0001628280-26-011620, runs 203 rows. Legacy US operating entities are being deleted from the exhibit as they merge into consolidation vehicles: **Western States Fire Protection Company, Grunau Company, Inc., Security Fire Protection Company, Inc., Metropolitan Mechanical Contractors, Inc., Tessier's Inc., Sunland Fire Protection, Inc., Delta Fire Systems, Inc., Landmark Sprinkler, Inc.** and **Olsen Fire Protection, Inc.** all appear in the 2020 and FY2021 exhibits and are absent from FY2025, while **APi Group Life Safety USA LLC** and **Sprinkler Acquisition LLC** persist. Every one of those deleted names is still a live trading nameplate with a branch, a licence and a truck livery. The current exhibit alone under-counts the register by roughly thirty names, and the 2020 S-4 exhibit — 92 rows, the richest legacy-name list — has to be read alongside it.

**Domain traps.**

| Trap | Detail |
|---|---|
| `pyebarkerfire.com` → `pyebarkerfs.com` | 302 redirect; register both |
| `sciensbuildingsolutions.com` → `sciensusa.com` | 302 redirect; register both |
| `vfpfire.com` → `vfpg.com`; `grunaufire.com` and `cogswellsprinkler.com` → `davisulmer.com` | 302 redirects; register all five |
| Guardian Fire Protection Services | publishes on two unrelated-looking domains, `guardianfireprotection.com` and `ars-guardian.com`; a matcher holding only the first misses every business trading on the second |
| CertaSite | the domain is `certasitepro.com`, not the obvious construction |
| Star Fire Protection (Altus) | trades on `starfireny.com`; the brand string is absent from the domain |
| Summit Fire & Security | Summit's own locations page links a WP Engine staging host; the production domain is `summitfiresecurity.com` |
| `spectrumsafetysolutions.com` | not the Sentinel carve-out — an unrelated fire-safety business serving the Indian market, whose brands are Fike, Fireco, DC and Signifire. The real domain is `spectrum-safety.com` |
| `encorefp.com` | not Encore's site; `encorefireprotection.com` is |
| Adcock Systems | Altus's own page renders the brand text as *"Addock Systems"* while the domain it publishes is `adcocksystems.com`; both spellings are carried |
| Frontier Fire, RCI Fire Systems, Texas Fire Alarm | no standalone domains; all resolve into `natfiresafety.com` sub-pages |
| `apigroupinc.com` | the operating-brand layer is reachable only through the per-company detail pages and the SEC exhibits, not through a brand or locations endpoint |

## 4.7 Add-on cadence

The bifurcation is the finding. One platform is closing deals at roughly an order of magnitude above everyone else, and a sponsor entering this market is not competing against one cadence but against two very different ones at once.

| Platform | Add-ons | Period | Implied cadence | Attribution |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Pye-Barker Fire & Safety** | **57** | calendar 2025 | **≈4.75 closings per month** | PRNewswire, 2026-03-17 |
| CertaSite (under Riverside) | 21st add-on at 2023-09-13; **26th** at 2024-03-13 | Sep 2023 – Mar 2024 | **5 add-ons in 6 months** | The Riverside Company, 2023-09-13 and 2024-03-13 |
| Guardian Fire Services (Nashville) | **12** | 2022 – 2025-12-03 | **≈3.0 per year** | Investcorp, 2025-12-03 |
| Altus Fire and Life Safety | **8** | 2021 – 2024 | **≈2.7 per year** | Lincoln International |
| Sciens Building Solutions | **13** since the 2016 formation; **ten** from 2019 | 2016 – Dec 2021 | **≈2.6 per year** on the 13-count | Huron Capital, 2021-12-15 |
| National Fire & Safety | **6** dated add-ons | 2019-09-16 – 2022-03-01 | **≈2.4 per year** | Businesswire, six releases |
| Relay Fire and Safety | **3rd** add-on | to 2025-09-16 | | The Riverside Company, 2025-09-16 |
| AI Fire | **20** under TruArc | Mar 2021 – Feb 2025 | | TruArc Partners, 2025-02-06 |
| Performance Systems Integration (under Riverside) | **16** | to 2025-08-12 | | The Riverside Company, 2025-08-12 |
| Encore Fire Protection | **70+** acquisitions; **75** brand partners named; *"over 60 successful partnerships integrated"* | since inception | not annualisable | Permira portfolio page; encorefireprotection.com |
| Summit Companies / SFP Holding | **55** strategic acquisitions, **82** in total since the 1999 founding | to 2021 | | Summit Fire & Security, 2021-09-20 |
| Marmic Fire & Safety | *"over 30 companies"* joined | since inception | | marmicfire.com |
| Convergint | **over 40** acquisitions | since 2018 | | Leonard Green & Partners |
| APi Group | not counted by number | FY2025; Q1 2026 | **$186 million** of cash acquisitions in FY2025; more than **$1 billion** signed or closed across three deals in Q1 2026 | APi Group IR, 2026-02-26 and 2026-04-30 |

Pye-Barker's 2025 alone consumed 57 sellers, most of them small enough that no other platform on this list was structured to absorb them at that rate. Below it sits a long tail running two to three deliberate deals a year, and one dense pocket in the middle: CertaSite added five businesses in six months while Riverside was preparing it for a strategic exit. Both patterns matter and they are priced differently. Against a 57-a-year absorber, a sub-$5 million seller in a covered metro will already have been called. Against a 2.5-a-year platform, the same seller may never have been.

Two supporting figures on the direction of travel. Pye-Barker grew 587% across 2021 to 2024, and its recurring monthly revenue went from **$15.3 million** in 2023 to **$50,391,844** at 2025-12-31 — a movement that is overwhelmingly acquisition-driven and should not be read as organic. Performance Systems Integration, over its Riverside hold, went from 4 locations to 14, from 108 employees to over 450, and from 64 technicians to 279, ending with 21,000+ customers before Summit Fire & Security bought it.

## 4.8 Control changes, last 24 months

Most large transaction figures in this market reach print through trade press citing unnamed sources, with the parties declining to disclose terms. In the table below, values marked **Disclosed** were published by a party or a filer; values attributed to an outlet were not confirmed by any party, and in several cases the parties' own releases state that terms were not disclosed. Both states are shown rather than blended.

**Control changes** — the owner changed, and each one resets a hold clock and sets a reference point for the next seller.

| Date | Entity | From | To | Value |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2024-07-02 | Carrier Industrial Fire → **Spectrum Safety Solutions** | Carrier (corporate) | Sentinel Capital Partners | **$1.425 billion** EV, Disclosed (Carrier, 2024-03-05) |
| 2024-07-24 | **Marmic Fire & Safety** | HGGC | KKR | not disclosed |
| 2024-12-02 | Carrier Commercial and Residential Fire → **Kidde Global Solutions** | Carrier (corporate) | Lone Star Funds | **$3 billion** EV, Disclosed (Carrier) |
| 2024-12-10 | **Becklar** | BV Investment Partners (rolled its stake) | Graham Partners | not disclosed |
| 2025-02-06 | **AI Fire** (Impact Fire, Academy Fire) | TruArc Partners (retained a stake) | Blackstone | seller's release names neither buyer nor price; **≈$1.1 billion including debt** (Bloomberg, 2025-02-07) |
| announced 2025-02-06, sponsors' releases 2025-03-12 | **Encore Fire Protection** | Levine Leichtman Capital Partners | Permira | not disclosed by any party; **$1.8 billion** (Bloomberg, 2025-02-06; Alternatives Watch, 2025-03-13) |
| 2025-08-04 | **Summit Companies / SFP Holding** — majority stake | BlackRock Long Term Private Capital | **BDT & MSD Partners** | not disclosed (Summit Companies newsroom, 2025-08-04) |
| 2025-08-12 | **Performance Systems Integration** | The Riverside Company | Summit Fire & Security | not disclosed |
| 2025-10-01 | **ADT's B2B multifamily segments** → Everon | ADT Inc. | Everon (GTCR) | Everon's release states no price; **$55 million** (Dallas Innovates) |
| 2025-10-28 | **FSS Technologies** — new platform | | Lightview Capital, with Glass Lake Holdings | not disclosed |
| 2025-12-03 | **Guardian Fire Services** (Nashville) | Northern Lakes Capital | Investcorp | not disclosed |
| announced 2025-12-10, closed 2026-02-02 | **CertaSite** | The Riverside Company | **APi Group (NYSE: APG)** — strategic, funded from cash on hand | not disclosed; CertaSite FY2025 revenue *"approximately $90 million"*, Disclosed (APi Group, 2025-12-10) |
| 2026-02-24 | **Security 101** | Gemspring Capital | Morgan Stanley Capital Partners | not disclosed |
| 2026-03-31 | **Marioff**, a division of Spectrum Safety Solutions | Sentinel Capital Partners | Inflexion | not disclosed |
| 2026-06-08 | **Onyx-Fire Protection Services, Inc.** (Canada) | | APi Group | annual revenue *"approximately $190 million"*, Disclosed |
| 2026-07-01 | **WTech Fire Group** (Europe) | | APi Group | annual revenue *"approximately $175 million"*, Disclosed |

Fifteen control changes in twenty-four months across fire services, fire products and the monitoring infrastructure beneath them. Two of them — Marmic and Spectrum — sit days outside the window and are shown because registers routinely mis-state both.

**Minority rounds — not control changes.** Pye-Barker took minority investments from an **ADIA** subsidiary and from **GIC**, closing January 2025, while **Altas Partners** retained its majority and **Leonard Green & Partners** invested significant new capital; financial terms were not disclosed (PRNewswire, 2025-01-10). This is capital in, not a seller out, and it does not produce a secondary asset.

**Continuation vehicles — not exits.** Convergint's sponsors closed an **$850 million single-asset continuation vehicle** on 2026-03-02, led by Leonard Green's Sage Fund with Vintage Strategies at Goldman Sachs Alternatives also investing (Leonard Green & Partners, 2026-03-02). The signal runs the opposite way to a sale: the largest security systems integrator in the country, one that names fire and life safety in its own service description, has just been recapitalised rather than sold. It stays in the market as a bidder and is under no pressure to exit.

**Asset and book purchases — a third category.** Guardian Protection's purchase of Monitronics' Commercial Alarm Accounts Division on 2025-03-27 moved 8,300 commercial and 4,300 residential accounts, not a platform. Books of accounts move on different terms from companies and should not be counted as platform transactions.

# PART V — REGIONALITY & COVERAGE


## 5.1 What can be mapped, and what cannot

City-level branch rosters exist, and they are genuinely useful, but only for a named subset of platform units. They are **named cities, not MSA codes**, and **no platform publishes a branch count per metro**. What follows is the honest extent of the base.

**Units with a published city-level roster:**

| Unit | Owner | Cities on the record |
|---|---|---|
| Western States Fire Protection | APi Group | **36** WSFP-branded office entries across 35 distinct cities, plus 5 sub-brand nameplates — 41 named offices against a stated *"More Than 45 Locations Across the Western U.S."* |
| Marmic Fire & Safety | KKR | **53** branch cities across 20 states |
| Summit Fire Protection | BDT & MSD Partners | **34** named cities across AZ, CO, IA, MI, MN, NE, ND, SD, WI |
| Encore Fire Protection | Permira | **33** branches across 13 states |
| Sciens Building Solutions | Carlyle | **31** divisions, each with a named city |
| Viking Fire Protection Group | APi Group | **21** named cities |
| CertaSite | APi Group | locations page states **17** locations and enumerates **16** cities |
| American Fire Protection Group | APi Group | **13** named offices |
| Cosco Fire Protection | Minimax Viking | **12** named city entries across CA, WA, OR, CO, NV |
| National Fire & Safety | Highview Capital | **8** named cities across 5 states; the 2022 release describes seven western states |
| Davis-Ulmer family | APi Group | partial — Rochester NY, Darien CT, Hawthorne NY, Holbrook NY, Plantsville CT and regional descriptions only |
| International Fire Protection | APi Group | 12 cities stated across 14 southern states, of which AL, AR, FL, GA, SC and TN are named |
| Everon | GTCR | partial — only the retained NewTech Systems nameplate carries named cities: Ashland KY, Lexington KY, Groveport OH, Bridgeville and Washington PA, Dunbar WV |

**Platforms that publish no city-level footprint at all.** AI Fire names states and district-office counts but no cities. Altus Fire and Life Safety names 12 Northeast states plus Georgia and Pennsylvania, with per-brand domains but no branch cities. Convergint reports over 220 technology centers by region only. Pavion reports 70+ US locations and 23 countries with no branch list. Zeus Fire and Security names seven states. Guardian Fire Services names 17 branches by region. Guardian Fire Protection Services names 9 locations across 4 states. ORR Protection names 15 branches serving 50 states. ASPYRE names no members and no locations. Spectrum Safety Solutions publishes no US branch network. Relay Fire and Safety names one add-on city.

**How much of the largest roster is captured.** Pye-Barker states 253 locations across 47 states, with 256 location URLs enumerated. Its canonical brands page names 144 businesses with no domains and no cities; a further **≈60** trading names carry a named city. City-level detail therefore exists for roughly a quarter of the largest estate in the industry, and the platform closing 57 deals a year is the one whose geography is least completely on the record.

**The instruction that follows from this.** No saturation index, share-of-metro figure, or ranking of under-consolidated territories should be published off this base. Three defects make it unfit for that purpose: named cities cannot be aggregated to MSAs without a crosswalk, no unit publishes branches per metro, and the two largest rosters — Pye-Barker's and Encore's — are the least complete. Any metro-level density claim requires a licence registry or an equivalent independent second source. The material below is a screening base for finding overlap that demonstrably exists, and nothing more.

## 5.3 Whitespace signals

These are screening priors, not conclusions. The mapping in §5.1 is incomplete by construction, and in this market absence of evidence is emphatically not evidence of absence — several states that read as empty from state-level disclosures alone turn out to carry three or four owners once branch rosters are read.

**States appearing in no verified operating footprint: Alaska alone.** Every other state appears in at least one named platform's published roster. The only Alaska presence anywhere in the record is Heat Trace Services, Inc., an Alaska-incorporated entity in APi's 2020 subsidiary exhibit and absent from the current one. For a buyer, one state out of fifty is the honest measure of unclaimed territory at the state level, and it is a small and structurally difficult state.

**The interior West and Plains are not empty, and a register that treats them as empty will be wrong.** Idaho carries 3-D Fire Protection at Idaho Falls and Delta Fire Systems at Meridian and Twin Falls, both under Western States Fire Protection, plus Pye-Barker nameplates at Boise, Nampa and Hailey. Wyoming carries a Western States office at Casper and appears in National Fire & Safety's service areas. North Dakota carries Summit Fire Protection at Fargo and Williston and ICS at Grand Forks. South Dakota carries Summit at Rapid City and Sioux Falls, a Western States office at Aberdeen, and a Pye-Barker nameplate at Rapid City. Nebraska carries Summit at Lincoln and Omaha, VFP Fire Systems at Omaha, Marmic at Omaha, and Pye-Barker nameplates at Gering, Kearney and North Platte. Kansas carries Marmic at Wichita, Topeka and Olathe, National Fire Suppression at Kansas City, and a Pye-Barker nameplate at Garden City. New Mexico carries A-Com Technologies and a Western States office at Albuquerque. Montana carries Western States offices at Bozeman, Kalispell and Missoula and a Pye-Barker nameplate at Black Eagle. Oklahoma carries four owners: Western States at Oklahoma City and Tulsa, Marmic at Oklahoma City, Tulsa and Lawton, Pye-Barker nameplates at Choctaw, Midwest City and Oklahoma City, and Kanske Fire Systems under Impact Fire.

**States a major platform entered for the first time recently.** The most acquisitive platform in the country names *"entrances into Hawaii and Arkansas"* as 2025 events (PRNewswire, 2026-03-17). Pye-Barker had no presence in either state before 2025, which makes both the strongest primary-source whitespace indicator available — not because those states are unoccupied now, but because the buyer with the widest funnel had not reached them until then.

**Two further directional signals, both from sponsors' own choices.** Percheron launched ASPYRE in November 2025 with a foundational partner in the **Southern United States** and a stated intent to expand nationally, which is that sponsor's own assessment of where unclaimed density remained as of late 2025. And ORR Protection's acquisition of Compass Fire Protection in August 2025 was framed as adding union sprinkler capability and **Pacific Northwest** presence, which identifies a capability gap rather than a geographic one.

**A signal about service lines rather than metros.** Summit Fire & Security runs single-service branches: **extinguishers only** at Portland OR and Seattle WA, and a dedicated **marine** branch at Fort Lauderdale FL. A platform that opens a single-line branch in a metro is present without being dense there, and the remaining lines in that metro are open ground. Those three cities are the only places in the verified rosters where a platform states its own coverage is partial by service line.
