# PART VI — VALUATION & THE DEAL RECORD


## 6.1 Five non-comparable populations

Every recurring error in this market's valuation commentary is a population error. A multiple here is meaningless without the population it was struck in, and the five populations below are reported separately throughout. They are not blended anywhere in this report, and no range in this report spans two of them.

| # | Population | Definition applied | Observations carrying a multiple |
|---|---|---|---|
| **P1** | **Platform / carve-out** | A business of roughly $50M+ EBITDA bought by a sponsor or a strategic, with its own management, brand and P&L | 3 with a disclosed or derivable EBITDA multiple |
| **P2** | **Bolt-on / tuck-in** | A $1–5M EBITDA independent bought by an existing platform | 0 with a per-deal disclosed multiple; 1 programme-level disclosed threshold (APi, `<6x`) |
| **P3** | **RMR multiple** | A multiple of *monthly* recurring revenue, the alarm and monitoring convention | 2 populations: size-banded market averages, and one disclosed bulk-account transaction |
| **P4** | **Public trading comparables** | Listed acquirers and adjacents, EV/EBITDA and EV/Revenue at a stated observation date | 10 tickers, vendor data dates 2026-06-07 to 2026-07-29 |
| **P5** | **Product / OEM adjacency** | Fire detection, suppression and gas-detection *manufacturers*, not service contractors | 3 transactions; 1 with a derivable EBITDA multiple |

P5 exists because the three largest 2024–2026 transactions carrying a disclosed enterprise value — Spectrum Safety Solutions, Kidde Global Solutions and Autronica — are manufacturers. Reading them as fire-services comparables is the most available category error in the record.

Four further populations appear in this Part as benchmarks and are equally never blended with the five above or with each other: **industry-agnostic size-banded private transactions** (GF Data), **portfolio marks** rather than transactions (Lincoln International's ≈7,100 US private companies valued for private funds, median $60.6M LTM Adjusted EBITDA, at 11.7x EV/LTM Adjusted EBITDA, Q1 2026 data), **banker survey expectations** (Capstone's 6.8x "typical" and 9.8x "premium" for 2026, sample size not disclosed), and **continuation vehicles**, which are valuation events without a competitive clearing price — priced by a lead secondary buyer against an NAV reference rather than by an auction.

## 6.2 Public trading comparables

All rows from a market-data vendor's statistics pages, retrieved 2026-07-29, with each row carrying the vendor's own stated data date rather than a uniform observation date.

| Ticker | Company | Enterprise value | EV/EBITDA | EV/Revenue | EV/EBIT | Vendor data date | Relevance |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| APG | APi Group | $19.41B | **21.47** | 2.37 | 33.88 | 2026-07-28 | Pure-play life-safety services consolidator |
| JCI | Johnson Controls | $94.41B | **26.36** | 3.86 | 33.99 | 2026-07-29 | Fire (Tyco/Simplex/Ansul) plus building management |
| FIX | Comfort Systems USA | $55.61B | **27.74** | 4.95 | 30.07 | 2026-07-29 | Mechanical/electrical contractor including fire protection |
| EME | EMCOR Group | $30.97B | **15.52** | 1.74 | 17.22 | 2026-07-29 | Mechanical/electrical contractor |
| ADT | ADT Inc. | $12.94B | **7.62** | 2.52 | 39.75 | 2026-07-29 | Residential monitoring, post-Commercial divestiture |
| CARR | Carrier Global | $63.51B | **22.59** | 2.87 | 41.56 | 2026-07-28 | Former owner of both divested fire businesses |
| MSA | MSA Safety | $7.28B | **15.80** | 3.80 | 18.81 | 2026-07-24 | Buyer of Autronica; safety and detection products |
| CTAS | Cintas | $89.71B | **28.76** | 7.96 | 34.42 | 2026-07-29 | Uniform and facility services with a Fire Protection segment |
| CNM | Core & Main | $10.81B | **11.76** | 1.41 | — | 2026-07-29 | Waterworks and fire-protection *distribution* |
| HLMA.L | Halma plc (GBP) | £18.25B | **30.46** | 7.57 | 37.30 | **2026-06-07** | UK safety and detection products group |

Every multiple in the panel is a data vendor's computation attributing its underlying data to S&P Global Market Intelligence, and no issuer's own Adjusted EBITDA definition has been reconciled to the vendor's denominator — which changes what the figures mean, and matters here because APG's 21.47x is the numerator of the arbitrage stated in §6.5.

Halma's page carries a 2026-06-07 data date, seven-plus weeks earlier than the US names, and is GBP-denominated without currency conversion; MSA's carries 2026-07-24. These are not one simultaneous observation and are not presented as one.

The panel does not resolve into a single population. EME at 15.52x and FIX at 27.74x are both mechanical and electrical contractors, 12.22 turns apart (V1). Three independently constructed versions of roughly the same listed-contractor idea span 14.8x to 27.74x: the vendor panel above, Houlihan Lokey's Industrial Services set at 17.2x on 2025A and 14.8x on 2026E (Houlihan Lokey, Q4 2025), and Chesapeake's Construction Services set at 17.8x mean and 16.0x median as of 2026-03-31 (Chesapeake Corporate Advisors, Q1 2026). That span is the honest width of what the listed comparables say. ADT at 7.62x is the lowest EV/EBITDA on the panel and the only name that is now a pure monitoring and RMR business in a listed wrapper. CTAS is the highest EV/Revenue name on the board at 7.96x, on a business where fire protection is a minority operating segment inside a uniform-rental company — it prices route density, not fire protection.

## 6.3 The transaction record

The record starts in 2021. No fire and life safety transaction announced in 2019 or 2020 carries a disclosed enterprise value or a disclosed multiple, and the earliest disclosed multiple anywhere in the record is APi/Chubb, announced 2021-07-26. Multiples struck before 2022-03 are marked `PRE-2023` because they belong to a different rate regime.

Most large transaction figures in this market reach print through trade press citing unnamed sources, with the parties declining to disclose terms; where that is the case below, the outlet is named and the row carries no derived multiple.

The $850M Convergint figure is the size of the vehicle, not the enterprise value of the company, and the two are not interchangeable.

Two conflicts in the disclosed record are carried rather than reconciled. Carrier's press release of 2024-12-02 gives net proceeds on the Commercial & Residential Fire sale of "approximately $2.2 billion" while its FY2024 10-K states "cash proceeds of $2.9 billion" — both stand, and neither document reconciles them. On the ADT Commercial sale, the headline price is "$1.6 billion", the seller's reconciliation table uses $1,613M, and net cash proceeds at close were $1,563M — all three stand, and the disclosed 11.2x is struck on $1,613M, because $1,600M against $144M yields 11.11x rather than 11.2x (V6).

That reconciliation carries the most transferable lesson in the record. The exhibit prints two multiples, and the pairing matters: **10.2x on $158M before the corporate-cost allocation, 11.2x on $144M after it.** The 11.2x is struck on $144M, after deducting $14M of estimated allocated corporate costs from the $158M of Commercial Adjusted EBITDA the same release reports. Building a comparable from the headline pair — $1.6B against $158M — books the transaction at 10.13x, roughly a full turn below what the seller disclosed (V6). **A carve-out EBITDA quoted before corporate-cost allocation is not the EBITDA the multiple was struck on**, and the bridge here appears only in a reconciliation table beneath the headline.

Twelve further control changes across the period carry no disclosed value at all: Sciens Building Solutions from Huron Capital to Carlyle (announced 2021-11-10, Huron retaining a minority stake); Summit Companies/SFP Holding from CI Capital to BlackRock Long Term Private Capital (2021-09-20); Protegis Fire & Safety from Align Capital to Summit Fire & Security (2022-05-02); Ares' partial Convergint stake to Leonard Green and Harvest Partners (2021-12-15); Guardian Fire Protection Services to Knox Lane (2024-01); Marmic Fire & Safety from HGGC to KKR (2024-07-24); Becklar from BV Investment Partners to Graham Partners (2024-12-10); FSS Technologies to Lightview Capital (2025-10-28); Guardian Fire Services from Northern Lakes Capital to Investcorp (2025-12-03); Security 101 from Gemspring to Morgan Stanley Capital Partners (2026-02-24); Minuteman Security and Life Safety from Prospect Partners to Tenex Capital Management; and VSC Fire & Security to Markel Corporation (announced 2019-11-08, "Additional terms were not disclosed"). Each was verified individually as undisclosed rather than assumed to be.

The bolt-on end of the record is disclosed only in aggregate. APi's investor materials give a programme series — 3 bolt-ons for $9M (2019), 7 for $42M (2020), 12 for $114M (2021), 1 for $5M (2022), 7 for $98M (2023), 12 for $250M (2024) — under the printed subheading "Weighted Average Adjusted EBITDA Multiple for Bolt-on Acquisitions <6x in Each Year", and excluding SKG in 2020, Chubb in 2022, Elevated in 2024 and non-material customer account purchases (APi Investor Update, 2025-02-27, p.12). The FY2025 10-K adds 14 businesses at $233M of total net consideration, all individually immaterial. The FY2024 filings resolve into 9 individually immaterial businesses at $77M plus three at $33M, $101M and $33M — 12 businesses matching the deck's count exactly, at $244M against the deck's $250M, a $6M difference that neither document reconciles (V7). Cintas discloses $232.9M and $186.8M of acquisition cash for fiscal 2025 and 2024 spanning three segments including Fire Protection, with no allocation and no deal count, and does not report Fire Protection revenue separately: it sits inside an "All Other" line with Uniform Direct Sales at $1,146.0M in FY2025, with FY2024 at $1,064.1M and FY2023 at $967.1M (Cintas FY2025 10-K).

The consequence is a finding in its own right. **No US public filer discloses a per-deal fire-services tuck-in purchase price against a target EBITDA.** APi comes closest, with aggregate consideration and a deal count but no target EBITDA and no revenue contributed.

## 6.4 Recurring-monthly-revenue multiples — the second language

RMR multiples and earnings multiples are different languages on different denominators, and the conversion between them is not available.

An EBITDA multiple prices the whole enterprise: install revenue, service revenue, monitoring revenue and the overhead supporting all three. An RMR multiple prices only the contracted monthly recurring monitoring stream, as a multiple of one month of it. It is a book-of-accounts price, used because alarm accounts have historically been bought and sold like receivables, independent of the business that installed them; install and service revenue are typically excluded and valued separately or at zero.

The one market series with a named methodology and a named presenter comes from Michael Barnes of Barnes Associates, presented in the closing keynote at the Electronic Security Expo and reported by Paul Rothman (Barnes Associates via SecurityInfoWatch, 2025-08-18):

| Seller size band | RMR multiple |
|---|---|
| Under **$50,000 RMR** | **36x** monthly revenue |
| Above **$500,000 RMR** | **46x** monthly revenue |

The transaction year these averages describe is given as 2024 in one account of the keynote and as a 2025 average in another; both stand, and the presentation date and reporting date are 2025 in either case. No 2026 update to these figures exists in the public record.

**These are market aggregates and are not attributable to any named transaction.** No named transaction in the consolidator record discloses an RMR multiple, and neither 36x nor 46x may be attached to any individual deal.

One disclosed transaction does produce an RMR multiple, and it is its own population. ADT's 8-K states a purchase price of "approximately $55,000,000 in cash" for "approximately 200,000 multifamily business customer units representing approximately $2.6 million of recurring monthly revenue" (ADT 8-K, 2025-09-15), which is ≈21.15x RMR at ≈$275 per unit and ≈$13.00 of monthly RMR per unit (V4). It must not be read against the 36x/46x bands. It is a bulk account and asset purchase rather than a company purchase — no branch infrastructure, no technicians and no backlog transferred — in residential multifamily, the lowest-RMR-per-unit segment, sold by a seller that had already sold its commercial arm to the buyer's sponsor two years earlier, and with a non-compete running only through 2028-10-02. It is a legitimate, fully disclosed RMR data point for bulk residential multifamily accounts and for nothing else.

The conversion problem is arithmetic, not judgement. Dividing an RMR multiple by 12 gives a multiple of annual recurring revenue: 46x becomes 3.83x of annual recurring revenue and 36x becomes 3.00x of it. Converting either to an EBITDA multiple requires an EBITDA margin on recurring revenue, and no source supplies one. The only margin figure in the record is a monitoring and services *gross* margin of 51.2%, reported down from 53.9% in 2017, with recurring gross margins having declined from 54% in 2015 and stabilised on 3–4% annual price increases with minimal attrition impact (Barnes Buchanan via SDM, 2026-02-16). Applied honestly, that margin converts 46x into ≈7.49x annualised monitoring gross profit and 36x into ≈5.86x (V2) — figures struck before any selling, branch, general, administrative or corporate cost. They are not EBITDA multiples, and the true EV/EBITDA equivalent is necessarily higher by an amount that depends entirely on how much incremental overhead the acquirer needs to carry the accounts.

The instruction that follows is unambiguous. **Do not convert an RMR multiple into an EBITDA multiple and present the result as a valuation.** Price the RMR book at an RMR multiple, price the install and service business at an EBITDA multiple, and add them. A single blended multiple quoted for an alarm business with meaningful install revenue conceals which side of the business the price is on.

## 6.5 The platform-versus-tuck-in spread

The roll-up thesis stated as a number requires a benchmark at the small end, and the only one that exists is industry-agnostic. GF Data's population is North American transactions of $10M–$500M total enterprise value, where the buyers are private equity firms, independent sponsors, family offices and mezzanine funds — 330-plus contributors submitting blind and confidentially — with **all industries pooled** (GF Data, Q3 2025, data through 2025-09-30).

**TEV/EBITDA by deal size, TEV in $M:**

| TEV band | 2023 | 2024 | YTD 2025 | All-period | N |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **$10–25M** | 5.9 | 6.4 | **6.4** | 5.9 | **2,084** |
| $25–50M | 6.9 | 6.8 | 6.8 | 6.7 | 1,671 |
| $50–100M | 8.1 | 8.1 | 8.3 | 7.7 | 1,156 |
| $100–250M | 9.5 | 8.5 | 10.3 | 8.6 | 654 |
| $250–500M | 10.2 | 9.8 | 8.5 | 9.7 | 104 |
| **Total** | 7.2 | 7.2 | **7.3** | 6.9 | **5,669** |

By target EBITDA size, the **$3–5M** band cleared at 6.7x in YTD 2025 on 1,503 all-period observations, and the **over-$10M** band at 8.3x on 1,238 of them. The edition consulted pools all industries into eight buckets, none of them fire or specialty trade, though GF Data separately publishes NAICS-level breakouts including one headed "Specialty Trade Contractors—NAICS Code: 238", which was not obtained; a fire ITM contractor would be classified by the contributor, most plausibly into Business Services, which cleared at 7.5x in YTD 2025 on 1,333 all-period observations.

Against that benchmark sits the only disclosed figure at the tuck-in end anywhere in US fire and life safety: APi's weighted average Adjusted EBITDA multiple for bolt-on acquisitions, disclosed in its investor materials as under 6x in each year from 2019 to 2024 and appearing in no SEC filing, on a base excluding SKG, Chubb, Elevated and customer-account purchases. APi's 2025 programme averaged $16.64M of consideration per deal across 14 businesses, which places it inside GF Data's $10–25M TEV band, and implies a minimum average acquired EBITDA per target of over $2.77M, just below the bottom of the $3–5M EBITDA band (V8).

**What that comparison licenses:** APi is acquiring its bolt-ons at least 0.4 to 0.7 turns below what the average private equity buyer paid for identically sized businesses in the same period, and the small end of that statement is measured against a 2,084-observation benchmark rather than an assertion. Because `<6x` is a ceiling on a weighted average and not a point, the true gap is wider than 0.4–0.7 turns rather than narrower.

**What it does not license.** GF Data's buyers are private equity sponsors while APi is a listed strategic, and GF Data pools all industries while APi buys fire and life safety, so this is a cross-population comparison and must always be labelled as one. It licenses no fire-specific size-tier range, because none exists (§6.7). And it does not license the widest spread in circulation: the "≈2.8–3.3x" figure is arithmetically identical to 17÷6 and 20÷6 (V9), which makes it a trade-press range for large-cap platforms divided by one company's disclosed ceiling on its own bolt-on programme. Because the denominator is a ceiling, both quotients are upper bounds — the correct notation is "under 2.83x to under 3.33x, cross-population, numerator unsourced" — and the numerator's provenance is set out in §6.6.

The decisive caveat is what happens when one methodology measures both ends. Inside GF Data's single dataset, method and period, the size premium from the $10–25M TEV band to the $100–250M band is 3.9 turns or 1.609x, and from the $10–25M band to the $50–100M band 1.9 turns or 1.297x; by target EBITDA, from $3–5M to over $10M it is 1.6 turns or 1.239x (V10). **When one methodology measures both ends, the size premium is ≈1.24x to 1.61x, not 1.88x to 3.33x.** The $250–500M row inverts in YTD 2025 on only 104 lifetime observations and should not be leaned on.

The listed-equity version of the same arithmetic is the mechanic in its starkest form: APG's own disclosed bolt-on ceiling of under 6x against its 21.47x vendor-computed trading multiple at 2026-07-28 is a spread of more than 15.47 turns, or under 3.58x (V11). Both ends are public — a company-disclosed entry threshold and a vendor computation on a listed security — and it bridges two populations, an entry multiple and a public equity multiple, which is why it is a bound rather than a measurement.

Two things qualify the thesis at its base. The first is durability. Average consideration per APi bolt-on moved from $3.0M (2019) to $9.5M (2021) to $20.8M (2024) and $16.64M (2025) (V8) while the disclosed cap never moved, so either materially larger EBITDA is being acquired per deal at a constant multiple, or the mix of very low-multiple deals that holds a weighted average under 6x is thinning. The second is competition. Pye-Barker reported 57 acquisitions in 2025 (Pye-Barker, 2026-03-17) against APi's 14, and a security-sector tracker counts 41 for the same company and period (Capstone, 2026-02-02) — both stand, and neither publication states a cut-off date or a counting rule that would account for the difference. Where two or more well-capitalised consolidators compete for the same sub-$5M-EBITDA sellers, erosion appears at the bottom of the spread first.

And the bottom of the spread cannot be tracked. **No verifiable time series of bolt-on entry multiples exists in US fire and life safety.** APi discloses a threshold, not a series: there is no disclosed 2019 multiple, no disclosed 2024 multiple, and no disclosed sequence of six annual figures, so any statement of the form "the programme paid 5.4x in 2023" is not sourced to the disclosure. Pye-Barker, Summit, Marmic, AI Fire, VSC, Firetrol and Fortis disclose no deal values at all. The durability of a number nobody can track is what the whole thesis rests on. Compounding it, the GF Data benchmark itself runs only through 2025-09-30, so movement during 2026 in the $10–25M band — the single band the tuck-in thesis depends on — is not observable.

## 6.6 A figure the industry repeats that nobody published

The range most often quoted for fire safety platforms has one origin, one date, and no transaction behind it. The chain is the finding.

**The origin.** *SDM Magazine*, article 103337, "Pye-Barker Could Be Eyeing $6B Market Listing This Year, Report Says", by Rodney Bosch, published 2024-07-15, relaying PE Hub: "The joint PE sponsors could receive bids valuing the company at 17x to 20x its $350 million EBITDA, resulting in a potential deal value exceeding $6 billion, according to PE Hub" (PE Hub via SDM, 2024-07-15). The same article records that a company spokesperson declined to comment on the report. The sourcing behind the figure is unnamed.

**What it actually described.** A prospective bid range for a single company, in the conditional — "could receive bids valuing" — reported before any process concluded, with a $350M EBITDA denominator itself attributed to PE Hub and unconfirmed by the company. The headline describes a market listing while the body describes bids from sponsors; both stand as printed. The three figures as printed are arithmetically consistent with one another, and the reported "exceeding $6 billion" corresponds to the bottom of the reported range rather than its middle (V12) — which establishes that they are one assertion rather than three independent observations.

**No transaction occurred.** Six months later, in January 2025, minority stakes went to ADIA and GIC, with Leonard Green investing significant new capital and Altas Partners retaining the majority of its ownership interest, and "Financial terms were not disclosed" (Pye-Barker, 2025-01-10). As of 2026-07-29 there is no announced sale and no announced listing. A predicted exit that resolved into an undisclosed-value minority recapitalisation is not evidence of a valuation.

**The restatement as a sector fact.** PE Hub, Michael Schoeck, 2025-04-15: "PE-backed fire safety platforms have ignited strong valuations over the past year, with sizable mid- to large-cap deals trading at 17x to 20x EBITDA multiples" (PE Hub, 2025-04-15). The visible text gives no sample size, no deal list and no denominator, and the article is paywalled beyond the lead.

**Two attributions that do not hold.** A 2026 valuation-aggregator page, last modified 2026-07-11, presents 17–20x for "scaled platforms" and attributes it to two named advisory publications. Neither publication contains the figure. The security-sector update credited with it reports "averaging a robust EV/EBITDA multiple of 11.8x and EV/Revenue multiple of 2.2x over the five-year period" (Capstone, 2026-02-02) — 5.2 turns below the bottom of the attributed range and 8.2 turns below its top. The fire alarm and life safety multiples note credited with it puts its highest tier, "Platform-ready (high RMR, low attrition, scalable ops)", at 7x to 10x on EBITDA (Breakwater M&A, 2026-02-01) — a ceiling 7 turns below the bottom of the attributed range. The range's only genuine ancestor is PE Hub, which that page lists among general sources without attaching to this number.

**The re-citation.** An operating, sponsor-backed fire and security consolidator's own resource page, dated 2026-07-15, states that "Scaled platforms with strong recurring revenue are transacting at 17x to 20x EBITDA", citing the aggregator page as its authority (Zeus Fire and Security, 2026-07-15). A reader encountering the range on an operator's site would reasonably treat it as sector corroboration of the PE Hub number. It is the same 2024 assertion arriving by a second route. The apparent multiplicity of sources for 17–20x — the 2024 report, the 2025 sector sentence, the aggregator page and the operator page — is **one unsourced assertion counted four times**. A "3x to 5x EBITDA" figure for project-heavy fire businesses travels the same route and has the same single ancestry.

The practical test follows from the shape of the chain rather than from any judgement about the publishers. An RMR range with an upper bound of 50x or 55x, or an EBITDA range for fire services quoted without a named population, should be traced to its origin before use; where the trail ends at a page whose methodology reference resolves back into its own domain, the figure has no independent existence.

## 6.7 What no bank publishes

**No investment bank or advisory house publishes fire-and-life-safety EBITDA multiples broken out by target size tier.** Thirteen publications from eight houses establish this: GF Data (two), Capstone Partners (three), Lincoln International, Houlihan Lokey (two), Meridian Capital (two), Chesapeake Corporate Advisors, Grant Thornton UK and Deloitte, read in full for a size-banded fire multiple. Not one contains one.

The one house with a sector-dedicated update is Meridian Capital, which publishes a *Fire & Life Safety Services M&A Market Update* twice a year. What it contains: 60-plus transactions from December 2024 to June 2025 with target, acquirer and business description (Meridian Capital, Summer 2025) and 50-plus more in the following edition (Meridian Capital, Winter 2025), a named investor universe including Pye-Barker, Summit, AI Fire, Encore, Agellus and RapidFire, PE-backed acquirer deal counts spanning 4 to 100-plus with the acquirer names withheld behind a contact line, and public strategics named — APi Group, Cintas, FirstService, EMCOR — with counts of 20 to 55-plus. What it does not contain: **a value on any of the 133 transactions listed across the two editions, and zero multiples of any kind.** Its deal-tempo figure is edition-specific and the two editions differ: an average of 38 transactions per quarter since 2020 (Winter 2025), against nearly 50 per quarter since the beginning of 2024 (Summer 2025).

The nearest substitutes each measure something else. Capstone publishes 11.8x EV/EBITDA and 2.2x EV/Revenue as a five-year average across a Security Solutions universe whose segments include Fire & Life Safety alongside Physical Security, Uniformed Guard, Access Control, Metal Detection, Tactical Products and School Safety, without breaking Fire & Life Safety out, and without stating whether the average covers all deals or only those with disclosed multiples — which materially affects its meaning, since a disclosed-only average leans toward larger, better-advised transactions (Capstone, 2026-02-02). Capstone's buyer-type split for Q3 2025 — private equity sponsors 12.0x, private strategics 9.8x, public strategics 8.6x — is US middle market across all industries (Capstone, 2025-12-17). Houlihan Lokey's Engineering, Industrial and Infrastructure Services updates are public-company trading comparables across a 37-company set with no fire or life safety mention anywhere in either edition. Chesapeake's 31-company AEC set mentions fire protection only inside target descriptions, not as a valuation tier. Grant Thornton's UK review publishes no multiples at all, and reports a different market: 184 UK deals in 2025, fire and security at 23% of them, and 70% of fire and security deals involving PE-backed corporates in 2025 against 57% in 2024. Deloitte's 2026 engineering and construction outlook contains no M&A multiples and no fire or life safety mention. GF Data's size-band table is the closest available substitute and is industry-agnostic (§6.5).

The retrieval surface compounds the gap. Across seven distinct queries for fire, life-safety and mid-market multiples, the organic result sets were dominated by unsourced valuation-aggregator pages, with a single domain returning in every query and frequently occupying four to seven of the top eight results; the genuine bank publications were reachable only by naming the house. Any search conducted by topic rather than by publisher retrieves almost exclusively unsourced material.

**The consequence for underwriting is direct.** The tuck-in end of the spread has no observed transaction anywhere in US fire and life safety. It has a benchmark that is not fire-specific (GF Data), a threshold that is one company's ceiling on its own programme (APi's `<6x`), and a survey of expectations rather than transactions (Capstone's 6.8x typical and 9.8x premium for 2026). Underwriting a fire-services tuck-in against a "fire multiple" means underwriting against one of those three, and the deal file should say which.

---

---

# PART VII — OPERATING ECONOMICS


## 7.1 EBITDA margin by scale tier

Three tiers exist in the evidence, they measure different things, and blending them is the most common error in a fire and life safety underwriting model. They are separated here and not reconciled, because they do not reconcile.

**Tier 1 — consolidated public roll-up.** Whole-company figures for diversified filers, carrying centralised procurement, captive functions and no owner compensation add-back.

| Company | Measure | FY2025 | FY2024 |
|---|---|---|---|
| APi Group | adjusted EBITDA / margin | **$1,041M / 13.2%** | **$893M / 12.7%** |
| APi Group | adjusted free cash flow conversion | **80%** of adjusted EBITDA | — |
| Cintas | Uniform Rental operating margin | **23.49%** (E4) | — |
| Cintas | First Aid & Safety operating margin | **24.20%** (E4) | — |

**Tier 2 — platform, segment and branch level.** This is the tier that actually informs what a scaled fire platform earns.

| Segment | Revenue | Earnings | Margin |
|---|---|---|---|
| APi Safety Services, FY2025 | **$5,456M** | **$916M** segment earnings | **16.79%** (E3); company states 16.8%, up 90 bps |
| APi Safety Services, FY2024 | **$4,797M** | **$765M** | **15.95%** (E3); company states 15.9% |
| APi Specialty Services, FY2025 | **$2,460M** | **$264M** | **10.7%**, down 70 bps |
| Cintas "All Other" (Fire Protection Services plus Uniform Direct Sale), FY2025 | **$1,146.0M** | gross profit **$542.4M**, operating income **$191.6M** | gross **47.33%**, operating **16.72%** (E4) |
| Cintas "All Other", FY2024 | **$1,064.1M** | operating income **$170.0M** | operating **15.97%** (E4) |
| Cintas "All Other", FY2023 | **$967.1M** | operating income **$143.2M** | operating **14.81%** (E4) |

Two scaled operators put a branch- or route-based fire and safety business in the mid-teens at the segment line in FY2025. These are different measures — segment earnings against segment operating income — on very different populations: a global fire, security and elevator segment of $5,456M, against a US route business of $1,146.0M of which fire protection is 71.33% (E4). Their near-identity is coincidence rather than corroboration. What it does mean is that a buyer arguing a scaled US fire platform clears the mid-teens at the segment line has two named public reference points rather than none. Branch-level disclosure is more useful still, and it is the only public statement of what a single operating unit earns. North American branch **median EBITDA margin ran ≈17% in 2024 against ≈13% in 2021**, with branches below a 10% margin falling from **34** to **11**; internationally, median branch EBITDA was **11.5%** in 2024, with loss-making branches falling from **55** in 2021 to fewer than **5** in 2024 (APi Group investor day, slides 21 and 32, 2025-05-21). Sub-scale branches exist in quantity inside a well-run platform, and they are fixable without closing them.

**Tier 3 — the small independent, which is the acquisition target population.** Establishments of the size a corp-dev practice acquires dominate the trade: **74.16%** of entire-year establishments in NAICS 238220 have fewer than ten employees and **1.58%** have a hundred or more (2022 Economic Census). This is the tier where the data runs out.

**No survey-based or association-based EBITDA benchmark for small US fire protection contractors exists in the public record.** The trade associations covering the sprinkler, alarm and equipment-distribution trades publish none on a retrievable basis, and neither does the construction financial-management body whose benchmarking product would ordinarily carry it. **No EBITDA margin is stated here for the small independent, because none exists that would survive contact with a diligence file.** That absence is itself the most decision-relevant fact on this page: a buyer entering this market has no external benchmark against which to test a seller's margin claim, and must build one from primary target data across several acquisitions. The one published reference point aimed at the private end of the market is market-level and sell-side: "Margins in excess of 20% [EBITDA] indicative of strong labor and operational efficiency" (Meridian Capital, Winter 2025). It is a threshold characterisation rather than a distribution, and it is not a benchmark.

What can be substituted is a government-sourced **cost-structure ceiling** for the container trade that fire sprinkler contracting is classified inside — NAICS 238220, Plumbing, Heating and Air-Conditioning Contractors, **114,427 US employer establishments**, within which fire sprinkler work is a small minority of receipts.

| 2022 Economic Census, NAICS 238220, US employer establishments | $ thousand | % of total receipts |
|---|---|---|
| Total receipts | 297,608,835 | 100.00% |
| Annual payroll | 80,351,785 | **27.00%** |
| Cost of materials, parts, supplies | 93,671,679 | **31.47%** |
| Payments to subcontractors | 21,205,813 | **7.13%** |
| Residual before all non-payroll overhead, rent, insurance, fleet running cost, bad debt, depreciation and owner distributions | 102,379,558 | **34.40%** |
| Value added less annual payroll | 95,566,027 | **32.11%** |

Ratios at E18. **Neither the 34.40% nor the 32.11% is EBITDA, and neither may be quoted as EBITDA under any circumstances.** Both exclude every non-payroll operating cost a contractor actually bears: facilities rent, general liability and auto insurance, fuel and fleet maintenance, software, licensing and bond costs, sales and marketing, professional fees and bad debt. They are a hard upper bound, and true small-contractor EBITDA sits materially below them. The only legitimate use is falsification — a margin claim above this band for a sprinkler contractor asserts something the cost structure of the entire containing trade cannot support. One further Census fact bears directly on the synergy model. Payroll per head runs **$66,225** for construction workers and **$75,164** for other employees (E18) — the office and supervisory layer is the more expensive layer per head. Back-office consolidation is therefore the highest-value-per-head synergy in a roll-up model, which is why the per-entity licensing rules in §7.4 are economically material rather than administrative.

**The mid-teens segment margin must not travel down-market.** It is a segment figure inside a multi-billion-dollar filer with centralised procurement, a captive insurance function and no owner compensation add-back. It is not what a single-branch sprinkler contractor earns, and using it as an exit-margin assumption for a small independent embeds a synergy that has not been named, costed or timed.

## 7.2 Inspection-and-service versus install mix, and the margin differential

**Disclosed mix, across the four sub-verticals a buyer will encounter.**

| Company | Measure | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| APi Group | inspection, service and monitoring share of net revenues | **54%** in 2025, from **40%** in 2021 | Q4 2025 earnings call, 2026-02-25 |
| APi Group | stated long-term objective | **60%+** | investor day, 2025-05-21 |
| Johnson Controls | "products and systems accounted for approximately 68% of sales from continuing operations and services accounted for 32% of sales from continuing operations" | **68 / 32** | FY2025 10-K, filed 2025-11-14 |
| ADT Inc. | monitoring and related services share of revenue | **84.9%** (E9) | FY2025 10-K |
| Cintas | Fire Protection Services share of total revenue | **7.9%** | FY2025 10-K |

**Two independent constructions of the install-versus-service margin differential exist. Both are carried, because they differ by an order of magnitude and the choice between them changes what a mix shift is worth.**

*Construction one — a filer's own income statement.* Johnson Controls splits net sales and cost of sales between products and systems and services on the face of its income statement, which makes it the cleanest disclosed differential available anywhere in the fire, security and building-systems universe. Population: consolidated, global, HVAC plus fire plus security plus controls — not a pure fire contractor.

| Fiscal year | Products and systems: sales / cost / gross margin | Services: sales / cost / gross margin | Differential | Service share of sales |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FY2025 | $16,124M / $10,543M / **34.61%** | $7,472M / $4,461M / **40.30%** | **+568 bps** | 31.67% |
| FY2024 | $15,967M / $10,677M / **33.13%** | $6,985M / $4,198M / **39.90%** | **+677 bps** | 30.4% |
| FY2023 | $15,789M / $10,736M / **32.00%** | $6,542M / $3,791M / **42.05%** | **+1,005 bps** | 29.3% |

Arithmetic at E1. Note the direction of travel: the differential compressed from 1,005 to 568 basis points in two years, because product and system gross margin rose 261 basis points while service gross margin fell 175 basis points. A buyer paying for service mix should establish which of those two differentials is embedded in the model. **The FY2025 568 basis points is the current-cycle figure; the 1,005 basis points is a cyclical peak reached when product margins were depressed, and it is not a structural constant.**

*Construction two — fire-specific work-type bands.* The Meridian bands of §3.4 imply a differential between testing and inspection at a 50%-plus floor and new construction at 25% to 35% of **15 to 25 percentage points** at the gross line (E2) — several times the products-versus-services differential above.

The two constructions are not reconcilable and should not be averaged. They differ because "services" in a global product company's income statement is a mixed category spanning warranty, controls, maintenance and parts across HVAC and security, while the Meridian bands are fire-specific and work-type-specific. **The narrow construction is the conservative one and the fire-specific construction is the relevant one; a model that applies the fire-specific band should say so, and a model that applies 568 basis points should not describe the result as a fire mix-shift benefit.** Directional corroboration inside a single filer, with its caveat repeated: adjusted gross margin of **37.3%** at the service-weighted segment against **18.9%** at the project-weighted segment, a 1,840 basis point spread (E3), is a difference between two businesses and not a work-type differential.

**Why install is structurally different, in the same filer's own disclosure.** Typical contract period "less than 6 months", maximum five years; payment terms of minimum 7 days, standard 30 days, maximum 90 days; remaining performance obligations of **$3,605M** at 2025-12-31 of which **74%** is expected to be recognised within twelve months (APi Group FY2025 10-K). That last figure equals 45.57% of a year's revenue (E7) — a project backlog, not an annuity, and it should never be presented as contracted recurring revenue.

## 7.3 Labour as the binding constraint

**The certification ladder is the moat and the growth constraint in one object.** NICET runs six fire protection and building systems programmes: Fire Alarm Systems; Inspection and Testing of Fire Alarm Systems; Water-Based Systems Layout; Inspection and Testing of Water-Based Systems; Special Hazards Systems; and In-Building Public Safety Communications.

The inspection certification for water-based systems, which is the credential the inspection annuity actually runs on:

| Level | Minimum work experience | Role, verbatim | Exam |
|---|---|---|---|
| I | "A minimum of 6 months of experience with … inspection, testing, repair and maintenance activities" | "technicians who perform limited job tasks under direct supervision" | 115 questions / 125 min |
| II | "A minimum of 2 years of work experience in the inspection and periodic testing of water-based fire protection systems", at least 1 year direct | "technicians who perform routine inspection and testing tasks under limited supervision" | 168 questions / 185 min |
| III | "A minimum of 5 years of work experience in the inspection and periodic testing of existing water-based fire protection systems", at least 3 years direct | "technicians who can work independently to perform complex system inspection and testing jobs" | 145 questions / 155 min |

Source: NICET Inspection and Testing of Water-Based Systems candidate handbook. Scope covers "applicable codes and standards, primarily NFPA 25."

The fire alarm ladder runs four levels on the same principle: a minimum of 6 months at Level I; 2 years including at least 12 months of fire alarm systems experience at Level II; 5 years including at least 45 months of fire alarm experience at Level III; and 10 years including at least 105 months at Level IV, with exams running from 85 questions in 110 minutes to 120 questions in 290 minutes (NICET Fire Alarm Systems candidate handbook). The layout certification splits its Level III into a general plan preparation exam of 70 questions in 175 minutes and a hydraulics exam of 60 questions in 240 minutes. The fire alarm inspection certification runs two levels and "serves as evidence of qualification for Authority Having Jurisdiction (AHJ) acceptance." Three cross-cutting requirements convert the experience gates into a hard constraint. Recertification falls due **every three years and requires 90 continuing-professional-development points per certification, drawn from at least two categories**. Work history must be verified by a supervisor "in a position and ha[ving] the authority to directly supervise, inspect, and/or approve" the applicant's work, and verifiers "must be technically competent and cannot be peers or subordinates". Levels III and IV additionally require personal recommendations from licensed professional engineers, NICET Level IV technicians, registered surveyors, architects, fire marshals or code officials, and recommenders cannot be current or former verifiers.

**A Level III water-based inspection technician cannot be created in under five years, three of which must be direct inspection and testing work.** That capability cannot be bought with training spend, cannot be compressed, and cannot be hired away from a competitor without the competitor noticing. The binding constraint on how fast an inspection platform grows organically is the number of Level II technicians it started the five-year clock with. The three-year, 90-point recertification cycle runs the other way in a cost-cut scenario: a platform that stops funding continuing development watches its certified base decay on a known schedule, which makes training spend a diligence line item rather than a discretionary one.

Certification-holder counts are not published — nationally, by state or by discipline. The only named certified-headcount disclosure available is **42 NICET-certified and 4 NAFED-certified associates across 15 branches** at ORR Protection, or 2.80 NICET-certified individuals per branch (E22). At a national platform servicing more than 8,000 sites a year, that is a usable order-of-magnitude prior for how thin the certified layer is even at scale.

**The organised sprinkler-fitter segment has an unusual structure with direct commercial consequences.**

| Fact | Value |
|---|---|
| Organisation | "UA Local Union 669 Road Sprinkler Fitters, AFL-CIO" |
| Jurisdiction | nationwide — a single national local with jurisdiction over sprinkler fitting across the United States |
| Membership, union's own statement | "Over 17,000 members", of which "more than 13,000 active journeypeople" and "over 4,000 apprentices" |
| Membership, Department of Labor LM filing | **16,631 members**, 2025 filing |
| Apprentice share | **30.77%** of journeypeople; **23.53%** of total membership (E17) |

The two membership figures disagree and both are carried: the union states over 17,000 and the LM filing shows 16,631, a gap of about 2.2% that changes no conclusion. **No midpoint is taken.** Because jurisdiction is national rather than metro-by-metro, a union sprinkler contractor's labour cost is set by a national agreement structure and manpower can move across state lines without renegotiating jurisdiction. An open-shop platform cannot access that pool. The union and non-union choice therefore changes cost structure and manpower mobility simultaneously, and the two effects run in opposite directions — which is why a union-shop target and an open-shop target are not comparable on labour cost alone. The open-shop training counterpart is the American Fire Sprinkler Association — "more than 1,000 member companies" — which runs a four-level fire sprinkler fitter apprentice training programme alongside designer, foremanship, residential installation, NFPA 25 and ASSE 15010 courses. **Neither the union nor the open-shop apprenticeship programme publishes its length or hour requirement**, so apprenticeship duration is not a modellable input on either side of the shop question.

Union density in adjacent filers brackets what a target's labour posture might look like: approximately 18,000 of 87,000 employees covered by collective bargaining agreements or works councils at Johnson Controls, or 20.69% (E25); approximately 900 of 48,300 at Cintas, or 1.86% (E25), which is what a pure open-shop route business looks like. APi discloses only that "a large portion" of its workforce is covered, with no percentage.

**Wage levels and wage inflation, Security and Fire Alarm Systems Installers (SOC 49-2098).** The occupation title fuses security with fire and is not a fire-only headcount.

| Survey vintage | Employment | Mean hourly | Mean annual | Median annual | 10th pct | 90th pct |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| May 2019 | 71,600 | $24.14 | $50,210 | **$48,970** | $31,180 | $73,920 |
| May 2021 | 77,420 | $25.08 | $52,170 | **$48,320** | $30,490 | $76,240 |
| May 2022 | 80,720 | $26.10 | $54,280 | **$50,130** | $34,840 | $77,980 |
| May 2023 | 83,540 | $27.59 | $57,400 | **$56,430** | $36,790 | $79,650 |
| 2025 wage data | 85,900 (2024) | $28.88 | — | **$60,070** | — | — |

Source: BLS Occupational Employment and Wage Statistics year archives, May 2019 through May 2023; 2025 wage data via O\*NET's restatement of BLS. Median annual pay rose **24.32%** between May 2021 and the 2025 vintage, a compound annual rate of **5.59%** (E15), with the sharpest single step a **12.57%** move between May 2022 and May 2023 and deceleration to **6.45%** across the final two years. Employment rose **16.68%** between May 2019 and May 2023 — the occupation grew and grew more expensive at the same time. Projected growth for 2024 to 2034 is "Much faster than average (7% or higher)", with 9,400 projected annual openings.

**For sprinkler fitters, no wage series exists, and substituting the containing occupation would be wrong.** Sprinkler fitters are classified inside SOC 47-2152, Plumbers, Pipefitters, and Steamfitters, which explicitly "Includes sprinkler fitters" and lists "Fire Sprinkler Service Technician" and "Sprinkler Fitter" among its reported job titles. That container reported 417,620 employed at a median of **$59,880** in May 2021, 436,160 at **$61,550** in May 2023, and 504,500 (2024 employment) at a median of **$63,800** in the 2025 vintage — median growth of **6.55%** across the period, a compound rate of **1.60%** (E16). Over the identical window the alarm-installer occupation inflated at 5.59% and the pipefitter container at 1.60%, a divergence of 399 basis points a year. Two readings are available. Either the low-voltage life-safety labour pool is genuinely tighter than the mechanical trades pool, or a 504,500-person container in which sprinkler fitters are a small minority has its aggregate wage set by residential plumbing and is not measuring sprinkler-fitter wages at all. The second is the more likely, which produces a clean and inconvenient conclusion: **there is no reliable published wage-inflation series for sprinkler fitters, and the 47-2152 series should not be used as a proxy for one.** The 49-2098 series is usable directly for the alarm and detection sub-vertical, where the occupation matches the work.

## 7.4 Licensing as a moat, and as an integration constraint

Most states license the **firm** through a **named individual** — a responsible managing employee or qualifying agent. That single design choice is why licensing is a genuine barrier to entry, and it is also why it becomes the most underestimated integration constraint in a multi-state roll-up.

**Florida is the clearest worked example of both halves.** Under Fla. Stat. § 633.328 (2024): "At least one member or supervising employee of the business organization as designated to the State Fire Marshal by such organization shall be certified under this chapter in order for the business organization to hold a current certificate as a contractor." Two further clauses do the damage. "A certified individual who is the sole contractor on behalf of a business organization may not affiliate simultaneously with another business organization." And if the sole certified individual departs, the organisation has "a grace period of 60 days from the date of termination in which to certify another person under the provisions of this chapter, failing which the certification of the business organization shall expire without further operation of law." The experience gates behind the qualifying certificate are substantial in their own right (Fla. Stat. § 633.318, 2024). Contractor I and II require "4 years' proven experience in the employment of a fire protection system Contractor I or a combination of equivalent education and experience in water-based and chemical fire suppression systems". Contractor III requires "4 years of verifiable employment experience … in chemical fire suppression systems". Contractor IV must be "licensed as a certified plumbing contractor under chapter 489" and must "successfully complete a training program acceptable to the State Fire Marshal of not less than 40 contact hours regarding the applicable installation standard used by the Contractor IV as described in NFPA 13D". Contractor V must have been "licensed as a certified underground utility and excavation contractor or certified plumbing contractor".

Three other states show the same architecture with different mechanics. Iowa requires a contractor licence plus a technician licence plus a technician-trainee licence with endorsements by system type, and the "Contractor must name a responsible managing employee (RME) with a current qualifying certification" (Iowa Code ch. 100C and 100D; 481 IAC ch. 265 and 266). Washington runs four contractor levels under RCW 18.160 and WAC 212-80, and **its Level U requires NICET Level III** — a direct statutory link from a state licence to a certification level, which converts the five-year clock of §7.3 into a hard cap on market entry. California requires a Fire Sprinkler Fitter Certification for C-16 qualifiers and journey-level personnel installing or repairing water-based systems for commercial or multi-family dwellings, with a registration for apprentices and one- and two-family dwellings excluded (CSLB Industry Bulletin 24-02, issued 2024-05-10).

**Where this binds on a roll-up, and it is the second half that buyers underestimate.**

1. **The qualifier cannot be released.** Florida's 60-day cliff hands the qualifying individual of an acquired Florida entity a licence-extinction option over that entity for two months after any termination. A retention agreement for the qualifier is not a courtesy; it is a condition of the licence surviving the close.
2. **Legal entities cannot always be collapsed.** Because a Florida qualifier acting as sole contractor "may not affiliate simultaneously with another business organization", an acquirer running several Florida-licensed entities needs a distinct qualified individual for each. Entity consolidation undertaken to save overhead can require *more* certified people, not fewer.
3. **The synergy that licensing destroys is the most valuable one.** Office and supervisory payroll runs at $75,164 per head against $66,225 for construction workers (E18), so the back office is the more expensive layer per head. A licensing regime that forces a qualified individual to remain attached to each branch or each entity removes the highest-value-per-head synergy in the model — and it does so quietly, because the licence register is usually built after the synergy model rather than before it.
4. **Build the licence register before the synergy model.** For every acquired entity in every state of operation: which licence class, which named individual qualifies it, whether that individual is an owner and therefore leaving at close, what the statutory grace period is, and whether the state permits one individual to qualify multiple entities.

Licensing questions are legal questions. Engage state-licensing counsel in each state of operation before signing, and ask specifically: does the licence survive the contemplated transaction structure, what is the notification obligation and its deadline, and what is the shortest statutory grace period across the target's footprint.

## 7.5 Working capital, retainage, capex, fleet and systems

This is the strongest disclosed block in the sector, and retainage and contract balances are the part a first-time buyer of a contracting business gets wrong.

**Receivables, retainage and contract balances.** Population: APi Group consolidated, $7,911M of revenue, install-and-service mix, global (APi Group FY2025 10-K).

| Item | 2025-12-31 | 2024-12-31 | 2023-12-31 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Accounts receivable, net of allowances | **$1,563M** | $1,444M | $1,395M |
| Contract assets | **$484M** | $453M | $436M |
| Contract liabilities | **$694M** | $590M | $526M |
| Retentions receivable | **$187M** | $160M | — |
| Of which may not be collected within one year | **$48M** | — | — |

| Derived (E5, E6) | Value |
|---|---|
| Days sales outstanding on receivables alone | **72.11 days** |
| Days sales outstanding including contract assets | **94.45 days** |
| Contract liabilities as share of revenue | **8.77%** |
| Net contract working capital (receivables plus contract assets less contract liabilities) | **$1,353M**, or **17.10%** of revenue |
| Retentions as share of revenue / of receivables | **2.36%** / **11.96%** |
| Share of retentions falling beyond one year | **25.67%** |

Disclosed payment terms run to a minimum of 7 days, a standard of 30 days and a maximum of 90 days. A fire protection contracting platform should be modelled carrying roughly 72 days of receivables and roughly 94 days once contract assets are included, with about 17% of revenue permanently tied up in contract working capital. Retainage is the install-side tax in its purest disclosed form: $187M at year end with a quarter of it collectible beyond twelve months. An inspection-only book carries materially less of both, which is a second and independent reason — beyond the margin bands of §3.4 — that inspection mix drives value.

Two traps sit in this block. First, contract liabilities exceeding contract assets, $694M against $484M, means this filer is billing ahead of cost in aggregate — a favourable position that a smaller install-heavy contractor without the same customer mix will not replicate, and one that must not be assumed into a target's model. Second, a billing-cycle-time metric is not a days-sales-outstanding metric. The vendor benchmark showing top performers invoicing within 1.6 days, with a second view of the same report giving 1.7 days for top performers against 5.7 and 11.1 days for middle and bottom cohorts (ServiceTrade, 2024), measures how fast an invoice leaves the building. It says nothing about when cash arrives, and it cannot be substituted for the 72-day figure. The same report's whole-population finding is that commercial contractors are "billing 20% slower" year over year. Revenue recognition on contracting balances is specialist work. Engage a construction-accounting specialist on the quality-of-earnings scope and ask for over- and under-billings by job, a retainage ageing, and a walk from contract assets to invoiced revenue for the three largest install jobs.

**Capex and fleet — a low-capex, high-working-capital business.**

| Item | Value |
|---|---|
| Operating cash flow / capital expenditure, FY2025 | **$759M / $96M** |
| Capex as share of revenue | **1.21%** (E8) |
| Capex per team member | **$3,310** (E8) |
| Property, plant and equipment: gross / accumulated depreciation / net | **$738M / $341M / $397M** |
| "Autos and trucks", gross cost | **$138M** (2024: $113M) |
| Autos and trucks gross cost as share of revenue | **1.74%** (E8) |
| Finance-lease property, plant and equipment, net | **$8M** (2024: $11M) |
| Weighted-average remaining lease term / discount rate, operating | **4 years 6 months / 5.60%** |
| Weighted-average remaining lease term / discount rate, finance | **2 years 10.8 months / 4.70%** |
| Lease terms by asset class | buildings 1–12 years; equipment 1–7 years; autos and trucks 1–7 years |

Source: APi Group FY2025 10-K. Gross truck cost per team member computes to $4,759 (E8), and **that figure should not be read as truck capex per technician** for three disclosed reasons: the denominator is total team members rather than technicians, and technicians are a minority of headcount in every filer that splits it; it is gross historical cost rather than replacement cost; and autos and trucks are leased on 1–7 year operating leases, so an unknown share of the fleet never appears in property, plant and equipment at all. The defensible statement is that the owned truck fleet carries a gross book cost equal to 1.74% of annual revenue while total capital expenditure runs at 1.21% of revenue. **The balance-sheet burden in this sector is receivables and retainage, not trucks.**

**Systems.** The inspection software stack is the one input in the sector with a fully published price list, and the pricing model is more informative than the prices.

| Vendor | Published pricing |
|---|---|
| ServiceTrade | **$59** Select / **$99** Premium / **$159** Enterprise, each "per month • per technician • billed annually", with "Unlimited office users" on all tiers. Select requires a minimum of 5 technicians; Premium is recommended for 10–20 technicians; Enterprise for 20 or more. Premium adds recurring contract invoices, geographical regions, multiple brands, an integration library, business analytics and 24/7 emergency support; Enterprise adds the certification programme as standard. |
| BuildOps | No published price. Priced on "the number of field and office users on your account", with implementation and onboarding support included, and no separate licence required for subcontractors. |
| Inspect Point | No published price. The only disclosed threshold is "Have 30 or more technicians? Contact us today to learn more about our customized enterprise plan." |

Annualised, the tiers run **$708**, **$1,188** and **$1,908** per technician per year (E24), and the top tier equals **0.76%** of revenue against a $250,000 revenue-per-technician assumption (E24, assumption named there). **The finding is the charging basis — per technician, with office users free.** Software cost therefore scales with exactly the headcount that certification and licensing constrain, and does not scale with the back office a roll-up is trying to consolidate — so the systems line in an integration model behaves in the opposite direction from the overhead line.

## 7.6 What is not measurable, and what to do about it

Four inputs a buyer wants most do not exist in the public record. For each, the absence is stated as a finding, and the useful distinction is whether the gap closes with more research or only with access to a target's own data.

**Revenue per technician for fire contracting — a research gap at the platform level, a diligence question at the target level.** No fire protection contracting platform discloses a technician count. Reported headcounts are totals only: approximately 29,000 team members, more than 9,000 team members, approximately 48,300 employee-partners and approximately 87,000 employees at the four large filers. Every revenue-per-employee figure computable from those is therefore a total-headcount figure and is not revenue per technician. The single filer that splits its payroll is in the alarm and monitoring sub-vertical: "we employed approximately 12,200 people, including approximately 1,600 direct field solution advisors; 3,100 installation and service technicians; 4,000 customer care professionals; and 700 phone sales representatives" (ADT Inc. FY2025 10-K), giving a technician share of **25.41%** and a field-facing share of **38.52%** (E9). The only defensible per-technician figure anywhere in the sector follows from it: installation, product and other revenue of $774,520 thousand over 3,100 installation and service technicians, or **$249,845** per technician (E9). It is not transferable to fire contracting, and it is not a monitoring productivity measure either, because 84.9% of that filer's revenue is produced by a central station and a billing system rather than by a truck. At the target level this closes immediately: request the payroll register by role and the certification register, and compute the figure directly.

**Route density in every form — a diligence question, and framing it that way is more useful than an estimate.** No named, credible, fire-specific source publishes stops per technician per day, drive time as a share of the workday, minimum viable metro density, or revenue per fire inspection route. What exists is a set of whole-company or whole-platform proxies, each labelled by what it actually measures:

| Proxy | Value | What it measures |
|---|---|---|
| Cintas local delivery routes, 2025-05-31 | **12,100** (11,700 a year earlier, **+3.42%**) | company-wide, all segments, dominated by uniform rental |
| Cintas revenue per route | **$808,678** on route-serviced revenue only (E19) | company-wide; not a fire protection route figure |
| Cintas customers per route | **≈83** (E19) | company-wide; "over one million businesses", no customer above 1% of revenue |
| Cintas facilities | **478** operational facilities plus **12** distribution centres; **490** facilities in **341** cities | company-wide |
| Everon customer locations per technician | **130.4** (E20) | 300,000-plus locations, 2,300 technicians; includes monitoring-only sites needing no visit, so an upper bound |
| Everon locations and technicians per branch | **3,000** and **23** (E20) | more than 100 branches |
| APi North America team members per branch | **84.7** (E21) | ≈10,500 team members, 124 branches; team members, not technicians |
| ORR Protection sites per branch | **533** (E22) | more than 8,000 sites, 15 branches; special-hazard weighted, low-frequency, engineer-heavy |
| Pye-Barker team members per location, locations per state | **36.0** and **5.32** (E23) | 9,000-plus team members, 250-plus locations, 47 states; both inputs are "plus" figures |

The Cintas revenue-per-route figure uses route-serviced revenue as its numerator, not all revenue, and it is stated on that basis only. The closest thing in the public record to a minimum-viable-density statistic is not a density figure at all but the branch-margin distribution of §7.1: a platform ran 34 of 124 North American branches below a 10% EBITDA margin in 2021 and cut that to 11 by 2024 without proportionally cutting branch count. Sub-scale branches exist in quantity and are fixable, but no source names the density threshold at which a branch becomes viable. Route-planning vendor material circulating in this space — stops per day before and after optimisation, drive time as a share of the day, minutes per location — rests on an uncited attribution and an unverifiable case study, and is not carried here as a figure. **Route density must be measured in diligence from the target's own dispatch and work-order data. It is not obtainable from the public web, and an estimate would be worse than an acknowledged gap because it would be used.** One research route remains genuinely open on route economics: Item 19 financial performance representations for the hood-cleaning franchise systems, which the state franchise registries in California, Minnesota, Wisconsin and New York publish free of charge. Aggregator restatements of those figures circulate without a sample size, a filing year or an Item 19 attribution and do not meet the evidentiary bar; the registry filings themselves would.

**Fully-loaded technician cost — a diligence question.** No source discloses a burden multiplier or a billable-utilisation rate for fire protection technicians, and the two together are what convert a wage into a cost. The disclosed inputs bracket the wage line only: a median of **$60,070** for the alarm-installer occupation, and Census construction-worker payroll of **$66,225** per first-quarter construction worker in the containing trade (E18, with the annual-numerator caveat named there). **No fully-loaded cost figure is stated here, because producing one requires two assumptions that would then be mistaken for evidence.** In diligence the inputs are available directly: payroll register, burden schedule, and billed hours against paid hours by technician for a full year.

**Certification-holder counts — partly a research gap, wholly answerable in diligence.** NICET publishes no count nationally, by state or by discipline, and the only named certified-headcount datum in the sector is one platform's self-reported 42 NICET-certified and 4 NAFED-certified associates across 15 branches. The consequence for market sizing is that the size of the certified labour pool — the true constraint on how fast the sector can grow — is unmeasured. At a single target it is measurable on day one: the certification register with levels, disciplines, expiry dates and continuing-development status, cross-referenced against the state licence register of §7.4 and against which individuals are selling shares.

**Two further absences belong on this page because they change what a buyer should fund.** The composition of an inspection book by contract form (§3.2) is the single most commercially decisive unquantified variable in the industry and it is purely a diligence question — the answer sits in the target's contract file. And whether the pull-through ratio of §3.1 holds outside the one issuer that publishes it is a research question with no answer yet: it is one management estimate with no published methodology, corroborated in direction by vendor platform data that measures a narrower quantity, and never independently replicated. A model that treats $3–$4 as a sector constant is extending a single company's disclosure across a fragmented industry of 114,427 establishments in the containing trade, and should carry that as a named assumption rather than as a fact. One question remains open that no available source can close: whether a building that empties keeps getting inspected. The nearest measure of inspection deferral is a single city's 63% currency rate, which is a cross-section rather than a time series and therefore cannot separate structural non-compliance from cyclical deferral. For a buyer underwriting an inspection annuity through a downturn in commercial occupancy, that is a live and unresolved exposure, and the only way to test it at a target is to trace three consecutive inspection cycles at sites whose tenancy changed.

---

---

# PART VIII — INSURANCE AND DEMAND


## 8.1 The insurance thesis, and why it currently cuts the other way

The standard fire-protection investment narrative runs on a single assumption: a hard property market forces building owners to upgrade fire protection, and the contractor collects. For the current cycle that assumption is inverted. Commercial property rates are falling, and a carrier sitting on surplus capacity has less leverage to make a sprinkler retrofit or an alarm replacement a condition of renewal.

Global commercial property rates fell **12%** in Q2 2026, following declines of **9%** in each of the two preceding quarters, Q4 2025 and Q1 2026 (Marsh, 2026-07-22). Global casualty moved the other way, at **+2%** in the same quarter. US commercial property rates fell **13%** in the same quarter, one of five regions posting a double-digit property decline (Marsh, 2026-07-22).

On a separate basis, the US broker channel reports the same direction. Average premium change across **all lines and all account sizes** was **−1.2%** in Q1 2026, which the publisher characterises as "a decisive sign of a softened market" (CIAB, 2026-05-13). The account-size split matters more than the headline for a route contractor's customer base:

| Account size | Average premium change, Q1 2026 |
|---|---|
| Large | **−2.7%** |
| Medium | **−1.9%** |
| Small | **+1.1%** |

Small accounts — the strip-centre restaurant, the single-tenant warehouse, the independent care home — are the only cohort still absorbing increases. The large accounts with the capital to fund a voluntary upgrade are the ones whose premiums are falling fastest.

The Marsh global property figure and the CIAB all-lines US figure are not comparable and are not combined anywhere in this report: different geography, different line mix, different respondent base. Both are carried on their own bases.

**One segment remains hard.** Habitational property is priced at **$0.20–$0.40 per $100 TIV** for non-CAT masonry of newer construction, **$0.60–$1.20** for older frame buildings, and **$1.50–$4.00+** for CAT-zone frame in Florida coastal and California wildland-urban-interface territory (Latent Insurance, 2026-05-22). The share of new habitational placement written in excess and surplus lines now runs **30%–40%**, against under **15%** a decade earlier, which the same publisher describes as major admitted-carrier retrenchment in "one of the hardest property segments in commercial insurance."

**What this touches and what it does not.** The distinction is the whole of the thesis:

- **Untouched: the code-mandated inspection, testing and maintenance annuity.** ITM obligation is created by code and, in healthcare, by federal conditions of participation. It does not move with the rate cycle. This is the defensible half of a target's revenue mix across sprinkler and suppression contracting, alarm and detection, and extinguisher and kitchen suppression.
- **Exposed: the carrier-driven upgrade cycle.** Retrofit and upgrade demand that depends on an underwriter demanding the work as a renewal condition weakens as capacity loosens. Install and upgrade backlog attributed to insurance pressure should be underwritten as cyclical, not structural, and separated line-by-line from the ITM base in quality-of-earnings work.

## 8.2 What insurance actually rates on, and what it does not

The published mechanism by which fire-suppression capability enters US property rating is the Public Protection Classification, which grades **more than 40,000** fire-response jurisdictions on a scale from Class 1, superior, to Class 10, below minimum criteria (ISO Mitigation, undated). "Virtually all U.S. insurers of homes and business property use Verisk's Public Protection Classifications in calculating premiums," and the price of fire insurance in a community with a good classification is "substantially lower than in a community with a poor PPC, assuming all other factors are equal" (Verisk, 2024-03-07).

**The scope limit is the point.** PPC grades the *public* fire service — department, water supply, communications — not the building. Within the Fire Suppression Rating Schedule the weighting is fire department **50%**, water supply **40%**, emergency communications **10%**, plus a community risk reduction bonus of up to **5.5%** (Federato, undated). It sits upstream of, and separate from, any building-level sprinkler credit. It generates demand for municipal water and hydrant work, not for sprinkler ITM, alarm monitoring or kitchen suppression.

Two quantified statements of the PPC premium effect are published. They give different numbers, are both weakly attributed, and are carried separately rather than reconciled.

An ISO-branded handout, undated on its face and hosted at a URL path dated 2022-05, gives annual premium on a $100,000 home of **$894** at Class 10 and **$373** at Class 5, with a stated saving of **$433/yr** moving from Class 9 to Class 5 — implying **$806** at Class 9 and a **58.3%** reduction from Class 10 to Class 5 (Derivations N14). The same document carries the structural fact that matters commercially: "HOMEOWNER'S RATES DO NOT DECREASE BELOW A CLASS 5." Residential credit stops at Class 5; **businesses continue to benefit down to Class 1.** That asymmetry is the commercial-property relevance of the whole mechanism.

A vendor publication, undated and explicitly self-labelled illustrative, gives bands instead (Federato, undated): up to **25%** discount at Class 1–2; roughly **10–15%** reduction at Class 3–4; minimal or no effect at Class 5–6; possible surcharges at Class 7–8, and significant surcharges in the Class 9–10 band. Community distribution on the same basis: Class 1 under **1%**, Class 2–3 about **7%**, Class 4–6 about **50%**, Class 7–9 about **40%**, and Class 10 about **2%** of communities. Its own caveat is that actual impacts vary by carrier, line of business and property characteristics. Non-load-bearing.

**The finding that matters most for anyone underwriting a retrofit-driven growth story: no carrier or broker publication establishes a quantified premium differential for sprinklered versus unsprinklered commercial risk.** The one quantified sprinkler discount figure in the public record is residential, four years old, and names no carrier: "discounts as high as 35% are offered for homes with sprinklers," attributed to a poll of property and casualty insurers with no carrier named and no poll methodology published, and conceding on the same page that discounts vary (Home Fire Sprinkler Coalition, last modified 2022-05-18).

That absence sits directly under the strongest-sounding claim in the standard fire-protection investment deck. A model that capitalises insurance-driven retrofit demand is capitalising an unquantified premium.

The corroborating evidence points the same way. The most on-point owner-facing insurance document available for the building classes this industry sells into — a 2026 habitational market guide — does not mention sprinklers, fire alarms, monitoring or fire protection upgrades as a coverage condition or a rating credit anywhere in its content (Latent Insurance, 2026-05-22). Construction class is doing the pricing work: on the stated midpoints, older frame prices at **3.0×** non-CAT newer masonry (Derivations N13).

**Where a buyer needs an insurance answer, buy it from someone licensed to give it.** Engage a licensed commercial property broker or an insurance due-diligence specialist and ask three questions on named accounts inside the target's book: whether any submission or renewal in the file shows a quantified sprinkler or alarm credit; whether protection class, as distinct from community PPC, appears in the rating; and whether kitchen-suppression standard compliance appears anywhere as a condition of coverage. Nothing in this report substitutes for that.

## 8.3 The loss evidence, including where it inverts

The loss data that would underpin any carrier credit is published by the standards body, not by a carrier. All figures below are from Ahrens, *U.S. Experience with Sprinklers* (NFPA, October 2021), data years 2015–2019:

| Metric | Value |
|---|---|
| Sprinklers present in reported structure fires | about **10%** of all reported structure fires |
| Operation rate, where present and fire large enough to activate | **92%** |
| Civilian death rate reduction per 1,000 fires, sprinklered vs no automatic extinguishing system | **89%** |
| Fires controlled by a single sprinkler head, where sprinklers activated | **77%** |
| Average loss per fire, all structures | **11% lower** in sprinklered structures |
| Average loss per fire, homes | **62% lower** |
| Average loss per fire, health care | **73% lower** |
| Average loss per fire, warehouses and manufacturing | **Higher** in sprinklered properties |

**The warehouse and manufacturing line is not a footnote.** The publisher attributes the inversion to smoke damage plus exceptional large-loss incidents. The life-safety case for sprinklers holds across every occupancy in the dataset; the property-loss case does not. It fails in exactly the two occupancy classes where highly-protected-risk underwriting concentrates and where the largest industrial accounts sit.

A buyer pitching a loss-reduction story into industrial and logistics accounts should know that the industry's own loss statistics do not support it on a property basis in those occupancies, and should expect a sophisticated risk manager in those segments to know it too. The argument that survives there is life safety, business continuity and code compliance — not average loss per fire.

## 8.4 The installed base

The commercial building stock against which the inspection obligation runs is sized from the 2018 Commercial Buildings Energy Consumption Survey (EIA CBECS 2018), the most recent completed cycle:

| Metric | Value |
|---|---|
| Total US commercial buildings | **5,918 thousand** (about 5.9 million) |
| Total commercial floorspace | **96,423 million sq ft** |
| Average building size | **16,294 sq ft** (Derivations N2) |

**Vintage, stated plainly.** Two EIA pages date the same release differently: the CBECS table pages state the data were "released September 2021 and revised September 2022," while the programme landing page states "final results released December 2022." Both values are carried; the usable description is a 2018 data year released across the 2021–2022 window. No 2024 cycle has been released or is underway, and no timeline for the next full cycle is published; the web-only office and education surveys run in 2023–2024 were not full CBECS cycles. **The installed base for this industry is therefore sized off eight-year-old data, and that is a structural limitation of every model built on it, including this one.**

Age distribution, which is the foundation of the ITM annuity:

| Year constructed | Buildings (thousands) | Floorspace (million sq ft) |
|---|---|---|
| Before 1946 | 709 | 9,246 |
| 1946–1959 | 517 | 6,937 |
| 1960–1969 | 685 | 10,360 |
| 1970–1979 | 831 | 13,061 |
| 1980–1989 | 794 | 13,465 |
| 1990–1999 | 921 | 15,535 |
| 2000–2009 | 924 | 17,500 |
| 2010–2018 | 537 | 10,319 |
| **Total** | **5,918** | **96,423** |

Both columns sum exactly to the published totals, so the partition is complete and non-overlapping. **59.7%** of buildings and **55.0%** of floorspace predate 1990; **24.7%** of buildings and **28.8%** of floorspace were built in 2000 or later (Derivations N1, N3).

Renovation status is the closest available proxy for system age.
