On floorspace, in millions of square feet, **45,165** — **46.8%** of the total — has had some renovation since 2000, while **43,832** (**45.5%**) has had none, and **7,426** (**7.7%**) was constructed in 2013 or later; the three categories are exhaustive and mutually exclusive (Derivations N4). Within the renovated stock, and on the same basis lighting upgrades cover 29,098, HVAC equipment 27,575 and roof replacement 20,862. The survey's one fire-adjacent renovation category is **"Fire, safety, or security upgrade"**, at **19,985** on the same floorspace basis and **570** thousand buildings (EIA CBECS 2018, Tables B10 and B6) — fire fused with safety and security, which is the same definitional problem that defeats the sizing instruments in Part II. **No instrument in the national building survey isolates sprinkler retrofit activity, and none establishes the sprinklered share of the stock.**

**No source establishes the sprinklered share of the commercial building stock** — which is precisely the input a retrofit market size requires. The survey does not ask. The nearest proxy is sprinkler presence in reported structure fires, which runs on a different denominator (fires, not buildings) and is biased downward, because sprinklered buildings generate fewer reportable fires in the first place:

| Occupancy | Sprinkler present in reported fires | Implied unsprinklered share of fires |
|---|---|---|
| Nursing home | **69%** | 31% |
| Hospital | **61%** | 39% |
| Prison or jail | **58%** | 42% |
| Manufacturing | **52%** | 48% |
| Educational | **40%** | 60% |
| Warehouse | **34%** | 66% |
| Store or office | **26%** | 74% |
| Public assembly | **25%** | 75% |
| Residential | **8%** | 92% |

Source: NFPA, October 2021, data years 2015–2019, Figure 1; implied shares at Derivations N9. These are shares of fires and must not be relabelled as shares of buildings.

On this evidence the retrofit headroom for sprinkler contracting sits in store, office and public assembly, where three-quarters of fires occur in unsprinklered premises — and those are also the occupancies with the weakest mandate and, in office, the weakest demand backdrop. Health care runs at 61%–69% presence, so healthcare exposure should be underwritten as an ITM and repair annuity with install upside confined to new build.

## 8.5 Occupancy and demographic drivers

### Healthcare and senior housing

Two federal regulations create the hardest recurring obligation in the industry, because the consequence of non-compliance is loss of Medicare and Medicaid reimbursement rather than a fine.

Long-term care facilities are held to NFPA 101, **2012 edition, issued 11 August 2011**, plus TIAs 12-1, 12-2, 12-3 and 12-4; were required to "install an approved, supervised automatic sprinkler system in accordance with the 1999 edition of NFPA 13" by **13 August 2013**; and must "test, inspect, and maintain" it in accordance with the **1998 edition of NFPA 25** (42 CFR §483.90, eCFR, accessed 2026-07-29). A sprinkler system out of service for **more than 10 hours** requires evacuation of the affected area or a fire watch until restored.

Hospitals are held to NFPA 101 and NFPA 99, both **2012 editions issued 11 August 2011**, with TIAs through 2013–2014, and to the same **10-hour** impairment rule (42 CFR §482.41, eCFR, accessed 2026-07-29). The hospital condition of participation carries no routine sprinkler testing frequency of its own; the frequency arrives through the incorporated standards.

**Note the edition freeze as a diligence item.** A contractor's healthcare book is scoped and priced against NFPA 101 and 99 in their 2012 editions and, for long-term care sprinklers, NFPA 13 (1999) and NFPA 25 (1998) — not current editions. Any target with healthcare concentration should be tested for whether its technicians, documentation and pricing are aligned to the editions the federal regulation actually incorporates.

The build side of senior housing is close to dormant (NIC MAP, Q2 2026, published 2026-07-09):

| Metric | Q2 2026 |
|---|---|
| Occupancy | **89.9%**, up 0.4 pp from Q1 |
| Occupied units | **639,650**, up about 3,700 QoQ |
| Inventory growth | **0.4% YoY** |
| Units under construction | **fewer than 16,000** |
| Implied inventory | about **711,500 units** (Derivations N11) |
| Under construction as share of inventory | about **2.25%** (Derivations N11) |

The publisher's own framing is that "high occupancy rates and static construction mean fewer new housing options for older adults." Highest occupancy runs Boston **93.3%**, San Francisco **92.7%** and Baltimore **91.8%**; the lowest are Miami **86.2%**, Atlanta **86.5%** and San Antonio **87.0%** on the same measure.

Direction of travel: ageing demographics are a **utilisation** story in senior housing, not a construction story. At 0.4% inventory growth, a fire-protection target selling into the segment is an ITM route business with negligible install exposure, and should be valued on route density and contract renewal rather than on backlog.

### Restaurants

Industry projections for 2026: sales of **$1.55 trillion**, real growth of **1.3%**, employment of **15.8 million** and about **100,000** jobs added, with the publisher "cautiously optimistic about 2026" while noting "persistent cost pressures, such as uneven traffic and rising costs" (NRA, 2026-02-11). A weaker secondary reading, undated and indicative only, reports that just **42%** of operators said they were profitable in the prior year (Restaurant Business Online, undated).

**The number that determines route economics does not exist in the public record.** Kitchen suppression and extinguisher routes run on locations, not sales dollars, and no public source publishes total US restaurant locations, gross closures or net unit growth. A route-density model for the extinguisher and kitchen-suppression sub-vertical cannot be built from public data and has to be built bottom-up from a target's own customer list, permit data and site counts in its actual service territory. Any seller-supplied market size for this segment should be treated as unsourceable until it is reconciled to that bottom-up count.

### Warehouse and industrial

| Metric | Q1 2026 |
|---|---|
| National industrial vacancy | **7.0%**, described as having dipped below its cyclical peak |
| Net absorption | **40 msf**, up 52% YoY |
| Completions | **54 msf**, down 27% YoY |
| Under construction | **284 msf**, up 6.2% YoY |

Source: Cushman & Wakefield US Industrial MarketBeat, report dated 2026-04-14. The same publisher subsequently headlined "Modest New Supply and Intensifying Demand Push Vacancy Back Below 7%" (Cushman & Wakefield, 2026-07-17).

Direction of travel: completions down 27% year on year with under-construction stock up 6.2% means the install pull flattens near-term and re-accelerates on a lag. Absorption is strong and vacancy is tightening, so the demand signal is intact; the timing is the issue. A model that straight-lines warehouse install revenue off current absorption will front-load a pipeline that arrives later.

### Data centres

This is the largest construction-driven pull in the demand set, and it is announced rather than contracted:

| Operator | Figure | Detail | Date |
|---|---|---|---|
| Alphabet / Google | **$195–205 bn** 2026 capex, raised | "as it accelerates AI data center buildout" | 2026-07-22 |
| Google | **$205 bn** capex | reported as an increase citing demand growth and capacity constraints | Jul 2026 |
| Meta | **$50 bn** | Richland Parish, Louisiana campus expansion to **5 GW** | 2026-07-13 |
| Meta + BlackRock | not stated | El Paso, Texas — **1 GW** of compute | 2026-07-29 |
| Meta | — | Temple, Texas facility begins operations | 2026-07-23 |
| AWS | — | added more capacity "than any other company" in 2025, including **1.2 GW in Q4** | 2026-07-02 |
| DTE Energy | — | data-centre pipeline above **8 GW** after deals with Oracle and Google | 2026-07-29 |
| Flexential | — | 110 acres, Talty, Texas — **108 MW** campus | Jul 2026 |
| Galaxy Digital | — | Waco, Texas — first **75 MW** live in 2028 | Jul 2026 |

Sources: DatacenterDynamics for all rows except the $205 bn single-point capex figure (Construction Dive, accessed 2026-07-29). The two Google readings are consistent — $205 bn is the top of the reported range — and both are carried.

Meta's Louisiana campus implies **$10 bn per GW** of announced capacity (Derivations N15), which is a usable scalar for sizing an announced campus. It does not convert into contractor-addressable revenue: the figure is total campus capex including land, power, shell, mechanical and electrical and IT, and no public source establishes fire protection as a percentage of data-centre construction value. Nor does the public record establish the split between clean-agent and pre-action water scope on a hyperscale campus, which is what determines whether the work lands with a sprinkler contractor or a special-hazard house. Both are gaps to close with contractor references and actual bid documents in diligence, not with a published ratio.

**The counter-signal arrived in the same week.** One operator cancelled a $30 bn project, the subject of a trade deep-dive titled "What QTS' Canceled $30B Project Reveals About AI Data Center Development" (Data Center Knowledge, accessed 2026-07-29). Announced capex is not contracted backlog, and a fire-protection pipeline attributed to announced campuses should be discounted for cancellation and phasing.

### Office

Two publishers disagree on national office vacancy, and both figures are carried: Cushman & Wakefield report **20.2%** for Q1 2026 (report dated 2026-04-14) while CommercialCafe report **17.7%**, down 170 bps year on year, for June 2026 (2026-07-20) — a 250-bp gap driven by methodology, specifically universe definition, sublease treatment and building-class cut-off, rather than by a data error. The addressable office ITM base sits somewhere between the two.

Underneath the headline, the recovery is Class A only. Net absorption was **−4.0 msf** in Q1 2026, against four-quarter rolling absorption of **+5.2 msf**, "the highest-level post-pandemic"; Class A four-quarter rolling absorption was **+18.7 msf**, with **+1.4 msf** in the quarter (Cushman & Wakefield, 2026-04-14).

New office construction has effectively stopped. Under construction as of June 2026 was **29.6 msf**, about **0.4%** of total office stock, with **11.1 msf** delivered year to date and implied total stock of about **7,400 msf** (CommercialCafe, 2026-07-20; Derivations N10). Office-to-multifamily conversion projects completed or under construction reached **11.8 msf** in 2025, "higher than in any prior year" — which is about **0.16%** of stock (Derivations N10).

Conversion is a record rate on a rounding-error base and will not reallocate the office ITM annuity at scale inside a hold period. The material office risk to route density is deferred inspection in half-empty and mothballed buildings, and no public source establishes a deferral rate or what inspection practice actually is in a vacant building. For any target with office concentration that is an open diligence question to be answered from the target's own recurring-revenue churn, work-order history and AHJ correspondence.

## 8.6 Third-party market sizing, and why it is not used

Two third-party estimates of the fire protection market are in circulation and are quoted widely enough that a reader will have seen them. Neither is used anywhere in this report.

| | Grand View Research | MarketsandMarkets |
|---|---|---|
| Report | Fire Protection Systems Market | Fire Protection System Market (Report 1018) |
| Publication | June 2023, last updated May 2026 | October 2025 |
| Base-year size | **$88,945.0 m (2024)** | **$85.06 bn (2025)** |
| Forecast size | **$130,369.2 m (2030)** | **$118.14 bn (2030)** |
| Stated CAGR | **6.6% (2025–2030)** | **6.8% (2025–2030)** |
| Scope | Global; detection, suppression, response, analysis, sprinkler; services span managed service, installation and design, maintenance | Global; products, services and equipment across residential, commercial and industrial |
| Segment detail | not shown | fire suppression **36.4%** share; APAC fastest region at **8.0%** CAGR; industrial fastest vertical at **7.3%** CAGR |
| Named players | not shown | Honeywell International, Johnson Controls, Siemens |

Rolling the Grand View 2024 base forward one year at its own stated CAGR gives an implied 2025 global market of about **$94.8 bn**, against **$85.06 bn** from MarketsandMarkets for the same year — **$9,755 m** apart, or **11.47%** higher, on nominally the same scope (Derivations N8). Both values stand; no midpoint is taken.

Four reasons neither figure appears in this report's sizing:

1. **Neither is US-specific.** Both are global. Nothing in either publication isolates the United States, which is the only geography this practice underwrites.
2. **Neither is decomposable into the three sub-verticals.** Sprinkler is a product line inside "products," not a services segment; inspection, testing and maintenance is buried inside "maintenance"; kitchen suppression and clean agent are not broken out at all. Neither figure can size sprinkler and suppression contracting, alarm and detection, or extinguisher and special hazard separately, which is the only cut that matters for a buy-box.
3. **One is published to a precision no stated methodology supports.** A base-year figure of $88,945.0 million is six significant figures on a global market estimate. Neither publisher publishes a traceable methodology on its public page — no source for the base year, no bottom-up build, no survey base.
4. **The stated forecast window and the arithmetic do not describe the same period in one case.** A 6.6% CAGR labelled 2025–2030 applied to the 2024 base compounds to about $130,516 m over six years, which reproduces the published 2030 figure of $130,369.2 m; the CAGR implied by the two stated endpoints across 2024→2030 is **6.58%** (Derivations N6). The label describes a five-year window and the arithmetic a six-year one. The second publisher's figures are internally consistent on this test: its stated endpoints imply **6.79%** over five years against a stated 6.8% (Derivations N7).

The practical consequence is that market size for this sector has to be built bottom-up — from the installed base in Section 8.4, the occupancy-level obligation in Section 8.5, and a target's own route density and contract file — rather than purchased.

---

# PART IX — AGGREGATION CHALLENGES


## 9.1 The central-station question

The single best diligence question in this market is whether the target owns its UL-listed central station, and if it does not, whose paper the recurring revenue sits on.

The market-level size premium quantifies what the answer is worth. Sellers above $500,000 RMR averaged 46x monthly revenue against 36x for sellers under $50,000 RMR — 10 turns of RMR, a ratio of 1.278x, a 27.8% uplift (V13) — inside one source, one methodology and one year (Barnes Associates via SecurityInfoWatch, 2025-08-18). The mechanism the record gives for that gap is infrastructure ownership: a buyer that owns a central station removes the wholesale monitoring fee at close, and a buyer that does not carries it as a direct cost for as long as it holds the accounts. Approximately 2% of industry RMR changed hands in the acquisition year covered by that keynote (Barnes Associates via SecurityInfoWatch, 2025-08-18), while about 21% of the industry has changed hands over the last four years (Barnes Buchanan via SDM, 2026-02-16) — the accounts in circulation in any one year are a small slice of a base that turns over quickly.

At least one major wholesale central station is sponsor-owned and has changed sponsor recently: Becklar, whose brands include AvantGuard, Freeus, Armstrongs and Eyeforce, went from BV Investment Partners to Graham Partners on 2024-12-10 with BV rolling its stake, and is itself consolidating monitoring centres, having acquired Dynamark Monitoring on 2025-09-03. National Monitoring Center sits inside The Netwatch Group after a merger with Netwatch, Onwatch Multifire and CalAtlantic. COPS Monitoring has acquired the wholesale monitoring business of Security Partners and AlarmWATCH. Ownership of COPS Monitoring, Rapid Response Monitoring Services and Affiliated Monitoring is not established in the public record — three top-tier wholesale central stations whose ownership bears directly on who controls the infrastructure under a target's RMR.

Three exposures follow when a target resells rather than owns. The gross RMR is not the margin, because the wholesale fee is a direct cost and the 51.2% blended monitoring and services margin in the industry data is not achievable by a pure reseller. The subscriber contracts may not be freely assignable. And the wholesale agreement carries its own change-of-control provisions, so a transaction can trigger a renegotiation of the cost line under the very revenue being bought. Buyers that own central stations — among them Everon, Securitas Technology, Zeus Fire and Security with two UL-listed centres, and National Fire & Safety with its own — are the population able to pay the top band.

A sub-threshold recurring share almost always resolves into something other than monitoring. Annualised RMR as a share of FY2025 revenue, computed from company-reported survey data (V14; SDM 100, 2026), runs from 98.86% at ADT and 54.47% at Kastle Systems and 52.99% at Vector Security, through 37.70% at Zeus, 35.38% at Securitas Technology, 32.13% at Everon, 30.24% at Pye-Barker and 23.65% at Pavion, down to 11.78% at Minuteman, 8.88% at ORR Protection and 6.07% at Security 101. A target in the 30–50% range is an RMR conversation. **A target under 10% is an EBITDA conversation, and its "recurring revenue" claim on inspection, testing and maintenance work is a different and less bankable form of recurrence with no assignable monitoring contract to value.** Special hazard sits structurally at the bottom of that column — low RMR, high engineering content, project-weighted — and should be underwritten on EBITDA and backlog.

## 9.2 Contract assignability

The recurring revenue may not travel with the deal, and there are three distinct places where it can fail to.

The subscriber contract is the first. Whether monitoring agreements are freely assignable is a document-by-document question and it determines whether the acquirer buys a contracted stream or an opportunity to re-sign a customer base; the record establishes that the question exists and is unresolved at the market level, not that any particular answer is standard. The second is the wholesale monitoring agreement beneath it, which carries its own change-of-control provisions and which the acquirer inherits as the cost side of the same revenue. The third is the form of recurrence itself: where the recurring claim rests on inspection and testing rather than monitoring, there is frequently no standing contract to assign at all, only a service history and a compliance obligation that keeps the customer returning.

Bulk account and asset purchases show what does travel and on what terms. The ADT multifamily transaction moved approximately 200,000 customer units representing approximately $2.6 million of recurring monthly revenue with no branch infrastructure, no technicians and no backlog, and with a non-compete running only through 2028-10-02 (ADT 8-K, 2025-09-15). Buying accounts is not buying a business, and the two are priced in different languages (§6.4).

The market's own convention makes the point. Alarm accounts have historically been bought and sold like receivables precisely because the contract, not the enterprise, is the asset. Where the contract does not assign, the receivable analogy fails and the multiple built on it fails with it.

## 9.3 Roll-up of the roll-ups

Platforms buy platforms in this market, and platforms are sold in pieces. Both patterns are on the record with named parties.

**Chains of three or more owners.** AI Fire: founded 2009 as Impact Fire, Caltius Equity Partners 2012, Audax 2017, TruArc Partners 2021 with TruArc retaining a stake at exit, Blackstone 2025 — four sponsors, with 20 add-ons completed under the fourth. Performance Systems Integration: Peterson Partners, then The Riverside Company, then Summit Fire & Security under BlackRock Long Term Private Capital on 2025-08-12, having grown from 4 to 14 locations and 108 to over 450 employees under the middle owner. Protegis Fire & Safety: Align Capital from 2017 via ISA Fire & Security, seven add-ons taking it from 6 to 13 branches, then absorbed into Summit Fire & Security on 2022-05-02. Sciens Building Solutions: Huron Capital from early 2016 with WSA Systems as the platform acquisition and ten add-ons, then Carlyle announced 2021-11-10 with Huron retaining a minority stake. Summit Companies itself: CI Capital Partners, then BlackRock LTPC on 2021-09-20, on a base of 82 acquisitions since 1999. Convergint: Ares from 2018, a stake to Leonard Green and Harvest Partners on 2021-12-15, then an ≈$850M single-asset continuation vehicle closed 2026-03-02 — eight years and 40-plus acquisitions without a sale.

**Platforms buying completed roll-ups.** Summit Fire & Security has twice been the terminal buyer of another sponsor's finished platform, taking Protegis from Align and PSI from Riverside, reaching 126 locations in 37 states after the second. APi acquired CertaSite, a Midwest inspection-first platform that had completed at least 21 acquisitions under Riverside, with cash on hand. Everon acquired a bulk account book from the corporate seller that had sold Everon itself to Everon's sponsor two years earlier. Becklar, a wholesale central station, is consolidating other monitoring centres. Guardian Protection, a residential-lineage alarm company, bought Monitronics' Commercial Alarm Accounts Division out of that estate on 2025-03-27. Riverside has run a fire extinguisher platform, a franchised kitchen-exhaust-cleaning platform and a fire ITM platform simultaneously, exiting two of them in 2025 and 2026 — the same house appearing as buyer and seller in the same sub-vertical in the same period.

**The instructive interval is a carve-out sold in pieces.** Sentinel Capital Partners acquired Carrier's industrial fire business as Spectrum Safety Solutions with a closing date of 2024-07-01 in Carrier's FY2024 10-K, at a disclosed enterprise value of $1.425B for the four-brand group. Twenty-one months later, on 2026-03-31, the Marioff water-mist division was agreed to Inflexion. Twenty-four months after the same closing date, on 2026-07-09, the Autronica unit closed to MSA Safety at a disclosed value of approximately $555 million (V15). Two of four brands were divested inside 24 months, leaving Det-Tronics and Fireye as the residual. A four-brand industrial carve-out was not a hold-and-integrate; it was a separation.

## 9.4 Brand policy, and why it decides what a target list can see

Three brand policies operate in this market, and each has a different consequence for anyone trying to tell an independent business from an owned one.

**Absorb everything.** The acquired name disappears at or shortly after close. Protegis and Performance Systems Integration both went into Summit Fire & Security. ADT Commercial was rebranded Everon at the GTCR close. Pavion is itself the rebrand of Corbett Technology Solutions and completed a "one Pavion" migration in 2025 consolidating its acquired brands. At the extreme, the acquired name never becomes public at all: APi's bolt-ons are disclosed as counts and aggregate consideration, with four Q1-2026 targets described only as "individually immaterial acquisitions" at $25M aggregate, and the FY2024 individually disclosed acquisitions appearing without names. Under this policy the acquired company vanishes from the observable universe and a target list built on trading names will show it as independent indefinitely.

**Prefix the legacy name.** The acquired brand survives as a division of the parent — "Impact Fire, a Division of AI Fire" — which keeps the operating name searchable and the ownership discoverable in the same string. This is the only one of the three policies that makes ownership legible from the brand itself.

**House of brands.** Regional identities are retained deliberately. Zeus Fire and Security states a "House of Brands" model with distinct regional brand identities, holding UAS, SMG, Alert Alarm and Martin Systems. National Fire & Safety operates Frontier Fire Protection, Elite FPS, RCI Systems and Texas Fire Alarm under one sponsor. Under this policy a single owner presents to a screen as four or five separate independents in four or five separate metros.

The consequence for screening is that a target list built by name-matching will be wrong in both directions. Four unrelated entities in this space share the word Guardian — Guardian Fire Services in Nashville under Investcorp, Guardian Fire Protection Services in the Mid-Atlantic under Knox Lane, Guardian Protection under Armstrong Group, and Guardian Alarm Company — and merging any two of them corrupts both an ownership map and a pipeline. The Summit name is reused across unrelated corporate families. Ownership must be established per legal entity from a primary source, not inferred from a trading name, and the absence of a brand from the record is not evidence that the business is unowned.

## 9.5 What cannot be evidenced about integration

Three things do not exist in the public record, and each of them is normally the load-bearing element of a synergy narrative.

**No named post-close margin-uplift case with before-and-after figures.** No acquirer in this market publishes what a named acquired business earned before it was bought and what it earned afterwards. What exists instead is growth claims without bases — "approximately quadrupled Adjusted EBITDA" since 2018 across 40-plus acquisitions at Convergint, with no base and no absolute value; "greater than 35-fold increase in revenue and EBITDA" under a prior owner at Sciens — and operational counts, such as PSI moving from 4 to 14 locations, 108 to over 450 employees and 64 to 279 technicians under Riverside. None of those permit a margin bridge.

**No disclosed synergy target.** APi's Chubb transaction was disclosed at ≈13.3x LTM Adjusted EBITDA *including synergies*, but no synergy dollar target accompanied it; the ≈$21.9M implied by the difference between that figure and the same company's disclosed revenue and margin inputs is inference from two disclosed numbers rather than a disclosure (V16). The two 2026 acquisitions closed with revenue contributions disclosed and no EBITDA, and the incremental margins implied by the guidance raises — 15.0% and 14.1% — could bundle FX, organic revisions, purchase-accounting effects or expected synergies in unknown proportion.

**Almost no platform-level organic or same-store metric.** The single exception in the record is APi's disclosure of 19 consecutive quarters of double-digit organic inspection revenue growth and a stated ratio of approximately $3 to $4 of incremental service work generated annually per $1 of inspection work. No sponsor-backed platform in this market publishes an organic growth figure, a same-store figure, a retention figure or an attrition figure at platform level. Segment-level margin exists for public filers — APi's Safety Services segment reported FY2024 Adjusted EBITDA of $765M on $4,797M of revenue, a 15.9% margin — but a segment margin is not an integration result.

Given that, a buyer should decline the synergy narrative and demand the following in its place, all of which are documentable at target level even where the market publishes nothing:

1. **The corporate-cost bridge, shown.** Target EBITDA before and after allocation, with the allocation itemised. The disclosed template exists: $158M of segment Adjusted EBITDA, less $(14)M of estimated corporate cost allocation, equals the $144M the multiple was struck on (ADT 8-K, 2023-10-02). A target's standalone EBITDA that has never carried its share of overhead is not the denominator the deal should be priced on.
2. **Monitoring revenue split by owned versus wholesale central station**, with the wholesale fee shown as a line item and the wholesale agreement's change-of-control clause produced.
3. **Annualised RMR divided by revenue, computed from the target's own contract file** rather than from management's characterisation of what is recurring, and a statement of how much of the recurring claim is inspection work rather than monitoring.
4. **Attrition and price-increase history by cohort.** The only market figures available are 3–4% annual increases reported with minimal attrition impact and a monitoring and services margin of 51.2% against 53.9% in 2017, with recurring gross margins down from 54% in 2015 — a declining-margin backdrop against which a target's own trend is the relevant fact.
5. **Assignability and renewal terms on inspection contracts**, and the customer-level compliance driver where no contract exists.
6. **The acquirer's own before-and-after margin on two named prior tuck-ins, with dates.** No acquirer in this market has published this. A buyer underwriting integration is entitled to ask its own operating team for what the market does not disclose, and an inability to produce it internally is itself a diligence finding.

---

---

# PART X — BUY-SIDE IMPLICATIONS

## 10.1 Where to hunt

The structure of this market points a buyer at three places, in this order.

**The inspection book in a jurisdiction with mandatory electronic reporting.** Where a jurisdiction has mandated third-party electronic ITM reporting, three things are true at once that are true nowhere else: the obligation is enforced rather than nominal, deficiencies are documented on a clock that forces the owner to act, and the currency of every system in the jurisdiction is a matter of record. That is the only condition under which the repair pull-through in §3.1 can be underwritten from something other than the seller's assertion. The 63% currency figure in §1.5 comes from exactly such a jurisdiction, which is the point: the measured shortfall sits where enforcement is strongest, not where it is weakest.

**Sub-vertical 3, deliberately, and for the reason the sizing failed.** The absence of a published revenue denominator for extinguisher, kitchen and special hazard work (§2.4) is a barrier to writing a market-size slide and an advantage in acquiring. No aggregator has published a number for sponsors to anchor on, the obligated-site count is large and countable, and the work is route-structured with statutory frequency. The layer within it that is unsized even in this report — clean agent and industrial special hazard — is the one with the highest technical barrier and the fewest qualified contractors.

**The interior West and Plains, on evidence rather than on the usual assumption.** §5.3 reverses the state-level read that circulates about this market. Alaska is the only state in no verified operating footprint, and states routinely described as open carry named branches from up to four distinct owners. The correct inference is not that these markets are empty; it is that the mapping is too incomplete to support a whitespace claim, and that any territory thesis needs a licence-registry pass before capital is committed to it. Treat §5.3 as a screening prior and nothing more.

What not to hunt on: a metro saturation ranking. §5.1 sets out why one cannot honestly be built from what is published, and a ranking assembled anyway would be systematically blind to several of the largest estates in the industry.

## 10.2 Value-creation levers to underwrite

Ranked by how well the evidence in this report supports them.

1. **Repair attach against a fixed inspection base.** The strongest lever, because the trigger is documented rather than sold: a deficiency found on a mandated visit is a work order the owner has a code duty to close. This is underwritable from a target's own records as the ratio of repair revenue to inspection revenue, split between electronic-reporting and paper jurisdictions.
2. **Mix shift from install to inspection and service.** The margin differential is real and it is disclosed in two independent constructions (§7.2). It is also slow: the one listed acquirer reporting the shift moved 14 points across four years.
3. **Route density in sub-vertical 3.** Structurally the right lever for a statutory-frequency route business — and the one this report cannot quantify. §7.6 puts it where it belongs, as a diligence question rather than a research question.
4. **Price.** Non-discretionary, licensed, statutorily scheduled work with a documented alternative of non-compliance is the textbook condition for pricing power. Nothing in the record measures realised price movement in fire ITM, so underwrite it as a hypothesis to be tested against the target's own rate history, not as a market fact.
5. **Central-station insourcing.** Where a target's recurring revenue sits on a third party's paper, bringing it onto owned infrastructure is worth roughly ten turns of recurring monthly revenue at market level (§9.1). This is a lever only for a buyer that already owns a listed station or can credibly build one.

Not on the list: synergies. §9.5 states plainly that no named post-close margin-uplift case with before-and-after figures, no disclosed synergy target, and no platform-level organic or same-store metric exists anywhere in the public record for this sector. A synergy number in a model here is an assumption wearing a fact's clothing.

## 10.3 Underwriting guardrails

- **Never blend the multiple populations.** §6.1 defines five that are not comparable, and every error in this market's published valuation commentary is a population error. A recurring-monthly-revenue multiple and an earnings multiple are different languages on different denominators (§6.4).
- **Never apply a large-platform multiple to a tuck-in, in either direction.** The widest spread in circulation is one company's disclosed ceiling divided into an unsourced trade-press range (§6.5).
- **Treat "recurring revenue" as a claim to be read, not a line to be accepted.** Ask for the contracts, in full, and sort them by form: written multi-year with an assignment right, annual purchase order, and undocumented habit. §3.2 shows the three behave completely differently in a change of control. A target below roughly a tenth of revenue in genuine recurring monitoring usually turns out, on inspection, to be describing inspection agreements with no assignable contract to value.
- **Establish the central-station answer before pricing anything** (§9.1).
- **Do not underwrite a retrofit-driven growth story on carrier pressure in the current rate environment**, and do not underwrite one on a sprinklered-versus-unsprinklered premium differential at all, because no carrier or broker publishes one (§8.2).
- **Do not size a retrofit market.** No source establishes the sprinklered share of US commercial building stock (§8.4).
- **Check the licence, then check whether it travels.** §7.4 is the section buyers underestimate: the licensing regime that protects the incumbent's margin is the same regime that can strand a book on a qualifying individual who is retiring. Engage licensing counsel in the relevant states on the specific question of whether the contractor licence and the qualifying-individual registration survive the transaction structure contemplated, and what the re-qualification timeline is if they do not.
- **Retainage and contract balances are where a first-time buyer of a contracting business gets hurt** (§7.5). Have a construction-experienced accountant model the working-capital unwind under the actual retainage terms rather than on a days-outstanding average.
- **A named target's value is out of scope for this report and for market data generally.** Market-level ranges are context. Anything at the level of a specific business requires a licensed appraiser, and any tax or securities question requires the relevant specialist named at the point it arises.

## 10.4 Thresholds that change the strategy

Each of these is observable, and each would move the conclusion rather than decorate it.

- **A jurisdiction of scale deletes an existing inspection, testing or maintenance requirement.** One state deleting a high-rise retrofit requirement is an event (§1.8); a second would make reversibility a trend and would require the durability of the annuity to be re-underwritten rather than assumed.
- **Commercial property rates return to increase for two consecutive quarters.** That restores the carrier leverage the upgrade-cycle story depends on, and turns §8.1's finding from a headwind into the tailwind the industry currently claims it already is.
- **Any fire or life-safety business files an S-1, or a rating agency publishes an unentitled opinion on one of the named platforms.** Either would supply, for the first time, the renewal and retention disclosure that §3.3 establishes does not exist — and the entire annuity thesis would move from proxy to measured.
- **The disclosed bolt-on ceiling at the fastest consolidator moves above 6x.** That is the single cleanest signal that competition at the sub-$5M-EBITDA end has begun to erode the entry advantage the thesis rests on (§6.5).
- **A second well-capitalised consolidator reaches 40-plus acquisitions in a calendar year.** Two buyers at that cadence chasing the same seller profile compresses the bottom of the spread first.
- **An electronic-reporting mandate is adopted by a top-ten metro.** It converts an unmeasured obligation into a documented one across a large installed base at once, and it is the condition under which the pull-through lever becomes underwritable at scale.

## 10.5 Likely acquirers versus likely sellers

**Likely acquirers.** The funded and demonstrably active set is unusually crowded for a market this size: the listed strategic that deployed over a billion dollars in a single quarter and holds a published inspection-mix target it has not yet reached; the private platform running the fastest add-on programme in the industry; the platforms whose sponsors entered within the last twenty-four months and are therefore early in a hold — Summit under BDT & MSD, AI Fire under Blackstone, Encore under Permira, Guardian Fire Services under Investcorp, Security 101 under Morgan Stanley Capital Partners, FSS Technologies under Lightview; and the permanent-capital and family holders, who compete for the same assets without a clock and can therefore outlast a fund bidder on process.

**Likely sellers.** This side is inference from hold period and nothing more, and it should be read as such. The longest unexited sponsor positions in the register are the natural candidates, together with the manufacturer platform already selling divisions individually rather than as a bundle. Two important qualifications: continued ownership could not be confirmed from a primary source for two of the platforms whose hold periods look longest, so those are candidates on the strength of an absence of news rather than on evidence; and one sponsor in this market is simultaneously an active buyer and an active seller, which makes hold-period logic a weak predictor for its portfolio specifically.

**What both sides mean for a buy-side mandate.** Every likely seller is a potential source of divested brands, and every likely acquirer is a competing bidder in the metros where coverage is demonstrable. Neither list is a target list. The independents that make good acquisitions here are, by construction, the businesses nobody has written about — which is why §5.1 refuses to build a target board from platform disclosures, and why a screen against state licence registries is the next piece of work rather than an optional refinement.

---


---

# WHAT WE DON'T KNOW YET

A named gap is worth more than a confident guess, and in this market several of the gaps are more decision-relevant than the figures around them.

**The fire share of alarm, detection and monitoring.** Both available instruments fuse fire with burglar at the definition level. The sub-vertical has a ceiling and no size, and no published source separates them.

**The clean-agent and industrial special-hazard layer.** Unsized in unit terms and in revenue terms alike. So is restaurant wet-chemical suppression service, which means a statutory twice-yearly obligation at 737,325 sites is missing from the sub-vertical 3 arithmetic entirely rather than estimated into it.

**Any inspection-book renewal, retention or attrition rate from a fire contractor.** Not disclosed by any participant, listed or private. There is no fire or life-safety-services S-1 in EDGAR, so the document that would normally settle it does not exist, and rating-agency opinions on the named platforms are entitlement-gated rather than absent.

**The composition of an inspection book by contract form** — written multi-year with an assignment right, annual purchase order, or undocumented habit. Nobody publishes it. Given §3.2, this is the most commercially decisive unquantified variable in the industry, and it is a diligence question rather than a research one.

**Route density in any form** — stops per technician per day, drive-time share, minimum viable metro density, revenue per inspection route. The only figures in circulation are vendor marketing with uncited attributions, and none is carried here.

**Revenue per technician for fire-protection contracting.** No filer discloses a technician count against a fire contracting revenue line.

**Whether a mothballed office building keeps getting inspected.** No source measures deferral of inspection, testing and maintenance. This is the live question on the annuity's durability in a market with office vacancy where §8.5 puts it, and it is answerable only inside a target's own book.

**The national share of legally inspectable systems that are current.** Two instrumented jurisdictions are on the record and they are not a sample. The top-down compliance-gap pitch cannot be sourced.

**The sprinklered share of US commercial building stock.** The survey that sizes the stock does not ask. Any retrofit market size requires it.

**The referenced-standard editions each model-code edition freezes.** The edition-freeze mechanism in §1.1 is the load-bearing link in the whole regulatory case, and the specific mapping is evidenced by a single fire district's adoption instrument and one trade-association statement rather than by the referenced-standards chapter itself.

**Fire-specific transaction multiples at any size tier.** Established as absent rather than merely unfound: eight houses, thirteen publications, and the one house with a sector-dedicated update lists 110-plus transactions with a value on none.

**Certification-holder counts**, nationally or by state. The licensing moat in §7.4 is real and cannot be sized.

Three routes to closing parts of this are identified and unwalked, and each is a defined piece of work rather than a dead end: a Census API key unlocks the product-line tables that split extinguisher servicing out of its containing industry code; four states publish franchise disclosure documents free, and the financial-performance item in a hood-cleaning or extinguisher franchise disclosure is the best available route-economics evidence anywhere; and a deduplicated pass across the fifty state licence registries would produce the fire-specific establishment count that no federal instrument publishes.

# APPENDIX — DATA CONFLICTS, CAVEATS & SOURCE NOTES

## A.0 Provenance

**Consolidated from twelve research workstreams**, compiled 2026-07-29 — 7,918 lines carrying 809 source URLs. Eight in the first round: government sizing; codes and the mandate; the consolidator landscape in sprinkler, suppression and integrated life safety; the consolidator landscape in alarm, monitoring and extinguisher or kitchen roll-ups; operating brand names and domains by platform; unit economics; valuation and the deal record; and regulatory, insurance and demand drivers. Four gap-fills in the second: brand rosters for the buy-side consolidator register; sub-vertical 3 sizing; multiples, bank reports and the platform-versus-tuck-in spread; and the inspection annuity quantified.

A primary-source verification pass ran on 2026-07-30 and is held as a source in the same folder, `verification-pass-2026-07-30.md`.

Every figure in the underlying research carries a basis label — a company or government table said it, a trade outlet said it, or it was computed with the arithmetic shown. Across the twelve files the counts are 1,062 · 198 · 182 in that order.

Every GF Data figure, every Census container figure, the Cintas dollar lines to the thousand, the federal 1998 and 1999 standard-edition references, the sprinkler loss inversion in warehouses and manufacturing, and the Rockville figures all confirmed exactly as written.

The first run of this market against the hardened guard set. **No figure was
re-measured and no source was re-read.**

The published working printed a rounded intermediate and then a product taken from the unrounded one, so the line did not compute — `crossfoot.mts` reports it. The finding the derivation exists to make, that the stated CAGR label and the arithmetic window differ, is unaffected. |

| Quoted as | The source's own words | Where |

### Source URLs carried through

The Sources register carried **193 of the corpus's 655 URLs**; the audit
reported the shortfall as "40 dropped" because it printed the length of a
40-item sample rather than the count. Both are fixed: the register now carries
every research URL by document, and the audit reports the true number. This
master is the reason that reporting bug was found.

### The coverage finding

The sourcing guard could classify **30% of this market's origins** and refused
to verdict. 188 domains were classed by hand — the operator and sponsor roster
this market is largely built from, its advisors, associations and trade press —
taking coverage to **98%**. The sighted run reports **15 findings**, of which
the three terminal classes (private-transaction multiples, broker and advisor
surveys, deal values with terms undisclosed) have no instrument and never will:
naming the source on the page is the whole of the sourcing available, and the
guard reporting them is the guard working.

## A.2 Caveats

**Primary-source verification has run on the load-bearing figures, and not on every figure.** The pass recorded in A.0.2 opened the issuing body's own instrument for each figure that carries weight in the argument, and it changed twelve of them. Figures outside that set remain traceable rather than verified — every one appears in a named source and every computed figure appears in Derivations with its arithmetic, but a figure faithfully carried from a source that is itself wrong survives a traceability check unchanged. Four items could not be reached at all and are named in §5 of the verification record; they are not carried as sourced. **One open item could still move a conclusion:** GF Data publishes a specialty-trade contractor breakout that was not obtained, and if it carries the size bands then the statement in §6.7 that no benchmark closer than all-industries exists would need revising.

**Two of three sub-verticals are not sized, and the report says so rather than filling the gap.** Alarm, detection and monitoring has a ceiling. Extinguisher, kitchen and special hazard has a floor that the arithmetic itself indicates is low, for the reason given in §2.4. No combined market total appears anywhere in this document, and one should not be constructed from its parts.

**The fragmentation distribution in §2.6 is a container distribution.** It measures the trade groupings the published tables carry, which fuse fire with adjacent work. It supports a statement about how the containing trades are structured; it does not license a statement about the size distribution of fire contractors specifically.

**Geographic coverage in Part V is incomplete by a known margin**, and §5.1 states which platforms it is blind to. No saturation index, share-of-metro figure or territory ranking should be built on it without a second source.

**The installed base in §8.4 rests on a survey now several years old.** That is a structural limitation of any model built on it, this one included.

**Vendor-published figures are labelled where they appear**, and the interest is disclosed. Three categories recur: electronic-reporting platform statistics published by the platform's operator, contractor price guides published by contractors, and public-company trading multiples computed by data vendors rather than reconciled to any issuer's own definition of adjusted earnings.

**Two figures in the body are open at the time of writing** in the ordinary sense that the series refreshes: the commercial insurance rate trajectory in §8.1 and the listed trading panel in §6.2, whose data dates are given per row.

## A.3 Primary source families

**Government and official statistics.** US Economic Census tables and API endpoints; Code of Federal Regulations and Federal Register; state administrative codes and fire-marshal rules; state licence registries; Department of Labor union filings; federal energy consumption survey; national education, hospital and long-term-care facility counts.

**Codes and standards bodies.** Model fire and building code adoption charts and edition text; consensus standards for water-based systems, alarm and detection, portable extinguishers, commercial cooking operations, clean agents and energy storage; municipal fire codes and adopting ordinances.

**SEC filings and issuer disclosures.** Annual and quarterly reports, subsidiary exhibits across multiple vintages, registration statements, earnings-call transcripts, investor days and company newsrooms.

**Market research and industry data.** Middle-market transaction databases with size-banded multiples, sector M&A updates, portfolio-mark studies, trade-association research and fire-loss studies.

**Trade and financial press.** Sector trade outlets, national newswires and financial press, with the outlet named wherever a figure reaches print through it.

**Vendor-published benchmarks, interest disclosed.** Electronic-reporting platform statistics, contractor and franchise price guides, and data-vendor computations of listed multiples.

## A.4 How to read the figures, and where they came from

**Everything in this report is drawn from publicly available information.** The sources are US government statistical releases, SEC filings and issuer disclosures, codes and consensus standards, published market-research and trade-association output, trade and financial press, and the public websites and press releases of the companies named. Each is cited where it is used and listed in full in the Sources section below.

**No proprietary or confidential information was obtained or used in preparing this report.** Nothing here derives from a confidentiality agreement, a data room, management access, or a client engagement. No company named in this document provided information for it.

**Every figure in the body sits in one of three classes.** The body does not label them, because a reader should not have to read a footnote to reach a sentence. The convention is:

| Class | What it means | How to recognise it |
|---|---|---|
| **Reported** | A named publisher states the figure. Government series, filings, standards bodies, research houses, trade press. | The publisher and date appear alongside it. |
| **Verified** | Checked against the primary source on the publication date — the table, the release, the filing itself. | Also carries its publisher; the verification record is A.0.n and the files in `research/`. |
| **Derived** | Arithmetic on reported figures, not published anywhere. | Registered in Derivations below with inputs, working and assumption. |

The **Verified** class applies to the figures listed in A.0.2 and in the verification record's confirmation register. Everything else in the body is **Reported** or **Derived**.

**One distinction the body does observe, because it changes what a number means.** Where a transaction figure reaches print through trade press citing unnamed sources, with the parties declining to disclose terms, it is public but it is not a disclosure. The outlet is named wherever one appears, and in this sector that covers most large transaction figures.

**On the multiples shown.** Market-level multiples and ranges are market context, not target pricing. No multiple is applied to a named private company's earnings anywhere in this document, no enterprise value is estimated for one, and nothing here is an appraisal or should be read as a valuation of a business a reader might transact with. §6.1 sets out five populations that are not comparable and are not blended.

**Where the figures are least stable.** The commercial insurance rate series refreshes quarterly and has moved materially in each of the last three quarters. The listed trading panel in §6.2 carries per-row data dates spanning 2026-06-07 to 2026-07-29. The size-banded transaction benchmark in §6.5 runs only through 2025-09-30, so movement during 2026 in the one band the tuck-in thesis depends on is not observable. Ownership of privately held platforms changes without announcement, and A.1 names the two positions where continued ownership rests on an absence of news.

**The correction record.** Corrections are recorded in A.0.1 above rather than quietly absorbed. Each entry states what the figure was, what it became, and the named source that overturned it.

## Derivations

Figures below appear in no source and are arithmetic on figures that do. Each states its inputs, its arithmetic and its assumption. Identifiers are scoped by series to the part that uses them.

### Part I–II — the mandate and market sizing (A-series)

**A1 — Complement of the Burlington, Vermont compliance rate.**
Inputs, both vendor-published for the same jurisdiction: 686 systems actively tracked; 89% in full compliance. 100% − 89% = **11%** non-compliant. 686 × 0.11 = **75.46 systems**, carried in the body as about 75 systems in total. **Assumption named:** that "in full compliance" on the reporting platform and "current on required inspection, testing and maintenance" are the same predicate. If the platform's compliance test is narrower than the full code obligation — for example if it tests only whether a report was filed, not whether every interval in §1.2 was met — the true non-current share is higher than 11% and this derivation understates it.

### Part III and Part VII — the annuity and operating economics (E-series)

**E1 — Products-and-systems versus services gross margin, Johnson Controls, $ millions.** FY2025: products and systems gross profit 16,124 − 10,543 = 5,581; 5,581 ÷ 16,124 = 34.61%. Services 7,472 − 4,461 = 3,011; 3,011 ÷ 7,472 = 40.30%. Differential 40.30 − 34.61 = 5.69 points = +568 bps on unrounded inputs. Service share of sales 7,472 ÷ 23,596 = 31.67%. FY2024: 15,967 − 10,677 = 5,290 → 33.13%; 6,985 − 4,198 = 2,787 → 39.90%; differential +677 bps. FY2023: 15,789 − 10,736 = 5,053 → 32.00%; 6,542 − 3,791 = 2,751 → 42.05%; differential +1,005 bps. Cross-check: 10,543 + 4,461 = 15,004 equals disclosed consolidated cost of sales; 16,124 + 7,472 = 23,596 equals disclosed net sales. Inputs from the Johnson Controls FY2025 10-K income statement.

**E2 — Work-type gross-margin spread.** Testing and inspection floor 50% less new construction band ceiling 35% = 15 percentage points; less new construction band floor 25% = 25 percentage points. Assumption named: the comparison uses the stated band endpoints and takes no midpoint of either band. Bands from Meridian Capital, Winter 2025.

**E3 — APi segment margins and gross-margin spread.** Safety Services FY2025: 916 ÷ 5,456 = 16.79% (company states 16.8%). FY2024: 765 ÷ 4,797 = 15.95% (company states 15.9%). Consolidated adjusted EBITDA margin 1,041 ÷ 7,911 = 13.16% (company states 13.2%). Safety Services share of consolidated revenue 5,456 ÷ 7,911 = 68.97%. Adjusted gross margin spread: 37.3% − 18.9% = 18.4 points = 1,840 bps. Assumption named: the spread compares two differently constituted segments and is not a work-type differential.

**E4 — Cintas segment margins.** "All Other" FY2025: gross 542,369 ÷ 1,146,018 = 47.33%; operating 191,608 ÷ 1,146,018 = 16.72%. FY2024 operating 169,979 ÷ 1,064,082 = 15.97%. FY2023 operating 143,217 ÷ 967,143 = 14.81%. Fire Protection Services share of "All Other" 817,463 ÷ 1,146,018 = 71.33%. Uniform Rental operating 1,873,390 ÷ 7,976,073 = 23.49%. First Aid & Safety operating 294,728 ÷ 1,218,090 = 24.20%. Inputs $ thousands, Cintas FY2025 10-K segment table.

**E5, E6 — APi days sales outstanding, contract working capital and retainage.** Receivables only: 1,563 ÷ 7,911 × 365 = 72.11 days. Including contract assets: (1,563 + 484) ÷ 7,911 × 365 = 94.45 days. Net contract working capital 1,563 + 484 − 694 = $1,353M; 1,353 ÷ 7,911 = 17.10%. Contract liabilities 694 ÷ 7,911 = 8.77%. Retentions 187 ÷ 7,911 = 2.36% of revenue and 187 ÷ 1,563 = 11.96% of receivables; 48 ÷ 187 = 25.67% falling beyond one year. Assumption named: year-end balances, not average balances; an average-balance calculation would differ.

**E7 — APi remaining performance obligations against revenue.** 3,605 ÷ 7,911 = 45.57%. Assumption named: measures a point-in-time obligation balance against a full year of revenue, and 74% of the balance is expected to be recognised within twelve months.

**E8 — APi capex and fleet.** 96 ÷ 7,911 = 1.21% of revenue; $96,000,000 ÷ 29,000 = $3,310 per team member. Autos and trucks 138 ÷ 7,911 = 1.74% of revenue; $138,000,000 ÷ 29,000 = $4,759 per team member. Assumption named: the denominator is total team members, the numerator is gross historical cost, and leased vehicles are excluded from property, plant and equipment entirely.

**E9 — ADT headcount split and revenue per technician.** Technician share 3,100 ÷ 12,200 = 25.41%. Field-facing share (3,100 + 1,600) ÷ 12,200 = 38.52%. Installation, product and other revenue per installation-and-service technician $774,520 thousand ÷ 3,100 = $249,845 per technician. Monitoring and related services share of revenue 4,354,087 ÷ 5,128,607 = 84.90%. Inputs from the ADT FY2025 10-K revenue note and human-capital disclosure.

**E10 — Annualised recurring monthly revenue, both denominators, FY2025.** End-of-period recurring monthly revenue $359M × 12 = $4,308M. Against monitoring and related services revenue: 4,308 ÷ 4,354 = 98.9%. Against total revenue: 4,308 ÷ 5,129 = 84.0%. Difference 14.9 percentage points. Assumption named: annualising a point-in-time month assumes the year-end run rate held across the year.

**E11 — Implied customer life from gross attrition.** 1 ÷ 0.131 = 7.63 years; 1 ÷ 0.127 = 7.87 years. Assumption named: constant-hazard attrition, which real books do not exhibit because early-life attrition runs higher; the result is an upper-bound approximation, not a cohort life.

**E12 — Executed national-account contract escalation, 2022 to 2025.** Year 1 $87,576 to year 2 $96,290 = +$8,714 = +9.95%. Year 2 to year 3 $104,979 = +$8,689 = +9.03%. Two-year compound rate (104,979 ÷ 87,576)^(1/2) − 1 = +9.49%. Sum check 87,576 + 96,290 + 104,979 = $288,845, matching the stated total.

**E13 — Pull-through repair revenue as a share of invoiced dollars, and quote-size skew, FY2025.** $822M of repair revenue tied to recurring inspections or maintenance ÷ $8,000M of invoices = 10.3%. Assumption named: the denominator spans fire protection and mechanical contractors on one platform while the numerator is pull-through repair only, so this is not a fire-only ratio and is not comparable to the $3–$4 ratio, which uses a wider numerator definition. Quote skew: 63% of quotes accepted by count against 40% of quoted value approved implies the mean accepted quote is 0.40 ÷ 0.63 = 0.635 times the mean issued quote.

**E14 — Municipal inspection capture, 2024-01-31.** Systems current on annual inspection 1,313 ÷ 2,083 = 63.03%, leaving 36.97% not current. Systems found compliant 1,933 ÷ 2,083 = 92.80% against the city's stated 90%. Both stated values carried; the city's arithmetic reconciles on the first and not the second.

**E15 — Wage inflation, SOC 49-2098.** Median annual $48,320 (May 2021) to $60,070 (2025 vintage) = +24.32%; compound rate (60,070 ÷ 48,320)^(1/4) − 1 = 5.59%. $50,130 (May 2022) to $56,430 (May 2023) = +12.57%. $56,430 to $60,070 = +6.45%, compound 3.18%. Mean annual $50,210 (May 2019) to $57,400 (May 2023) = +14.32%, compound 3.40%. Employment 71,600 to 83,540 = +16.68%. Assumption named: the 2025 median is drawn from a restatement of BLS data through a different publication path than the May 2019–2023 medians, so the series is not strictly like-for-like at the final step.

**E16 — Wage inflation, SOC 47-2152.** Median annual $59,880 (May 2021) to $63,800 (2025 vintage) = +6.55%; compound rate 1.60%. $61,550 (May 2023) to $63,800 = +3.66%. Divergence against E15: 5.59% − 1.60% = 399 bps a year. Assumption named: 47-2152 is a 504,500-person container in which sprinkler fitters are a minority, so its aggregate wage is not a sprinkler-fitter wage.

**E17 — Sprinkler-fitter apprentice ratio.** 4,000 apprentices ÷ 13,000 journeypeople = 30.77%; 4,000 ÷ 17,000 total = 23.53%. Membership conflict: union statement over 17,000 against a 2025 Department of Labor LM filing of 16,631, a gap of 2.22%. No midpoint taken; both figures carried.

**E18 — Census cost structure and payroll per head, NAICS 238220, 2022 Economic Census, $ thousands.** Inputs: total receipts 297,608,835; annual payroll 80,351,785; cost of materials, parts and supplies 93,671,679; work subcontracted out 288,331,268 less 267,125,455 = 21,205,813; value added 175,917,812; construction-worker annual payroll 56,060,961 with first-quarter construction workers 846,519; other-employee annual payroll 24,290,824 with first-quarter other employees 323,173. Payroll ÷ receipts = 27.00%. Materials ÷ receipts = 31.47%. Subcontract payments ÷ receipts = 7.13%. Residual (297,608,835 − 80,351,785 − 93,671,679 − 21,205,813) ÷ 297,608,835 = 34.40%. Value added less payroll = 95,566,027 = 32.11% of receipts. Construction-worker payroll per head $56,060,961,000 ÷ 846,519 = $66,225; other-employee payroll per head $24,290,824,000 ÷ 323,173 = $75,164 per head. Assumptions named: both per-head figures divide an annual payroll numerator by a first-quarter employment denominator, which is how the Census publishes these variables, so they indicate relative cost per head rather than exact annual compensation; and **neither the 34.40% nor the 32.11% is EBITDA**, since both exclude all non-payroll operating cost.

**E19 — Route economics, route-serviced numerator.** Total revenue $10.3B × 95% route-serviced = $9,785M; $9,785M ÷ 12,100 routes = $808,678 per route. Customers per route: over one million businesses ÷ 12,100 = ≈83. Route growth 11,700 to 12,100 = +3.42%. Assumptions named: **the numerator is route-serviced revenue only, not all revenue**; the figure is whole-company across uniform rental, first aid and fire protection, and is not a fire protection route figure; and a customer buying more than one service line may be served by more than one route, so 83 is a lower bound on stops per route-year and says nothing about stops per day.

**E20, E21, E22, E23 — Platform density proxies.** Commercial platform, 2023-10-02: 300,000 customer locations ÷ 2,300 technicians = 130.4 locations per technician; 300,000 ÷ 100 branches = 3,000 locations per branch; 2,300 ÷ 100 = 23 technicians per branch. North American safety-services platform, 2024: ≈10,500 team members ÷ 124 branches = 84.7 per branch. Special-hazard platform: 8,000 sites ÷ 15 branches = 533 sites per branch; 42 NICET-certified individuals ÷ 15 branches = 2.80 per branch. National platform footprint: 9,000 team members ÷ 250 locations = 36.0 per location; 250 ÷ 47 states = 5.32 locations per state. Assumptions named: "more than", "over" and "plus" qualifiers on nearly every input make these upper bounds on the denominators and lower bounds on the ratios; team members and customer locations are not technicians and not serviced sites; and monitoring-only sites requiring no technician visit inflate the location count.

**E24 — Inspection software cost per technician.** $59 × 12 = $708 per year; $99 × 12 = $1,188; $159 × 12 = $1,908 per year. Against a revenue-per-technician assumption of $250,000: 1,908 ÷ 250,000 = 0.76%. Assumption named: the $250,000 denominator is an assumption, taken from the order of magnitude of the only disclosed installation-revenue-per-technician figure in the sector (E9), and it is not a fire contracting figure.

**E25 — Union coverage in adjacent filers.** Approximately 18,000 ÷ 87,000 employees = 20.69%; approximately 900 ÷ 48,300 employee-partners = 1.86%. Assumption named: the first covers collective bargaining agreements and works councils across a global workforce, the second covers union representation in a US route business; they are not like-for-like measures of union density.

### Part IV–V — the consolidation map and regionality (M-series)

Every figure below is computed here. Inputs are named, arithmetic is shown, and the assumption is stated.

**M-1 — Owner-class counts (§4.1).** Class 1a is the 17 platforms enumerated in the fire-led platform table in §4.3; Class 1b the 5 platforms enumerated in the security-led table in §4.3; Class 3 the 8 holders enumerated in §4.4. Class 2 counts Kidde Global Solutions, Spectrum Safety Solutions and Marioff, giving 3 entities. Class 4 counts CertaSite, Performance Systems Integration, Protegis Fire & Safety, the Cosco and Firetrol franchise under Consolidated Fire Protection, and ADT Commercial, giving 5 entities. *Assumption: a platform is counted once, under the owner that holds it as of 2026-07-29; add-ons and divisions are not counted as platforms.*

**M-2 — Pye-Barker 2025 closing rate.** 57 acquisitions ÷ 12 months = **4.75 closings per month**.

**M-3 — CertaSite add-on rate across two dated sponsor statements.** Eastman Fire Protection was the 21st add-on at 2023-09-13; Weber Fire and Safety was the 26th at 2024-03-13. 26 − 21 = **5 add-ons in 6 months**, an annualised rate of 10 per year over that window. *Assumption: both counts are platform-cumulative and count the same class of transaction. The window is six months, so the annualised figure is not a hold-period average.*

**M-4 — Sciens add-on rate under Huron.** 13 add-ons ÷ 5 years (2016 formation to the December 2021 sale) = **2.6 per year**. The separate count of ten complementary businesses from 2019 covers a shorter window and is not divided into the same denominator.

**M-5 — Altus add-on rate under AE Industrial.** 8 add-ons ÷ 3 years (2021 to 2024) = **2.67 per year**.

**M-6 — Guardian Fire Services add-on rate under Northern Lakes.** 12 acquisitions ÷ 4 years (2022 to 2025-12-03) = **3.0 per year**.

**M-7 — National Fire & Safety add-on rate.** Six dated add-ons from 2019-09-16 to 2022-03-01 = 30 months = 2.5 years. 6 ÷ 2.5 = **2.4 per year**.

**M-8 — Sciens division count.** The platform's own all-locations page enumerates **31** divisions, including two cities carried under one San Antonio division and several divisions with two named cities.

**M-9 — Encore brand-partner count.** The affiliated-brand-partners page yields **75** distinct tokens, treating the two case variants of "Allstate Fire Equipment" as one and counting "Allstate Fire Equipment of Rhode Island" separately.

**M-10 — Encore state count.** The published locations sitemap yields 33 branches in RI, VT, MA, CT, NJ, PA, ME, NH, NY, MD, VA, FL and LA = **13 states**, against 17 states on the sponsor's portfolio page. 33 ÷ 13 = 2.54 branches per state.

**M-11 — Western States Fire Protection named offices.** 36 WSFP-branded office entries (35 distinct cities, Centennial CO appearing twice) + 5 sub-brand nameplates = **41 named offices**, against a stated "More Than 45 Locations."

**M-12 — Pye-Barker city-level capture.** ≈60 trading names tied to a named city ÷ 253 stated locations = **23.7%**; ÷ 256 enumerated location URLs = **23.4%**. Both round to roughly a quarter. *Assumption: one recovered trading name per location page, which is a floor — some pages carry two nameplates.*

**M-13 — Pye-Barker brand token total.** 144 canonical brands + ≈60 location-derived trading names = **≈204** tokens.

**M-14 — APi Safety Services share of FY2025 net revenues.** $5,456M ÷ $7,911M = **68.97%**, using the reported total rather than the segment sum. Segment sum: $5,456M + $2,460M = $7,916M, which exceeds the stated total by **$5M** of presumed intersegment eliminations. Neither segment figure is adjusted to force the shares to sum to a hundred.

**M-15 — Summit acquisitions predating the disclosed programme.** 82 total acquisitions since the 1999 founding − 55 strategic acquisitions = **27** predating that count, as at the 2021 disclosure.

**M-16 — CertaSite location count.** The locations page states 17 locations and enumerates 16 cities, leaving **one** location without a named city.

**M-17 — Marmic unnamed acquisitions.** "Over 30 companies" joined; 8 acquired names appear in the public record. At least **22** acquired trading names are unnamed.

**M-18 — Cintas "All Other" segment.** $1,146,018k ÷ $1,064,082k − 1 = **+7.70%** FY2025 against FY2024. Share of total company revenue = $1,146,018k ÷ $10,340,181k = **11.08%** of the group. *Assumption stated as a limit, not an estimate: All Other contains Fire Protection Services and Uniform Direct Sale, so Fire Protection Services is strictly smaller than $1,146,018k and is not separately disclosed.*

**M-19 — Sponsor hold periods as of 2026-07-29.** Pavion under Wind Point Partners from June 2020 = **≈6.1 years**. Sciens under Carlyle from 2021-12-15 = **≈4.6 years**. Spectrum Safety Solutions under Sentinel from 2024-07-02 to the Marioff agreement of 2026-03-31 = **20 months** to first carve-out.

**M-20 — Multi-owner named cities.** §5.2 contains **38** named cities with two or more distinct owners: 2 cities with four owners, 12 with three, and 24 with two. *Assumption: exact city-name matches only, within the units listed in §5.1 as publishing city-level rosters.*

**M-21 — Pye-Barker recurring revenue movement.** $50,391,844 at 2025-12-31 ÷ $15,300,000 in 2023 = **3.29 times** over two years. *Assumption: acquisition-driven; 57 acquisitions closed in 2025 alone, so this is not an organic growth rate.*

**M-22 — States with no verified operating footprint.** Fifty states, less every state named in the published rosters of the units in §5.1 and the state lists of the platforms in §4.3, leaves **1**: Alaska.

### Part VI and Part IX — valuation and aggregation challenges (V-series)

Inputs are sourced in the body. Every output below is arithmetic on those inputs; inputs carrying "approximately" in the original are marked ≈ and propagate their imprecision.

**V1 — Listed contractor panel width.** 27.74 − 15.52 = **12.22 turns** between FIX and EME. Assumption: both vendor data dates are 2026-07-29, so this is a same-date comparison within one vendor's methodology.

**V2 — RMR converted to annual revenue and to monitoring gross profit.** Annualised monitoring revenue per $1 of RMR = **$12.00**. 46 ÷ 12 = **3.83x** annualised monitoring revenue; 36 ÷ 12 = **3.00x**. Annual gross profit per $1 of RMR at the sourced 51.2% monitoring and services margin = $12.00 × 0.512 = **$6.144**. 46 ÷ 6.144 = **7.49x**; 36 ÷ 6.144 = **5.86x**. Assumptions: applies a 2026-reported industry average margin to 2024/2025 transaction multiples, and the outputs are EV/monitoring gross profit before any selling, branch, general, administrative or corporate cost. They are not EBITDA multiples.

**V3 — Autronica.** Inputs, all disclosed and all approximate: price $555,000,000; 2025 sales $160,000,000; adjusted EBITDA margin 20%. Implied 2025 Adj. EBITDA = $160,000,000 × 0.20 = **$32,000,000**. EV/Adj. EBITDA = $555,000,000 ÷ $32,000,000 = **17.34x**. EV/Revenue = $555,000,000 ÷ $160,000,000 = **3.469x**. Sensitivity, because all three inputs are approximate: at an 18% margin, $555M ÷ $28.8M = **19.27x**; at 22%, $555M ÷ $35.2M = **15.77x** — a band of ≈15.8x to 19.3x on a ±2 point margin swing alone.

**V4 — ADT multifamily bulk accounts.** Inputs, disclosed: price ≈$55,000,000; ≈200,000 units; ≈$2,600,000 recurring monthly revenue. RMR multiple = $55,000,000 ÷ $2,600,000 = **21.15x**. Price per unit = $55,000,000 ÷ 200,000 = **$275.00**. RMR per unit = $2,600,000 ÷ 200,000 = **$13.00 per month**. Implied annual recurring revenue = $2,600,000 × 12 = $31,200,000, so EV/ARR = **1.763x**.

**V5 — CertaSite.** Inputs, disclosed: total consideration $271M; FY2025 revenue ≈$90M. EV/Revenue = $271M ÷ $90M = **3.011x**. Assumption: the numerator is total consideration transferred ($268M cash plus $3M escrow), not an enterprise value struck on a debt-free basis, so if debt was retired at close the true ratio is higher and 3.011x is a floor.

**V6 — ADT Commercial reconciliation.** Inputs, all from the seller's own reconciliation: EV $1,613M; Commercial Adj. EBITDA $158M LTM to 2023-06-30; estimated corporate cost allocation $(14)M; Commercial Adj. EBITDA including estimated corporate costs $144M; H1-2023 Commercial revenue $683M. $158M − $14M = **$144M**; $1,613M ÷ $144M = **11.201x**, closing exactly on the disclosed "approximately 11.2X". On the un-allocated denominator: $1,613M ÷ $158M = **10.209x**; on the headline pair, $1,600M ÷ $158M = **10.127x**; on the headline price and the allocated denominator, $1,600M ÷ $144M = **11.11x**. Annualised H1-2023 revenue = $683M × 2 = $1,366M, so the implied Adj. EBITDA margin is $158M ÷ $1,366M = **11.57%** before allocation and $144M ÷ $1,366M = **10.54%** after it; the second is the margin the 11.2x was struck against.

**V7 — APi 2024 bolt-on row, deck against filing.** Deck: 12 bolt-ons, $250M. Filing: 9 individually immaterial businesses at $77M plus three at $33M, $101M and $33M. Count: 9 + 3 = **12**, matching exactly. Value: $77M + $33M + $101M + $33M = **$244M** against **$250M**, a **$6M** difference. Both stand and neither document reconciles to the other. The count matching while the value differs is the usable signal: the disclosed deal counts are exact and the dollar totals are approximate.

**V8 — APi bolt-on programme, per deal.** Average consideration per deal = aggregate ÷ count: 2019 $9M ÷ 3 = **$3.0M**; 2020 $42M ÷ 7 = **$6.0M**; 2021 $114M ÷ 12 = **$9.5M**; 2022 $5M ÷ 1 = **$5.0M**; 2023 $98M ÷ 7 = **$14.0M**; 2024 $250M ÷ 12 = **$20.8M**; 2025 $233M ÷ 14 = **$16.64M**. Implied minimum aggregate EBITDA acquired in 2025, given a weighted average strictly below 6.0x = $233M ÷ 6.0 = **over $38.83M**; implied minimum average EBITDA per target = $38.83M ÷ 14 = **over $2.77M**. Assumption: a ceiling divided into a total yields a floor on EBITDA, so both figures are minima and the gap to any benchmark is wider than shown.

**V9 — Provenance arithmetic on the "2.8–3.3x" spread.** 17.0 ÷ 6.0 = **2.8333**; 20.0 ÷ 6.0 = **3.3333**; rounded, **2.8x–3.3x**. Assumption: because the denominator is a ceiling rather than a point, both quotients are upper bounds and the correct notation is under 2.83x and under 3.33x rather than a range centred on them.

**V10 — Size premium inside one methodology, YTD 2025.** $10–25M band 6.4x to $100–250M band 10.3x: **3.9 turns**, ratio **1.609x**. $10–25M band 6.4x to $50–100M band 8.3x: **1.9 turns**, ratio **1.297x**. $3–5M EBITDA band 6.7x to over-$10M band 8.3x: **1.6 turns**, ratio **1.239x**. This is the only size premium available measured inside a single dataset, single method and single period.

**V11 — Disclosed entry ceiling against the listed multiple.** 21.47 − 6.0 = **over 15.47 turns**; 21.47 ÷ 6.0 = **under 3.58x**. Assumption: the denominator is a ceiling, so the turns figure is a lower bound and the ratio an upper bound; the two ends are different populations — a private entry threshold and a vendor computation on a listed security observed 2026-07-28, and neither prices the other.

**V12 — Internal consistency of the 2024 report.** $350M × 17 = **$5,950M**; $350M × 20 = **$7,000M**; the reported "exceeding $6 billion" sits inside that band, and $6,000M ÷ $350M = **17.14x**, the bottom of the reported range rather than its middle. Assumption and limit: this tests only whether the three printed figures cohere with one another, which establishes that they are one assertion rather than three observations. It says nothing about their accuracy, and no transaction occurred at any of them.

**V13 — RMR size premium.** 46 − 36 = **10 turns of RMR**; 46 ÷ 36 = **1.278x**, a **27.8%** uplift. Both figures sit inside one source, one method and one year.

**V14 — Annualised RMR as a share of revenue.** Formula: (RMR × 12) ÷ FY2025 revenue, on company-reported survey inputs as of 2025-12-31. ADT ($358,700,000 × 12) ÷ $4,354,000,000 = **98.86%**; Kastle ($12,800,000 × 12) ÷ $282,000,000 = **54.47%**; Vector Security ($20,075,000 × 12) ÷ $454,590,000 = **52.99%**; Zeus ($6,467,326 × 12) ÷ $205,859,618 = **37.70%**; Securitas Technology ($102,000,000 × 12) ÷ $3,459,840,000 = **35.38%**, both inputs survey-estimated; Everon ($43,340,856 × 12) ÷ $1,618,725,121 = **32.13%**; Pye-Barker ($50,391,844 × 12) ÷ $2,000,000,000 = **30.24%**; Pavion ($17,832,431 × 12) ÷ $904,972,750 = **23.65%**; Minuteman ($1,986,667 × 12) ÷ $202,454,000 = **11.78%**; ORR Protection ($1,254,129 × 12) ÷ $169,428,932 = **8.88%**; Security 101 ($1,522,313 × 12) ÷ $300,765,253 = **6.07%**. Assumption: annualising a point-in-time December RMR figure against a full-year revenue figure overstates the ratio where RMR grew during the year, which acquisition-led platforms did.

**V15 — Hold-to-carve-out interval on the 2024 industrial fire carve-out.** Close 2024-07-01 to the Marioff sale agreement 2026-03-31 = **21 months**. Close 2024-07-01 to the Autronica agreement 2026-05-05 = **22 months**; to the Autronica closing 2026-07-09 = **24 months**. Assumption: measured from the closing date in the seller's 10-K rather than the acquirer's announcement date of 2024-07-02; using the latter shortens each interval by one day and changes nothing.

**V16 — Chubb, pre-synergy denominator.** Inputs, disclosed: EV $3.1B; 2020A revenue ≈$2.2B; Adj. EBITDA margin ≈9.6%. Implied 2020A Adj. EBITDA = $2,200M × 0.096 = **$211.2M**; pre-synergy EV/Adj. EBITDA = $3,100M ÷ $211.2M = **14.68x** against the disclosed ≈13.3x including synergies. Reconciliation: $3,100M ÷ 13.3 = $233.1M of implied synergy-inclusive EBITDA, less $211.2M = **≈$21.9M of credited synergies**. Assumption: this is inference from two figures the acquirer disclosed separately, not a disclosed synergy target; both the 13.3x and the 14.68x stand and neither should be averaged into the other. EV/2020A revenue = $3,100M ÷ $2,200M = **1.409x**.
